# Sūrah al-Nabaʾ (Chapter 78) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Hanging Over Makkah**

Sūrah al-Nabaʾ, "The Tiding," "The Announcement," or as it is most often rendered in English, "The Great News," is a Makkan sūrah of forty verses, and the first chapter of the final thirtieth part (juzʾ ʿamma) of the Qur'an. It takes its name from the phrase in its second verse — *al-nabaʾ al-ʿaẓīm*, "the Great Tiding" — around which the entire chapter is organized. That phrase functions as the chapter's dramatic pivot: a question is asked, a dispute is described, a decisive answer is promised, and then forty verses unfold the evidence that the answer is true. The whole architecture of the sūrah is thus already visible in its title, because the tiding is the thing about which people ask, the thing about which they disagree, and the thing which, the chapter insists, they will come to know with certainty.

Like the sūrahs that surround it in the final part of the Qur'an — al-Mursalāt before it, with its thundering refrain *"Woe on that Day to the deniers,"* and al-Nāziʿāt after it, with its opening oaths by the angels who wrench and draw forth — al-Nabaʾ belongs to the concentrated, incantatory Makkan style. Its verses are short, its rhythms emphatic, its imagery elemental: earth, mountains, sleep, night, day, heavens, sun, rain clouds, grain, gardens. It moves from the cosmic to the moral, from the cradle of the earth to the fires of Hell and the gardens of Paradise, and it closes with the single most haunting image in the chapter: the disbeliever staring at what his own two hands have sent ahead, and crying out that he wished he were mere dust.

**The Names of the Sūrah and What They Reveal**

The chapter is known by several names, and each name is a genuine window into its content rather than a mere label. The first and best-known name, al-Nabaʾ, comes directly from its second verse, and was fixed upon it by the Prophet ﷺ himself and the Companions, so that it appears under this name in the classical collections of hadith and in the lists of sūrah names compiled by early authorities. The second name, ʿAmma Yatasāʾalūn ("About what do they question one another?"), is taken from its first words, as with so many sūrahs of this part of the Qur'an — a naming practice that preserves the incipit as a memory-hook for reciters. The third name recorded for it, al-Muʿṣirāt ("The Rain-Clouds"), comes from verse 14, and points to one of the chapter's most distinctive rhetorical moves: its appeal to the natural world as a gallery of divine signs. And a fourth name, al-Tasāʾul ("The Mutual Questioning"), isolates the human drama of the opening — the image of people interrogating one another, and being interrogated by the truth itself.

These names map exactly onto the chapter's three movements. The first movement (vv. 1–5) stages the question and the dispute. The second movement (vv. 6–16) answers not with a syllogism but with a tour of creation, piling up rhetorical questions — *"Did We not make...?"* — that turn the reader's own surroundings into testimony. The third movement (vv. 17–40) announces the Day of Division, the trumpet, the opened sky, the moving mountains, Hell lying in ambush, and the rewards of the reverent, before ending on the individual reckoning that no soul can evade. The names al-Nabaʾ, al-Muʿṣirāt, and al-Tasāʾul thus correspond precisely to the tiding, the signs, and the questioning — the chapter compressed into three words.

**Time and Place of Revelation**

There is broad agreement among the classical commentators that Sūrah al-Nabaʾ is Makkan, revealed before the Hijrah, and that it belongs to the early-middle period of the Makkan phase, when the Prophet's ﷺ opposition from the Quraysh had hardened into open disputation. The evidence is internal. The sūrah makes no reference to legislation, to warfare, to the hypocrites of Madinah, or to the people of the Book as a settled community; instead its entire address is to those who dispute the Resurrection itself, which was precisely the Makkan controversy. Al-Ṭabarī records the setting: the Quraysh, when the Prophet ﷺ began to warn them of the Resurrection and the reckoning, would sit in their assemblies and question one another about this astonishing claim, some scoffing, some genuinely bewildered, and the revelation answered them by naming their very question. The commentators note that the opening *"About what do they question one another?"* is not a request for information — God knows the answer — but a rhetorical device of rebuke and wonder, drawing attention to the absurdity that creatures surrounded by so many signs should dispute the most momentous fact of all.

Some authorities, placing the sūrah slightly later or earlier within the Makkan sequence, connect its position to the chapters on either side of it. The Tiding of the Resurrection in verse 2 continues the Day of Division announced in the closing verses of al-Mursalāt (77:13–14, 38); and the chapter's own Day of Division in verse 17 is taken up again in al-Nāziʿāt (79:6–14). The sūrahs of this stretch of the Qur'an are best read as a single rolling sermon on the Hereafter, each one striking the same bell from a different angle: al-Mursalāt with its refrains, al-Nabaʾ with its questions and signs, al-Nāziʿāt with its oaths, ʿAbasa with its rebuke, al-Takwīr and al-Infiṭār with their unravelling of the cosmos. Al-Nabaʾ is the one that most carefully assembles the *argument* — the sequence from the bed of the earth to the lamps of heaven to the coming of the Day — and it is therefore the one that most rewards slow, ordered commentary.

**The Great Tiding: What Is It?**

The central interpretative question of the chapter is posed by its own title: what is the Great Tiding about which they dispute? The classical commentators record three principal answers, and the chapter's own content helps weigh them. The first and most widely held answer, given by the majority and adopted by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī in their dominant readings, is that the Great Tiding is the Resurrection — the news that the dead will be raised, gathered, judged, and recompensed. The chapter itself overwhelmingly supports this: every verse from 17 onward describes the Day of Division, the trumpet, the gathering, Hell, and Paradise. The second answer, also strongly represented, is that the Great Tiding is the Qur'an itself, the mighty revelation that came to them and divided them — a reading reinforced by the parallel verse *"Say: It is a Great Tiding, from which you turn away"* (38:67). The third answer, recorded by al-Rāzī among others, is that it refers to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself, whose message was the news that divided the Quraysh. These are not finally contradictory: the Qur'an is the tiding *in this world*, the Prophet is its *bearer*, and the Resurrection is its *content* — one news, three faces. What all agree upon is the chapter's insistence that the tiding is real, that the dispute over it is temporary, and that knowledge of it is coming.

**A Chapter Built from Questions**

It is worth pausing on the chapter's method, because it is the single most distinctive feature of al-Nabaʾ's rhetoric. The sūrah opens with a question — *"About what do they question one another?"* — and then answers a question with a series of further questions. Verses 6 through 16 are a cascade of six rhetorical interrogatives: *"Did We not make the earth a resting place... and the mountains stakes... and create you in pairs... and make your sleep repose... and make the night a garment... and the day for livelihood... and build above you seven firmaments... and make a radiant lamp... and send down water pouring from the rain clouds?"* Each question carries its answer inside it, because no sane person can answer "No" to any of them. The rhetorical strategy is Socratic in the best sense: the chapter does not argue the Resurrection from first principles; it builds a ladder of undeniable facts — the earth beneath you, the sleep that renews you, the sky above you, the rain that feeds you — and then asks, once you have granted all of these, how you can doubt the One who arranged them when He announces that He will gather you. The method is captured in the sūrah's own logic: the God who prepared the cradle is the God who can convene the court.

This question-driven method has a psychological purpose that the commentators did not miss. Disbelief in the Hereafter is rarely a conclusion reached by argument; it is usually a posture maintained by habit and by the wish that no reckoning exist. The chapter therefore does not argue against the disbelief so much as *crowd it out*: it floods the listener with the things he already trusts — the solidity of the earth, the certainty of night and day, the miracle of rain — until the doubt has no standing ground left. This is why the sūrah can move so confidently from "About what do they question one another?" to "Nay, but they will know!" in the space of five verses. The knowledge is already latent in the signs; the chapter merely disinters it.

**Structure and Thematic Architecture**

The chapter's forty verses divide into three clear movements, each performing a distinct task. The first movement, verses 1–5, stages the controversy: the mutual questioning, the naming of the Great Tiding, the fact of disagreement, and the doubled warning that they will come to know. The second movement, verses 6–16, builds the case: eleven verses of creation-signs — the earth as cradle, the mountains as pegs, humanity in pairs, sleep as a death and resurrection in miniature, night as a covering, day as the arena of livelihood, the seven heavens, the blazing lamp of the sun, the rain-clouds, and what the rain produces. The third movement, verses 17–40, delivers the verdict and the vision: the Day of Division as an appointed tryst, the trumpet and the throngs, the sky opened into gates, the mountains set in motion, Hell lying in wait for the rebellious, their drink of extremes, the fitting recompense, the recorded book, the command to taste, and then — in a turn as sudden as the trumpet itself — the rewards of the reverent, the gardens and companions and overflowing cups, the peace of a place where no idle talk is heard, the gift from the Lord of the heavens and the earth, the speechless awe before the Compassionate, the ranks of the Spirit and the angels, and finally the closing verse, which brings the whole chapter home to a single human being looking at the record of his own life.

One further structural observation deserves mention, because it is a key to the chapter's unity. The second movement's signs are arranged as a *vertical journey*: it begins with the earth (vv. 6–7), rises to the human creature (v. 8), descends again to the rhythms of sleep, night, and day (vv. 9–11), then ascends to the heavens, the sun, and the rain that falls from them (vv. 12–14), and descends once more to the grain and gardens the rain produces (vv. 15–16). The movement of the whole is earth → sky → earth, the complete circuit of the human environment, so that no region of a person's lived world is left without its sign. Then the third movement reverses the direction entirely: it is no longer a tour of this world but an unveiling of the next, in which the sky is opened, the mountains are moved, and every claim of stability in the first movement is undone by the very power that first established it. The God who *built* the heavens in verse 12 is the God who *opens* them in verse 19; the mountains He planted as pegs in verse 7 are the mountains He sets in motion in verse 20. The sūrah is a single sentence: the world is the sign; the sign points to the Maker; the Maker has appointed a Day; and on that Day the world itself will testify.

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the forty verses as an independent, fully developed discourse, in strict verse-by-verse order, exactly as the method requires. The source commentary groups certain verses — 4–5, 15–16, 21–22, and 24–25 — and each of these is here separated so that every verse receives its own complete treatment. Each verse is quoted individually, then expanded through the Arabic language and its roots, the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAbbās, and others), authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, the logic of its teaching, and its bearing upon the life of the modern human being. The sūrah's own method has been honored in the manner of the reading: nothing compressed for speed, no verse merged with its neighbor, no sign passed over in haste. From the question that opens it to the cry that closes it — *"Oh, would that I were dust!"* — the chapter is read the way it demands to be read: unhurried, complete, and with the eye fixed on the Day it announces.

## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:1

> **About what do they question one another?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Opens the Final Part of the Qur'an**

The sūrah opens with a single interrogative that has no stated subject except a pronoun: *ʿamma yatasāʾalūn* — "About what do they question one another?" The Arabic compresses an entire scene into two words. *ʿAmma* is the contraction of *ʿan mā* — "about what" — the preposition *ʿan* ("about, concerning") fused with the interrogative *mā* ("what"). And *yatasāʾalūn* is the sixth verbal form of the root *s-a-l* — to ask — a form that in Arabic carries the meaning of mutual or reciprocal action: not "they ask" but "they ask *one another*," each party putting the question to the other. The grammar therefore paints a picture of a crowd in motion, turning to each other, interrogating each other, passing a question from mouth to mouth. The verb is in the present tense, which in Qur'anic rhetoric often conveys not a single event but a *continuing* scene: this is not one conversation but an ongoing condition, a society in which the same question circulates endlessly without resolution.

Who is "they"? The classical commentators are essentially unanimous that the primary referent is the Quraysh, the Prophet's ﷺ own tribe, whose assemblies in Makkah were ringing with this very question at the time of revelation. Al-Ṭabarī transmits the setting from the earliest authorities: when the Prophet ﷺ began to speak of the Resurrection — of bodies raised from graves, of a reckoning, of a Garden and a Fire — the leaders of Quraysh gathered in their circles and asked one another in astonishment, "What is this he is telling us? Is a man to be brought back after he has died and rotted?" Some asked with sincere bewilderment; others asked with mockery, putting the question to each other in order to sharpen their ridicule of it. The verse answers their question with a question of its own — not because God needs to know what they were discussing, but as a rhetorical device that both *names* their condition and *rebukes* it. The commentators explain the rebuke embedded in the grammar: the interrogative particle here is not a request for information but an expression of wonder at the object of their dispute — as though to say, "What is this stupendous thing that they make the subject of their idle questioning?"

**The Root of Asking: How Human Beings Seek and How They Evade**

The root *s-a-l* runs through the Qur'an in every direction, and it is worth unfolding because the chapter opens its whole argument with it. From it come *suʾāl* (a question), *masʾalah* (a petition or a problem), *masʾūl* (one who is asked and will be held to account), and *suʾāl* in the sense of begging. The Qur'an uses the same root to describe the believers' legitimate asking — *"Ask the people of remembrance if you do not know"* (16:43) — and the disbelievers' evasive asking — the endless demand for proofs that is really a demand for permission to disbelieve. The sixth form chosen here, *yatasāʾalūn*, is significant in a further way that the lexicographers note: in the Qur'an it appears precisely at the moments when human beings are pictured turning to one another rather than to God — questioning *each other* about a truth that could only be settled by turning to its Source. In Sūrah al-Takwīr, the souls are pictured *"when the soul is paired"* and the questions of the grave come; in Sūrah al-Tūr (52:25) the people of Paradise turn to one another with *yatasāʾalūn* — but there the mutual questioning is sweet, because it is recollection of how God saved them. Here the mutual questioning is sterile, because it is the substitution of horizontal debate for vertical submission.

The psychological point is precise and modern. When a community is confronted with a truth that would reorganize its life — a truth like "you will be resurrected and judged" — its first reflex is often to talk it to death rather than act upon it. Mutual questioning becomes a substitute for decision: as long as we are still "discussing" whether the Day is real, we are excused from preparing for it. The sūrah exposes this reflex in its very first breath. It catches the Quraysh mid-conversation, as a camera catches a room full of people arguing about a fire alarm that is already ringing.

**The Content of Their Question: The Resurrection as the Dividing News**

What were they actually asking about? The commentators connect this verse to the precise controversy that dominates the Makkan chapters: the resurrection of the body after death. The Quraysh did not, for the most part, deny that a Creator made the world; the Qur'an itself records that they would concede creation to God (29:61–63). What they found outrageous was the claim that the Creator would *repeat* creation — that a man reduced to scattered bones would be reassembled and made to stand before his Lord. The Qur'an preserves their own scoffing words in several places:

> *"And they say: 'What, when we are bones and crumbled dust, shall we indeed be raised up as a new creation?'"* (Qur'an 17:49)

and again:

> *"He says: 'Who will give life to bones when they are decayed?'"* (Qur'an 36:78)

This was the question circulating in Makkah — "about what do they question one another?" — and the sūrah will answer it not by abstract argument but by the tour of creation in verses 6–16, and then by the direct description of the Day in verses 17–40. But the sūrah's first move is simply to *name* the question, to hold it up, and to promise that the answer is coming. There is a quiet drama in this: the very question the disbelievers use as a weapon is about to be turned into the title of the chapter that refutes them.

**The Teacher's Method: Opening with a Question**

There is a pedagogical wisdom in the sūrah's choice of opening that deserves reflection. Classical rhetoric, both Arabic and otherwise, has always recognized the question as the most powerful of openings, because a question does what a statement cannot: it engages the listener's own mind, forces him to supply the missing information, and makes him a participant in the discourse rather than a recipient of it. The Qur'an, which is the ultimate teacher, opens here with a question and then sustains the question-form through the entire middle of the chapter. This is not accidental. A person who is *asked* "Did We not make the earth a resting place?" is compelled to answer, if only inwardly, "Yes, You did" — and each inward "yes" is a step in the argument. The method converts the listener from an opponent into a witness against his own doubts. The sūrah is, in this precise sense, a Socratic dialogue in which the questioner is God and the one being led to the truth is the human soul.

The Prophet ﷺ himself used this method, as the hadith literature shows. When he wanted to teach the Companions the meaning of a verse or a ruling, he often began with a question: "Do you know what backbiting is?" (Muslim); "Shall I not tell you of the best of you?" and so on. The question is the pedagogy of the compassionate, because it assumes the learner already possesses the seeds of the answer and only needs them watered. The opening of al-Nabaʾ is the divine employment of the same mercy: before God tells them anything, He asks them what they are so agitatedly discussing — as a teacher asks a rowdy classroom what the noise is about, not for information, but to restore attention to the one thing that matters.

**Modern Echoes: The Question That Never Dies**

Fourteen centuries later, the question has not been retired; it has only changed costume. The same *yatasāʾalūn* continues in every generation, because the human heart's resistance to the Resurrection is perennial. In the modern world it takes the form of "Is there really anything after death?" — asked not in the assemblies of Quraysh but in lecture halls, in late-night conversations, on the internet, at the bedsides of the dying. The sūrah's opening is therefore not an antiquarian curiosity about seventh-century Makkah; it is a standing description of the human condition. Every era has its own version of the crowd questioning one another, and the Qur'an's answer is the same in every era: the question is about the Great Tiding, the matter is not resolved by talk, and knowledge of it is coming to every questioner. The first verse thus performs the function of all great openings: it does not argue; it *arrests*. It stops the reader mid-question and turns his face toward the only answer that can satisfy.

The verse also teaches something about the manners of inquiry that remains directly applicable. There is a questioning that seeks the truth — the believers' "ask the people of remembrance" — and there is a questioning that seeks only to postpone the truth. The sūrah does not condemn asking as such; the Qur'an is full of God inviting questions and answering them. What this verse exposes is the specific corruption of asking: the use of mutual debate as a shelter from personal responsibility. The cure, as the rest of the chapter will show, is not the silencing of questions but their redirection — from asking one another to *being asked* by God, from the horizontal chatter of the crowd to the vertical summons of the trumpet.

## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:2

> **About the Great Tiding,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The News at the Center of the Chapter**

The second verse supplies the object that the first verse withheld: *ʿani al-nabaʾi al-ʿaẓīm* — "About the Great Tiding." The verse is grammatically continuous with the first — "About what do they question one another? About the Great Tiding" — and this continuity is itself a piece of rhetoric: the question is held open for a full verse, the reader's curiosity suspended, and then the answer is dropped with the weight of the adjective *al-ʿaẓīm*, "the Great," "the Tremendous." The word *nabaʾ* is not merely "news" or "information" in Arabic; it denotes *important* news, a report of consequence, the kind of tidings that change everything for the one who hears them. The lexicographers distinguish *nabaʾ* from the lighter *khabar* (ordinary report) precisely on this ground: a *nabaʾ* is a report that carries weight, that arrives with authority, that cannot be received with indifference. Al-Qurṭubī and others note that the Qur'an reserves the word for its most momentous announcements — the tiding of the Resurrection, the tiding of revelation, the tiding of a prophet's coming. So the phrase "the Great Tiding" is not a mere label; it is a claim that the matter in question is the most consequential report a human being can ever receive.

And what is this tiding? Here the classical tradition divides into three main positions, each of which deserves careful statement, because the chapter itself is large enough to hold all three without strain.

**The First Reading: The Tiding of the Resurrection**

The most widely held interpretation — adopted as primary by Ibn Kathīr, and strongly represented in al-Ṭabarī's transmission from Ibn ʿAbbās and others — is that the Great Tiding is the news of the Resurrection and the Hereafter: that the dead will be raised, gathered, and recompensed. This reading is supported, first of all, by the internal structure of the chapter, whose final twenty-four verses are a sustained description of exactly that Day — the trumpet, the opened sky, the moved mountains, Hell, Paradise, and the individual reckoning. It is supported, second, by the verse's own parallel elsewhere in the Qur'an:

> *"Say: It is a Great Tiding, from which you turn away."* (Qur'an 38:67)

The context of that verse, in Sūrah Ṣād, is the same dispute over the Resurrection, and the same word *nabaʾ* is used. And it is supported, third, by the psychology of the Makkan controversy itself: the one claim that most scandalized the Quraysh, the one thing about which they questioned one another in astonishment, was the claim of bodily return after death. Ibn ʿAbbās, the Companion famed for Qur'anic commentary, is reported to have identified the tiding straightforwardly as "the Resurrection," and this became the majority view because it fits the sūrah's own evidence best. On this reading, the adjective *al-ʿaẓīm* modifies the *event*: it is a day "tremendous" in its certainty, its terror, and its finality.

**The Second Reading: The Tiding of the Qur'an**

A second, well-attested reading identifies the Great Tiding as the Qur'an itself. This view is recorded by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī from a number of the early commentators, and it finds support in the Qur'an's own description of itself as a mighty announcement:

> *"And We have certainly given you seven of the oft-repeated, and the Mighty Qur'an."* (Qur'an 15:87)

The argument for this reading is that the Quraysh's dispute was not only about the Resurrection but about the very claim that Muhammad ﷺ was receiving revelation — a claim they found as astonishing as the Resurrection, and which they discussed with the same mixture of wonder and rejection. On this reading, the "Great Tiding" is the news that God has spoken, that the Book has descended, and the sūrah's function is to answer those who question this tiding by pointing them to the signs that confirm the One who sent it. The two readings are not rivals so much as two faces of one reality: the Qur'an *is* the tiding, and its central content *is* the Resurrection. The tiding is great because its bearer is God and its content is eternity.

**The Third Reading: The Tiding of the Prophet**

A third interpretation, transmitted by al-Rāzī and others, is that the Great Tiding refers to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself — the arrival of the Messenger, which was itself the greatest news the Arabs had ever received, and about which they disputed most bitterly. On this reading the sūrah's opening is asking, "About what are they questioning one another? About this Messenger and his message" — and the rest of the chapter answers by vindicating the message he brought. This reading too is continuous with the others, because the Prophet is the bearer of the Qur'an, which announces the Resurrection; the tiding and its messenger and its content form a single chain. The classical tradition's willingness to hold all three readings together is itself a lesson in how the Qur'an is to be read: its statements are often deliberately polyvalent, and the richness of meaning is a feature of the text, not a defect of the commentators.

**Why the Tiding Must Be Called "Great"**

What makes this news *great*? The sūrah will spend its entire length answering this, but the adjective already carries the answer in germ. A piece of news is great in proportion to the difference it makes to the one who hears it. The news "rain is coming" is great to the farmer; the news "the city has fallen" is great to the soldier; the news "you have a terminal illness" or "you have been forgiven" is great to the patient. The Resurrection is the news that comprehends all news, because it is the news that *every other piece of news is provisional*. Every human life is lived under the shadow of a question mark — what happens when it ends? — and the Great Tiding is the answer to that question mark. It declares that the end is not an end but a transition; that the grave is not a terminus but a gate; that nothing done is ever lost, because everything done is recorded and will be returned. This is why the news is "great" — not because it is loud, but because it is final. The disbelievers sensed this, which is precisely why they disputed it: to accept the tiding is to accept that one's entire life is already under judgment, and that is the one piece of news the heedless heart most wishes were false.

There is a further, quieter reason the tiding is great, and it belongs to the logic of mercy. News of a reckoning is, on its face, frightening; but the Qur'an repeatedly frames the Resurrection as *good news* for the believer, a *bushrā*, a glad tiding:

> *"And give good tidings to those who believe and do righteous deeds, that theirs shall be Gardens beneath which rivers flow."* (Qur'an 2:25)

The same word-family that gives us *nabaʾ* gives us the Prophet's title *al-Nabī* — the bringer of the tiding. The tiding is great because it is the guarantee of justice: for every victim who was wronged and never vindicated, for every good deed done in secret and never rewarded, for every life of patience that ended without visible compensation, the Great Tiding is the promise that the account is not closed. A world without resurrection is a world where justice is finally a rumor; the tiding is the news that justice is a fact, merely deferred to its appointed day.

**The Tiding and the Modern Heart**

Modern humanity has not stopped asking about the tiding; it has only stopped calling it by that name. The question of what happens after death — whether consciousness survives, whether deeds are weighed, whether the wrongs of history are ever set right — remains the deepest question a human being can ask, and every culture answers it with something: an afterlife, an annihilation, a vague hope, a studied silence. The sūrah's opening two verses confront this universal question directly, and they do so with a claim of astonishing specificity: the answer is not a mood or a wish but a *report* — a *nabaʾ* — something that has been told to you by the One who knows, and about which your mutual questioning is only the prelude to your certain knowledge. The chapter thus reframes the whole human conversation about death: it is not a matter to be debated among ourselves forever, but a tiding already delivered, about which we will be informed — and, as the next verses promise, about which we will come to know with a knowledge that leaves no room for dispute.

The practical teaching of the verse is therefore an orientation of the whole life. If the Great Tiding is true — and the rest of the sūrah is its proof — then every day is a day of preparation, and every act is a draft of the record that will be read back. The believer is the one who has heard the tiding and let it reorganize his time, his wealth, his speech, and his fears. The disbeliever is not the one who has never heard it, but the one who has heard it and kept on questioning one another about it, as though the questioning itself were a shelter. The verse stands as the chapter's thesis: the most important news ever delivered is on the table, and the only question that remains is what you will do with it.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:3

> **regarding which they differ.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Tiding That Divides Rather Than Unites**

The third verse completes the chapter's opening sentence: *hum fīhi mukhtalifūn* — "they, regarding it, are in disagreement." The word *mukhtalifūn* is the active participle of the eighth form of the root *kh-l-f*, and its anatomy is worth unfolding because it carries the verse's entire argument. The root *kh-l-f* in Arabic means to succeed, to come after, to take the place of; from it comes *khalīfah* (a successor, a vicegerent), *khilāf* (disagreement, opposition), and *ikhtilāf* (difference, variance). The eighth-form participle *mukhtalifūn* pictures people who have *taken different positions behind one another* — who stand on opposite sides of a matter, each facing away from the other. The preposition *fīhi* ("regarding it") ties the disagreement firmly to the tiding of the previous verse: their variance is not scattered and general but concentrated on this one decisive matter. And the subject "they" is the same crowd as in verse 1 — the Quraysh who questioned one another, now shown to be not merely curious but *divided*.

This is a subtle and important distinction that the sūrah draws in the space of three verses. Questioning (verse 1) is an activity; disagreement (verse 3) is a condition. A question can be innocent; a settled difference is not. The chapter's opening thus moves from the *act* of asking to the *state* of conflict: the Quraysh are not on the way to the truth through honest inquiry; they have taken up fixed positions on either side of it and are entrenched there. The commentators — al-Ṭabarī chief among them — read the verse as describing the two parties into which the disputants divided: those who believed and confirmed, and those who denied and rejected. On this reading, the tiding did not simply pass over Makkah; it *split* it, the way every genuine truth splits its audience into those who accept it and those who refuse it.

**The Two Parties, and the Shades of Rejection**

Al-Ṭabarī transmits from the earliest authorities a twofold description of this divergence. Some said the disagreement is between the believers and the disbelievers — some affirming the Resurrection, some denying it — and this is the plainest sense. But the verse's range is wider than a simple binary, and the later commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, were careful to map the many ways a single tiding can be differed about. They note that the disbelievers themselves were not a monolith: some denied the Resurrection outright; some accepted a God but denied the return of the body; some accepted a "spiritual" survival but mocked the idea of a physical resurrection; some believed in the Prophet's honesty but could not bring themselves to believe his message. The Ashʿarite theologians, in particular, drew from this verse a point about the degrees of rejection: disbelief is not a single act but a spectrum of refusals, and the verse's *mukhtalifūn* — those who differ — includes all who accept the tiding partially and reject it partially. The tiding is one; the positions taken toward it are many.

This observation has a sharp edge for every generation, including ours. The Qur'an does not divide humanity into those who have heard and those who have not, but into those who, having heard, *differ* about what they heard. The believer and the denier are not separated by information — both have received the same report — but by the posture each takes toward it. The verse thus quietly abolishes the excuse of ignorance for those who have heard the tiding and still debate it: their difference is not a lack of data but a divergence of will. As the Qur'an says elsewhere, of those who denied after the truth came to them, their denial is not from want of evidence:

> *"Those who dispute concerning the signs of God without any authority having come to them — grievous is it in the sight of God."* (Qur'an 40:35)

**Why a Single Truth Produces Divergence: The Logic of the Heart**

Why does a tiding as momentous as the Resurrection not simply unite everyone who hears it? The answer lies in a principle the Qur'an states in many forms: a truth is received differently depending on what a person has invested in the alternatives. The Resurrection is not a neutral fact like the boiling point of water; it is a claim that reorders every value a person holds. To accept it is to accept that wealth must be earned lawfully, that desire must be disciplined, that the oppressed will be vindicated and the oppressor called to account, that death is not the end of consequence. A person whose life is arranged around the assumption that there is no reckoning will *experience* the tiding as a threat, and will differ from it — not because the evidence is weak but because the cost is high. This is the Qur'anic diagnosis, stated plainly:

> *"Nay, but man desires to persist in sin before him. He asks: 'When is the Day of Resurrection?'"* (Qur'an 75:5–6)

The verse's *mukhtalifūn* are thus revealed, in the deepest sense, as people who differ not in their intellects but in their loves. The sūrah, having named their difference, will spend the next thirteen verses not refuting their arguments one by one but *re-lighting the signs* — as if to say, the disagreement is not for want of light, but for want of the will to see. And then, in verses 4 and 5, it will pronounce the sentence that resolves the whole dispute: the difference is temporary, and knowledge is coming.

**Disagreement as a Sign of the Tiding's Weight**

There is a paradoxical truth worth drawing out: the very fact of fierce disagreement is itself a testimony to the tiding's greatness. People do not argue passionately about trivialities; they argue passionately about what matters. The intensity of the Makkan dispute over the Resurrection — the fact that it divided families, ended friendships, and rearranged the whole society — is evidence of the news's magnitude. Al-Rāzī makes a related point in his commentary: the Qur'an's own phrase "the Great Tiding" is vindicated by the scale of the controversy it generated. A report that produced indifference would have been small news; a report that produces sustained difference is, by that very measure, great. The verse thus invites a reframe: the disagreement the believer faces, whether in Makkah or in a modern workplace, is not proof that the tiding is doubtful but proof that it is weighty enough to be worth disagreeing about.

This reframe has a pastoral use. Believers who find themselves opposed, ridiculed, or simply in a minority of one regarding their faith are sometimes tempted to conclude that the faith must be shaky, since so many differ from it. The sūrah's opening corrects this: the Prophet ﷺ himself, with the Qur'an fresh on his lips, faced a city that differed from him; the disagreement was predicted, not accidental, and it was the disagreement of the tiding's own greatness. The believer's task is not to eliminate the difference — only the Day will do that — but to hold the true side of it with patience, exactly as the chapter will go on to teach.

**The Difference That Will Not Stand**

Finally, the verse looks forward. Its grammar is present-tense and descriptive — "they differ" — but its placement between the question of verse 1 and the warning of verse 4 gives it the force of an epitaph already being written. The difference it describes is real, but it is also *dated*. The next two verses will declare that the disputants "will know" — that the disagreement is a provisional condition of this world, scheduled to be dissolved by the event itself. The sūrah's whole movement is compressed into this opening triplet: they ask; they differ; they will know. The question is temporary, the difference is temporary, but the knowledge is permanent. In the meantime, the believer is taught by this verse a specific kind of serenity: not to be surprised by disagreement, not to be shaken by it, and not to imagine that the majority's posture is the final verdict. The final verdict belongs to the Day the chapter is about to describe, when the differing parties will be separated by a knowledge no one can dispute.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:4

> **Nay, but they will know!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hardest Word in the Qur'an's Opening**

The fourth verse is one of the shortest in the entire sūrah, and one of the most heavily weighted: *kallā sa-yaʿlamūn* — "Nay, but they will know!" Three words in the original, and the first of them, *kallā*, is among the most discussed particles in the whole of Qur'anic philology. It is a word of *rebuke and repulsion*, a sharp corrective — something like "No indeed!" or "By no means!" or "Not so!" — used in the Qur'an precisely where a false claim has been made or a wrong posture assumed and must be struck down. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the word occurs more than thirty times in the Qur'an, and that in every place it serves to negate what precedes it and to set up a decisive correction. Here it negates the whole scene of the opening three verses: their questioning, their difference, their comfortable debate. "No" — this is not a matter that will be settled by their talk. And then the correction: *sa-yaʿlamūn*, "they will know" — with the particle *sa-* attached to the verb, the Arabic prefix of the *near future*, giving the meaning "they will come to know," "they shall certainly know," a future so certain that it is already, so to speak, on its way.

The verb is *yaʿlamūn* — from the root *ʿ-l-m*, "to know" — and the choice of this root over its synonyms is deliberate. The Qur'an distinguishes between kinds of knowing: *maʿrifah* (recognition), *fiqh* (understanding), *khabar* (being informed). But *ʿilm* is the word for certain, settled, indisputable knowledge — the knowledge of a fact that can no longer be doubted. The verse does not say "they will be told," or "they will suspect," or "they will fear." It says *they will know* — with the same certainty with which they now know the sun has risen. The promise is that their present doubt will be replaced not by argument but by direct acquaintance. What they now dispute as hearsay they will then experience as fact.

**The Two Knowings: Why the Verse Is Doubled**

A reader immediately notices that the next verse, verse 5, repeats the same words — *thumma kallā sa-yaʿlamūn*, "Then indeed they will know!" — and the commentators were therefore at pains to explain the doubling. The classical discussion, preserved in al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī and summarized by Ibn Kathīr, offers several explanations, all of which have value. The simplest is *emphasis*: the repetition is the Arabic idiom of intensification, like striking the same nail twice, and the Qur'an frequently doubles a warning to impress its weight — as in *"the striking blow! What is the striking blow?"* (101:1–2). The second explanation is *sequence*: the two knowings refer to two different moments of knowledge. On this reading, the first "they will know" refers to their coming to know the truth of the Resurrection — the reality they now deny — while the second refers to their coming to know the truth of the *punishment* that follows their denial. Al-Rāzī records a refinement of this: the first knowing is at death, when the scales fall from the eyes, and the second is at the resurrection and reckoning, when the full reality is unveiled.

The third explanation, also well attested, is that the two verses are addressed to two different groups: the first *kallā* to the disbelievers — "they will know" the truth they mocked — and the second to the believers, "they will know" the reward they were promised, or know with the knowledge of certainty the very thing they already believed by faith. This reading has the elegance of making the sūrah's warning double-sided, as its final movement will be double-sided: the chapter ends with Hell for the rebellious (vv. 21–30) and Paradise for the reverent (vv. 31–36), and the doubled "they will know" at the opening is, on this reading, a seed of that final division. All three explanations agree on the essential point: the doubling is not idle, and the knowledge promised is total.

**The Psychology of Deferred Knowledge**

The verse teaches something fundamental about the nature of truth and time. It declares that knowledge is *coming* — that there is a gap between the moment of doubt and the moment of certainty, and that the gap is not the same as the absence of truth. A person who denies the Resurrection is not in possession of a contrary fact; he is simply *early* in a process whose end is fixed. The Qur'an's image throughout is of a reality that is not yet visible but is nonetheless certain — like the dawn that is coming while the night still claims the sky. The disbeliever's present confidence is therefore not evidence of anything except the incompleteness of his vantage point. The verse's *sa-yaʿlamūn* — "they *will* know" — is the Qur'an's way of saying that time itself is on the side of the truth, and that the only thing separating the denier from knowledge is the interval in which he is permitted to choose freely.

This is also the Qur'an's deepest answer to the problem of why God does not compel belief. If certainty were imposed now — if the trumpet were sounded on demand — the test would be over and the choice would be meaningless. The gap between doubt and knowledge is precisely the arena of moral freedom: it is in that gap that a person decides whether to believe on the evidence of the signs or to wait for the evidence of the event. The verse's warning is therefore not a threat so much as a *saving disclosure*: it tells the denier, while there is still time, that his present posture is temporary and his eventual knowledge is certain, so that he might change his posture before the knowledge arrives. As the Qur'an says in the voice of the wrongdoers when the knowledge finally comes:

> *"Our Lord, we have seen and we have heard; so send us back, that we might work righteousness. Truly we are now certain."* (Qur'an 32:12)

The tragedy the sūrah wants to prevent is precisely this: knowledge arriving too late to be of use.

**A Warning That Is Also a Mercy**

It is important to see the verse's tone correctly, because it is easy to misread *kallā sa-yaʿlamūn* as pure threat. The commentators are clear that the verse is a *warning*, and the Qur'an's warnings are, in their essence, acts of mercy — the mercy of a physician who tells a patient the truth about his condition while it can still be treated. God's warning here is a kind of grace: He tells the disputants, plainly and in advance, that they will know, so that the knowing, when it comes, will be a confirmation rather than a catastrophe. The entire sūrah is constructed on this gracious logic: the threat is stated first (vv. 4–5), then the evidence is laid out (vv. 6–16), then the Day is described in full (vv. 17–40) — so that no one can say he was not told. The warning is the *first* gift, the evidence the *second*, and the description the *third*; the punishment, when it falls on the unheeding, will have been preceded by an exhaustive mercy.

This reframes the believer's own use of the verse. When a Muslim recites *kallā sa-yaʿlamūn*, it is not a curse upon opponents but a re-immersion in certainty: a reminder that the truth he holds by faith is scheduled to become knowledge by sight, and that the mockery he may face for it is scheduled to end. The verse is a resource for patience. The Prophet ﷺ and the early Muslims recited these very words in Makkah while the city differed from them, and the *sa-yaʿlamūn* was their consolation — the promise that the debate had an expiry date. Believers in every later age, facing the same differing crowds, inherit the same consolation: the argument is not endless, and the last word belongs to the truth.

**The Certainty of the Future Tense**

Finally, a note on the grammar that carries the theology. The prefix *sa-* in Arabic indicates the near future, but in the Qur'an's use it carries more than temporal proximity; it carries *assurance*. When God says *sa-yaʿlamūn*, the future tense is not a prediction but a decree. It is the same grammar God uses for His own promises — *"they shall not fear, nor shall they grieve"* (2:38, 2:62, and many places) — and for His own warnings. The Arabic does not hedge; it does not say "perhaps they will know" or "they may come to know." It states the future as already fixed, because it is fixed by the One who owns the future. This is the verse's final teaching: the knowledge is not merely probable; it is guaranteed. The only open question in the entire opening of the sūrah is not *whether* they will know, but *in which condition* they will know — as believers whose certainty is confirmed, or as deniers whose certainty is their ruin. And the rest of the chapter exists to settle that question in the reader's favor.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:5

> **Then indeed they will know!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Stroke of the Warning**

Verse 5 repeats verse 4 almost word for word, adding only the particle *thumma* at its head: *thumma kallā sa-yaʿlamūn* — "Then indeed they will know!" The addition is small and the commentators made much of it, because in the Qur'an nothing is mere filler. *Thumma* is the Arabic particle of *sequential delay* — it means "then," but a "then" that implies a gap, an interval, a progression from one thing to a later thing, often with a note of emphasis on how far the later thing exceeds the earlier. It differs from *fa-* (which indicates immediate succession) precisely in this: *fa-* chains events tightly together, while *thumma* inserts distance and gradation. When the Qur'an says *thumma* it is marking a stage — "and then, after that..." — and the stage it marks here is the escalation from one knowledge to a greater knowledge.

So the sūrah's opening warning, read in full, is a two-stage sentence: "Nay, but they will know!" — and then, after an interval, "Then indeed they will know!" The first stroke announces that knowledge is coming; the second announces that *more* is coming — that the knowing they are headed toward is not a single revelation but a sequence, each stage more overwhelming than the last. The commentators filled this sequence in with the stages of the Hereafter: the first knowing at the approach of death, when the dying person sees what he had denied (cf. 50:19, *"And the throes of death bring the truth — that is what you were shunning"*); the second at the grave; the third at the trumpet and the resurrection; the fourth at the standing before God and the reckoning; and on, into the judgment itself. *Thumma* is the hinge on which these stages turn: each one arrives after the one before, and each is a fuller knowledge than the one before.

**The Two Verses, Two Audiences, Two Recompenses**

The classical discussion of whether verses 4 and 5 address one group or two was introduced in the previous commentary, and it deserves completion here, because it shapes how the whole sūrah is read. The majority view, transmitted by al-Ṭabarī and adopted by Ibn Kathīr, is that both verses address the disbelievers, and that the doubling is for emphasis and for the staging of their knowledge — first the knowledge of the Resurrection's truth, then the knowledge of the punishment their denial has earned. But a second, elegant reading, recorded by al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, distributes the two verses between the two groups the chapter will later separate: verse 4 addresses the disbelievers, who "will know" the punishment they are promised (the content of verses 21–30), and verse 5 addresses the believers, who "will know" the reward they are promised (the content of verses 31–36). On this reading, *thumma* marks not only a temporal sequence but a *division*: first the deniers' knowledge, then — separately — the believers' knowledge, each group coming to know, on the Day of Division, exactly what its own hands had prepared.

Both readings are defensible and both are illuminating, and the tradition's preservation of both is characteristic of its method: where the text will bear multiple true meanings, the commentators do not insist on one to the exclusion of the rest. What is common to all readings is the verse's function as a *hinge*: it closes the sūrah's opening scene (the questioning and the differing) and opens the two great movements that follow (the signs, and then the Day). The warning is the door between the dispute and the evidence. Having said "they will know," the sūrah can now do its real work — which is not to repeat the warning but to make it unnecessary, by showing why the knowledge, when it comes, will be deserved.

**The Grammar of Threat as the Grammar of Mercy**

It is worth dwelling once more on the tone, because a modern reader can easily hear these two verses as pure menace, and the classical tradition heard them otherwise. The doubling *kallā... kallā* is indeed emphatic — the Arabic idiom leaves no doubt that the matter is settled. But the settled matter is a *warning*, and a warning is by definition an act addressed to someone who still has time. God does not warn the dead; He warns the living. The very fact that the sūrah says "they will know" in the future tense, rather than "they now know" in the present, is the proof that the door is still open. The disbelievers are being told, in advance, that their doubt has an expiry date — and this advance telling is precisely what makes repentance possible. Al-Rāzī makes the point with characteristic clarity: the warning is a form of mercy because it transfers knowledge *from* the moment when it can no longer help *to* the moment when it can. The same verse that threatens is the verse that saves, because a person who believes the threat today is a person who will not suffer it tomorrow.

This is the deeper reason the sūrah can move so swiftly from warning to wonder. The very next verse, verse 6, begins the cascade of creation-signs — *"Did We not make the earth a resting place?"* — and the transition is not a change of subject but a change of *mode*. Having warned, the sūrah now reasons; having announced the knowledge that is coming, it now supplies the evidence that makes belief possible before the knowledge arrives. The two *kallā*s are thus not the end of the disbelievers' chance but the beginning of it. The sūrah's structure is a staircase of mercy: warning (vv. 4–5), evidence (vv. 6–16), detailed disclosure (vv. 17–40) — each step making the next possible, and all of them together making ignorance inexcusable.

**Certainty as a Reorganizing Force**

What does it do to a human being to be told, on divine authority, that he "will know"? The question is worth asking concretely, because the answer is the whole psychology of the sūrah. A person who lives as though there will be no reckoning arranges his life in a particular shape: he defers repentance, spends his strength on what decays, accumulates what he cannot keep, and treats the wrongs he commits as costless. A person who believes he "will know" arranges his life in the opposite shape: he hastens to good, spends on what endures, keeps his accounts short, and treats every wrong as a debt that will be collected. The difference between the two lives is not information but *certitude*, and the sūrah's opening is an act of certitude-induction: it plants, in two hammer-strokes, the knowledge that the present state of doubt is temporary and the future state of knowledge is fixed. Faith, in the Qur'anic sense, is largely this: living now as you will wish you had lived when the knowing comes.

There is a famous portrait of this psychology in the hadith. The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said, in a report recorded by al-Tirmidhī and others:

> *"The wise person is the one who takes himself to account and works for what is after death; and the incapable is the one who lets his soul follow its desires and yet hopes in God."* (al-Tirmidhī; a similar teaching appears in Ibn Mājah)

The "wise person" of this report is precisely the one who has internalized *sa-yaʿlamūn*: he knows he will know, so he acts now. The "incapable" is the one who has heard the same warning and let it slide into the category of things he will attend to later. The verse's two strokes are aimed at this procrastination: "they will know" is spoken now so that the knowing, when it comes, will find the hearer already turned. This is the sūrah's entire project in miniature — to collapse the distance between the future knowledge and the present act, so that the believer lives in the light of the Day before the Day dawns.

**The Seed of the Sūrah's Final Scene**

Finally, the two verses look forward to the sūrah's closing, and the connection is not accidental. The chapter ends with the single disbeliever beholding what his hands have sent forth and crying out *"Oh, would that I were dust!"* (78:40) — and that cry is the exact fulfillment of *sa-yaʿlamūn*. The "they will know" of the opening is, forty verses later, shown to be a scene: a man looking at the record of his life with a knowledge he can no longer use. The sūrah is thus a single arc from warning to fulfillment, from the announcement of knowledge to the picture of it. And because the believer has been allowed to witness that picture in advance — to read the sūrah and see the ending — he is given the very thing the man in the last verse lacks: the knowledge *before* it is too late. The doubled warning is the reader's privilege. To recite *thumma kallā sa-yaʿlamūn* is to be told the ending while the story is still being written, and to be handed, in that telling, the power to write a different ending.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:6

> **Did We not make the earth a resting place,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn from Warning to Wonder**

With verse 6 the sūrah changes its method entirely. The opening five verses were rebuke and warning — the question, the difference, the doubled "they will know." Now the tone drops from the prophetic warning to the parental question: *a-lam najʿal al-arḍa mihādā* — "Did We not make the earth a resting place?" The particle *a-lam* is the key to the whole movement: it is the Arabic interrogative with the negative, and it produces what grammarians call a *rhetorical question that expects the answer "yes."* The question is not a request for information but an appeal to the listener's own witness: "Have We not made...?" — and every hearer, believer or denier, must answer inwardly, "Yes, You have." The sūrah has stopped arguing and begun to *remind*, and the form it chooses is the form of the witness stand, where the listener himself is made to testify. This is the first of eight such questions — verses 6 through 16 — each one laying another undeniable fact before the denier until the denial has nowhere to stand.

The word *mihād* is rich. Its root is *m-h-d*, which gives the Arabic for "cradle" (*mahd*) and for the act of smoothing and preparing a bed. A *mihād* is a thing spread out and made level, a place prepared for rest — the bed that is spread for a guest, the cradle that is prepared for an infant, the even ground that is made ready before a traveler lies down. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both note that the earth is called a *mihād* because God flattened and spread it, then made it habitable, then set humanity upon it as a child is set in a cradle. The image is tender and precise: the earth is not a hostile surface we happened to survive on; it is a *prepared* surface, arranged in advance for our comfort. The verse's question thus carries an entire theology in its grammar — the earth is not self-made; it was *made* (*najʿal*, "We made"), and it was made *for* a purpose, and the purpose is rest.

**The Cradle Beneath Your Feet: A Sign You Stand On**

The Qur'an uses this same image of the earth as cradle and resting-place repeatedly, and the repetition is itself a teaching. In Sūrah Ṭā Hā, Moses is addressed at the burning bush with the words:

> *"And what is that in your right hand, O Moses?"* (Qur'an 20:17)

— and the whole encounter is framed by God's earlier declaration in the same sūrah:

> *"Who has made the earth a cradle (mahd) for you, and threaded for you ways therein, and sent down water from the sky..."* (Qur'an 20:53)

In Sūrah al-Zukhruf the same idea appears with an added note:

> *"He it is Who made the earth a cradle for you and made paths for you therein, that haply you may be guided."* (Qur'an 43:10)

The commentators read these verses together, and they draw out the same point each time: the earth's habitability is not a lucky accident but a *sign*, and a sign is not merely a wonder but a *pointer*. The earth points beyond itself. A person who walks on level ground, sleeps in safety, and plants in soil that yields, is walking on an argument — the argument that the One who smoothed this cradle is both powerful enough to have made it and purposeful enough to have made it *for* us, and that such a One is not to be doubted when He says He will gather us. This is the logic of the sūrah's middle section in a single verse: the resting place implies the Rest-Giver; the prepared bed implies the One who prepares; and the One who prepared the cradle can be trusted to convene the court.

There is also a second, subtler layer that the source commentary notes: the earth is a resting place not only for the body but for the *message*. God placed guidance *upon* the earth — the prophets, the scriptures, the mosques, the scholars — so that the earth is a cradle in two senses: a cradle for the body that rests and a cradle for the soul that is guided. The same earth that yields grain yields the reminders that point the soul toward its Lord. When the sūrah asks "Did We not make the earth a resting place?" it is asking about both rests at once — the rest of the body in sleep and the rest of the soul in guidance — and the rest of the chapter will unfold both.

**The Evidence of Arrangement: Why the Sign Convinces**

The logical force of this verse deserves careful statement, because the whole sūrah rests on it. The argument is a form of the argument from *order*: a thing that shows arrangement shows an arranger. The earth's fitness for human life is not a bare fact but a *coordinated* fact — its size and gravity, its distance from the sun, its atmosphere and water, its soil and seasons, all meshing together so that a creature with our exact needs can live and rest upon it. The Qur'an does not here lay out the coordination in the language of science; it compresses it into the single image of the *mihād*, the prepared bed, and lets the image do the work. A bed does not prepare itself; a cradle does not smooth its own surface. If the earth fits us the way a cradle fits an infant, the fitting is not an accident but a *making* — and the sūrah's question "Did *We* not make...?" is the assertion that the making has a Maker.

The commentators extend this logic in a direction that bears directly on the Resurrection. If the earth was *prepared* for the body, then the body has a purpose on the earth; and if the body has a purpose, then its life here is not a meaningless interval but a *term* that leads somewhere. The same Maker who spread the cradle and set the term can, at the term's end, gather what He spread. The disbelievers' error, as the Qur'an frames it, is not that they doubt God's power but that they doubt His *purpose* — and the earth's purposiveness is the standing refutation of that doubt. A God who made nothing by accident will not end anything by accident; the cradle implies the return to the One who prepared it.

**A Question That Includes the Questioner**

There is a pastoral genius in the verse's form that should not be missed. By asking rather than asserting, the sūrah *includes* the denier in its argument. "Did We not make...?" is addressed directly to the "they" of the opening verses — the very people who question one another — and it makes each of them the witness in his own trial. They cannot answer "No" without absurdity; they cannot answer "Yes" without conceding the first premise of everything they deny. The question-form is thus a form of mercy: it does not club the denier with a truth he is not ready to receive; it *leads* him, step by step, through truths he already holds, until the conclusion he resists is the only one left standing. This is why the sūrah's middle section is composed entirely of questions, and why each question is about something the hearer has touched, seen, or felt: the ground, the mountains, his own paired nature, his sleep, the night, the day, the sky, the sun, the rain. The argument never leaves the hearer's own experience, because the goal is not to import a foreign truth but to *uncover* a truth the hearer already knows and has suppressed.

**The Modern Ear and the Prepared Earth**

The verse speaks with undiminished force to a modern hearer, though the modern hearer may need the image translated. The earth is a *mihād* — a prepared resting place — and contemporary science, far from dissolving this, has made the preparation more astonishing than the seventh century could have imagined. The earth's orbit, its magnetic field that shields the surface, its precise chemical endowment of water and atmosphere, its distance from a stable star, its self-regulating cycles — every one of these is a thread in the cradle's fabric, and the fabric, the more closely it is examined, the more it looks woven. The Qur'an's method is vindicated by its own time-traveling: it did not need to wait for modern astronomy to make its point, because it made the point from the one piece of evidence every human being has always had — the simple, reliable, gift-like fact that the ground beneath his feet holds him and lets him rest. The verse is an invitation to walk outside, feel the solidity of the earth, and ask the question the sūrah asks — not "Did We not make...?" to no one in particular, but to oneself, with the answer already forming: yes, and therefore I owe the Maker of the cradle the response the cradle demands.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:7

> **and the mountains stakes,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pegs That Hold the Earth's Fabric**

The second question in the sūrah's cascade is *wa-al-jibāla awtādā* — "and the mountains stakes?" — continuing the syntax of "Did We not make...?" from the verse before. The image is exact and striking: *awtād* is the plural of *watad*, the peg or stake driven into the ground to anchor a tent. The Bedouin audience of the first revelation knew the word intimately: their entire dwelling was a tent, and a tent's stability depended wholly on its pegs — the ropes might be strong and the cloth sound, but without pegs driven deep, the first wind would carry the whole structure away. By calling the mountains *awtād*, the Qur'an says to that audience: what the peg does for your tent, the mountain does for the earth — it is driven deep, it anchors, it steadies. The comparison is drawn from the hearer's own domestic life, and it is therefore immediately felt: the mountains are the pegs of the world, and the world is a tent whose occupant never notices the pegs until they are gone.

The classical commentators develop this in two complementary directions. The first is *physical*: the mountains steady the earth and prevent it from shaking. This is the Qur'an's own repeated statement elsewhere:

> *"And He cast into the earth firm mountains, lest it should shake with you..."* (Qur'an 16:15)

and again in Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ:

> *"And We placed in the earth firm mountains, lest it should shake with them, and We made therein broad passes, that haply they may be guided."* (Qur'an 21:31)

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both transmit this understanding: the mountains are the earth's stabilizers, the weights that hold its surface from convulsion, exactly as a peg holds a tent from collapse. The second direction is *providential*: the mountains are not only stabilizers but *refuges* and *storehouses*. The Qur'an says God has made in the mountains places of shelter and dwelling — *"And God has made for you, from what He created, shade, and made for you in the mountains places of refuge"* (16:81) — and the ancient peoples the Qur'an describes as carving homes out of rock (cf. 15:82; 26:149) lived precisely by this provision. The mountain is thus a triple gift: it anchors, it shelters, and it stores — water in its snows, minerals in its veins, pasture in its folds.

**The Root and the Reality: What a Watad Actually Is**

The lexicographers note that *watad* is one of those Arabic words whose every derivative carries the sense of *fixing into the ground*. The verb *watada* means to drive in and make firm; the same root gives the tent-peg itself, and by extension anything made fast and immovable. The choice of the word is therefore not arbitrary imagery but a claim about function: the mountain is to the earth what the driven peg is to the tent — the thing that makes the whole stable by being itself fixed. And there is a further precision in the plural: the mountains are not one peg but *many* — *awtād* — distributed across the earth's surface as pegs are distributed around a tent's circumference, so that the stability is everywhere and not merely local. The single word *awtād* thus contains a whole cartography: the earth's surface mapped with its anchors, each mountain a driven point holding the fabric taut.

It is worth connecting this to the previous verse's image. Verse 6 called the earth a *mihād*, a cradle or spread bed; verse 7 now gives the spread surface its anchors. The two images belong together: a bed that is spread needs its corners fixed, and the mountains are the fixing. The sūrah is building a picture of the world as a *prepared dwelling* — the floor spread, the corners pegged, and (as the coming verses will show) the roof raised, the lamp lit, and the water sent down. Each question adds one element to the dwelling until, by verse 16, the hearer has been walked through the entire house of the world and shown that every room is a provision. The mountains are the second room: the anchors that keep the house from moving.

**What the Mountain Teaches About Power and Care**

The commentators draw two lessons from the mountain, and they are the twin lessons of every sign in this sūrah: *power* and *care*. The mountain is a display of power because it is immovable — the most massive thing the hearer knows, the thing that, in the ancient imagination, was the very image of permanence. The Qur'an itself will later use the mountain as the standard of weight against which human presumption is measured:

> *"Had We sent down this Qur'an upon a mountain, you would have seen it humbled, rent asunder by the fear of God."* (Qur'an 59:21)

Yet the same mountain, massive as it is, is also a display of *care*: it is not merely heavy; it is heavy *for us* — anchored to steady the earth we walk on, folded to shelter us, stocked to feed us. The sign's logic is therefore the logic of the whole sūrah: power and care are not rivals in God but one reality. The God who can drive the mountain into the earth is the God who drives it in *for our benefit*; the same might that could crush is the might that steadies. This is precisely the answer the sūrah is giving to the disbelievers' doubt about the Resurrection: they doubt that God will gather them, and the sūrah points to the mountain and says — the One who pegged the earth for you will not lose track of you; the power that anchors a continent is not exhausted by that feat, and the care that arranged the tent arranges also the return.

There is a further, quieter lesson in the image of the peg. A peg works *hidden*. Most of its length is underground; only its head is visible. The mountain, too, works in ways the eye does not see — its roots in the earth, its role in the balance of wind and water and climate, its slow provision of what the valleys need. The sūrah's choice of this image is therefore also a lesson in *humility before hidden causes*: the things that hold a life together are often the things one does not see, and the most obvious visible reality — a mountain — is itself only the visible head of an invisible function. The denier who scoffs at the Resurrection because he cannot see it is, the image suggests, like a man who denies the peg because he only sees the tent: the invisible is not the unreal, and the hidden cause is the cause of everything visible.

**The Mountains and the Coming Day**

Finally, the sūrah plants here a seed it will harvest twenty verses later. The mountains are introduced in verse 7 as the earth's immovable pegs — the very image of stability. But in verse 20, on the Day of Division, the same mountains will be *set in motion* — *"and the mountains are set in motion as if they were a mirage"* (78:20). The sūrah's rhetoric depends on this reversal: the thing it held up as the peak of permanence will be shown, on the Day, to be a mere mirage before the power that made it. The peg that held the tent will be pulled; the anchor that steadied the earth will be lifted; and the hearer who took the mountain as the final symbol of an unshakable world will learn that only God is unshakable. This is the sūrah's method in miniature: build up the world's solidity, then dissolve it on the Day, so that the contrast itself becomes the argument. The mountain that stabilizes is the mountain that will be moved — and the lesson of both facts is the same: the stability was always borrowed, and the Borrower is the only One who does not change.

For the modern reader, the verse's invitation is concrete. Stand before any mountain — or its photograph, or the memory of it — and the question the sūrah asks assembles itself: Did We not make the mountains stakes? The same question works on the skeptic as on the believer, because it begins from something both concede: the mountain is there, it is massive, and it does not fall. The only difference is the next step the hearer takes — whether he stops at the massiveness or follows it to the Maker of massiveness, and from the Maker to the Day the Maker has appointed. The sūrah is confident that the second step is the reasonable one, and it has built its whole argument to make taking it feel less like a leap than like walking on level ground.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:8

> **and create you in pairs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn from the World to the Self**

The third question changes direction in a way that deserves to be felt. The first two questions pointed *outward and downward* — at the earth spread as a cradle, at the mountains driven as pegs. The third points *at the hearer himself*: *wa-khalaqnākum azwājā* — "and [did We not] create you in pairs?" The pronoun shifts from "the earth" and "the mountains" to "you" — *khalaqnākum*, "We created *you*" — and in that shift the sūrah's argument takes its decisive turn. A person can look at the earth and the mountains and remain a spectator, admiring from a distance. But the sign in this verse is the person's *own constitution*: he is not looking at a wonder; he *is* the wonder. The question "Did We not create you in pairs?" has no possible answer except "Yes, You did," and no possible detachment, because the answer is written in the questioner's own body, his own origin, his own most intimate reality.

The word *azwāj* is the plural of *zawj*, and its meaning in Arabic is double and important. *Zawj* means a *pair* and also a *mate* — a *spouse*. It is used of the two members of any matched couple, and most commonly of husband and wife. The root sense is of *pairing, coupling, joining* — two things made to fit and complete each other. So the verse carries at least two meanings at once, and the commentators unfold both. First and foremost, "We created you in pairs" means *We created you male and female* — the human race in its two complementary sexes. The Qur'an states this elsewhere with the same word:

> *"And that He creates the two mates (zawjayn) — the male and the female."* (Qur'an 53:45)

But the verse also reaches further, as the commentators note, to the *pairedness of all things* — the fact that the whole creation is made in complementary opposites:

> *"And of all things We created pairs (zawjayn), that haply you may remember."* (Qur'an 51:49)

The human being is thus a sign twice over: he is himself a pair, and he lives inside a creation that is all pairs.

**Male and Female: The First Human Sign**

The primary reading — male and female — is the one the commentators give most weight, and for a reason the sūrah's own structure reveals. The previous verses described the dwelling God prepared: the cradle-earth, the peg-mountains. This verse introduces the *inhabitants* — and the first fact about the inhabitants is that they come in two kinds, made for each other. The sequence is not random: the home is prepared (vv. 6–7), and then the family is formed (v. 8), and then the rhythms of the family's life — sleep, night, day, livelihood — are laid out (vv. 9–11). The sūrah is describing a *household economy of grace*: God built the house, then made the people, then arranged their rest and their work. The creation of the two sexes is the hinge of the whole passage, because from it flows everything human — love, family, generation, and the transmission of faith itself.

The Qur'an's teaching about the pair is developed most beautifully in Sūrah al-Rūm, and the commentators habitually read this verse in its light:

> *"And among His signs is that He created for you mates from among yourselves, that you might find rest in them, and He placed between you affection and mercy. Truly in that are signs for a people who reflect."* (Qur'an 30:21)

Three ideas in that verse unlock the meaning of *azwāj* here. First, the mate is created *from among yourselves* — the spouse is not an alien but a same-kind counterpart, which is why the pairing works at all. Second, the purpose of the pair is *rest* — the same word-family (*sakana*, to be still, to dwell) that gave the earth as a resting place in verse 6; the spouse is the soul's resting place, the human answer to the human need for stillness in another. Third, the pair is held together by *affection and mercy* — two gifts placed by God between the two hearts, so that the pairing is not merely biological but moral. The sūrah's "We created you in pairs" is therefore not a statement about anatomy but about *providence*: the deepest human need — the need for another — was met by design before it was ever felt.

**The Logic of the Pair: Complementarity as a Sign**

Why does the pairedness of humanity point to God? The commentators' answer is a model of Qur'anic reasoning. A pair is a *designed* thing: two halves that fit are two halves that were *made* to fit. The complementarity of male and female — physical, emotional, and in the whole shape of a shared life — is not the sort of thing that falls into place by accident; it is the signature of an arranger. And the fit is not merely functional but *generative*: from the pair comes the child, and from the child the next pair, so that the entire human world is a continuous stream of pairing — a river whose every wave repeats the same design. The denier who asks "How can God bring me back after death?" is standing, the sūrah points out, inside the answer: he is himself the product of a pairing, a creature brought into being from two parents, by a process he did not design and cannot repeat; and the One who began him can begin him again. The Qur'an makes exactly this argument against the resurrection-deniers in Sūrah Yā Sīn, and it uses the same creation-logic:

> *"Does not man see that We created him from a drop? And behold, he is a clear adversary. And he strikes for Us a similitude and forgets his own creation..."* (Qur'an 36:77–78)

The point is devastating in its gentleness: the denier who argues against his own re-creation has already been created once — from a drop, through a pair — and the first creation is the standing proof of the possibility of the second.

**All Things in Pairs: The Wider Wonder**
