The second, wider reading of *azwāj* — the pairedness of all things — extends the sign from the human to the cosmic. The Qur'an's own statement in 51:49, *"And of all things We created pairs,"* was read by the commentators in two complementary ways. Some took it as the observation of *complementary opposites* throughout creation: night and day, sky and earth, land and sea, male and female, life and death — the whole world built on the rhythm of paired opposites. Others, and notably the later commentators who had the benefit of generations of natural observation, took it as pointing to the *pairedness within things themselves* — a claim whose depth modern science has only amplified, since the deeper one looks into matter, the more one finds pairs: positive and negative charge, particle and antiparticle, the two strands of the double helix, the pairings that structure chemistry and life itself. The sūrah's audience did not need modern physics to feel the force of the verse; the human pair, the pair of night and day, the pair of the two hands and the two eyes, were evidence enough. But the modern reader has the remarkable privilege of discovering that the verse's claim is *truer than its first hearers could measure*: the pairedness the Qur'an pointed to in humanity turns out to run through the entire fabric of existence.

And the commentators add a subtle epistemological point about why pairedness is a sign: we *know things through their opposites*. Al-Rāzī transmits this reading of 51:49 — that the creation in pairs is the condition of knowledge itself, because a thing is known by contrast with its other. We know light through darkness, rest through weariness, health through sickness, good through evil, and life through death. The creation of pairs is thus not only an arrangement of things but an arrangement of *meaning*: the world is legible because it is paired. And a world made legible is a world made by a Maker who intends to be known — which is the exact conclusion the sūrah is driving toward: the signs are not decorations but *letters*, and the pairs are the alphabet.

**The Pair as a Daily Sign and a Daily Duty**

For the believer, the verse's practical teaching flows directly from its meaning. If the spouse is a *sign* and a *rest*, then marriage is not merely a social arrangement but a form of worship lived out — the pair is a place where God's providence is touched daily. The affection and mercy God placed between the two hearts are a trust to be tended; the rest the spouse provides is a gift to be reciprocated. And the verse's placement in the sūrah gives it a further, eschatological edge: the very pairs God created will be gathered before Him together, and the sūrah's own final movement will picture the believers in Paradise *in pairs* — *"buxom maidens of like age"* for the men (78:33), and, as the tradition consistently teaches, the believing spouses reunited, the pairs restored in the Garden without the sorrows of the world. The pair that begins in a cradle on earth is completed in a Garden; the *zawj* of this world is the seed of the *azwāj* of the next. The verse, read in the light of the sūrah's ending, is thus a promise wrapped in a question: the One who paired you here will pair you — or re-pair you — there, and the affection and mercy of the first pairing are a foretaste of the second.

**The Modern Question, Answered by the Modern Self**

The modern person, told "you will be resurrected and judged," often replies with the question of the sūrah's first verses: how can this be? The sūrah's answer in this verse is disarmingly simple, and it works on the modern hearer exactly as it worked on the Quraysh: *look at yourself*. You were not always here; you came into being through a pair, from a cell you could not see, by a process you did not command; and you grew into a creature with a heart, a mind, and a conscience that cannot be accounted for by any arrangement of your own. Your own existence is the miracle you carry around and forget. The One who did that is not perplexed by the smaller task of doing it again. The verse's question — "Did We not create you in pairs?" — is the sūrah's way of turning the denier's gaze from the distant sky to the nearest evidence, and the nearest evidence is always the self. There is no human being so poor in signs that he is without this one: he is here, and he was made, and he was made with a mate — and all three facts testify to the Maker the sūrah is announcing.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:9

> **and make your sleep repose,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Daily Death and Resurrection**

The fourth question turns to the most ordinary thing in a human life — sleep — and makes of it one of the sūrah's most powerful signs: *wa-jaʿalnā nawmakum subātā* — "and [did We not] make your sleep a repose?" The word *subāt* is precise and untranslatable in a single English word. Its root is *s-b-t*, which carries the core meaning of *cutting off* — the same root from which the Sabbath (*sabt*) derives, because the Sabbath is the day on which work is *cut off*. So *subāt* is not mere sleep but *the cutting off* — the suspension, the severance of the self from the world of waking. When a person sleeps, the Qur'an is saying, he is *cut off*: from sensation, from activity, from choice, from the stream of daily life; his body lies where it fell, his tongue is still, his limbs are quiet, and for those hours he is, to all outward appearance, gone. The word thus carries a deliberate echo of death, and the commentators did not miss it. They read this verse together with the Qur'an's own explicit statements that sleep is the lesser death:

> *"He it is Who takes your souls by night..."* (Qur'an 6:60)

and:

> *"God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that die not, in their sleep..."* (Qur'an 39:42)

Sleep, on this reading, is the soul's daily rehearsal: every night God takes the soul, and every morning He returns it — or does not. The waking to a new day is thus a miniature resurrection, and every sleeper is daily shown, in his own body, the very thing the disbelievers call impossible.

**The Meaning of the Word: What Sleep Actually Is**

The lexicographers and commentators unfold *subāt* with care because so much of the verse's force depends on it. Al-Qurṭubī records the basic sense as *rest* — the repose that sleep gives the body after the wear of the day — and this is the first, obvious layer: God made sleep a mercy, a nightly restoration, the built-in repair that the body performs on itself without the self's command. But he and others also transmit the deeper sense of *cutting off*: sleep severs the sleeper from perception and from the senses, so that he is, in effect, absent while present. The two senses together give the full picture: sleep is the *cutting off that restores* — the daily interruption that makes the daily resumption possible. A creature that could not be cut off could not be restored; the capacity for sleep is the condition of the capacity for work. The sūrah is pointing to this rhythm as a *sign*: the alternation of waking and sleeping is a designed cycle, and a designed cycle points to a designer.

There is a further precision in the word's derivation from the Sabbath root. Just as the Sabbath cuts off the week's work, sleep cuts off the day's work — both are God's legislated interruptions of human toil, and both teach the same lesson: the human being is not a machine of continuous labor but a creature whose rhythm includes divinely appointed stoppages. The one who invented sleep is the one who invented the Sabbath, and both inventions are mercies aimed at the same need — the need to stop, to be restored, to remember that the world does not depend on one's unceasing effort. The sūrah's word choice thus smuggles a whole theology of rest into a single noun: rest is not an absence but a *provision*, not a defeat but a *gift*.

**Sleep as a Sign of the Resurrection: The Argument from the Daily Miracle**

The commentators' deepest use of this verse is as an argument for the Resurrection, and it is one of the most elegant arguments the Qur'an anywhere deploys. Its form is: *you have already experienced resurrection; you call it waking up.* Every night the sleeper's conscious life is suspended — his awareness, his agency, his very selfhood are cut off, so that a watching stranger could not tell the sleeper from the dead. Every morning that self is returned — not partially but wholly, the same "I" restored to the same body, with memory and identity intact. The denier who says "God cannot bring me back after death" has, the sūrah implies, been brought back *thousands of times*; he has been resurrected every morning of his life and has not noticed, because the daily miracle is too reliable to seem miraculous. The Qur'an makes the argument explicit in Sūrah al-Anʿām:

> *"And He it is Who takes your souls by night and knows what you commit by day; then He raises you up therein, that a term appointed may be fulfilled..."* (Qur'an 6:60)

"Then He raises you up" — the word for waking is the word for resurrection, because in the Qur'an's lexicon they are the same event at different scales. Sleep is the small resurrection rehearsed daily; the Day of Judgment is the great resurrection performed once. The One who manages the small one without fail will manage the great one without fail; the only difference is that the great one is final.

The psychological power of this argument is that it needs no preacher and no book: it is re-enacted in every bedroom, in every life, on every night, from the first human being to the last. No culture, no era, no person has ever been without this daily proof. The sūrah's genius is to take the most universal and least regarded fact of human existence — the fact that you fall asleep and wake up — and turn it into the standing refutation of the denial it is answering. The disbelievers "question one another" about the Resurrection, and all the while they are each, every night, demonstrating its possibility in their own bodies.

**The Two Risks of Sleep: Why the Waking Is a Grace**

There is a darker edge to the sign that the commentators also mark, and it gives the verse its urgency. If sleep is the cutting off of the soul, then every sleep is a *risk*: the soul is taken, and its return is not owed but granted. The sleeper who lies down has no guarantee of rising; the Qur'an's word *subāt* — the cutting off — is a reminder that the severance is real and that only God's returning of the soul prevents it from being final. This is why the Prophet ﷺ taught specific supplications at the threshold of sleep, prayers that acknowledge both the mercy and the risk. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the most famous of them, which he taught to al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib:

> *"O God, in Your Name I die and I live."* (al-Bukhārī 6312; Muslim 2711)

— *bismika Allāhumma amūtu wa-aḥyā*: the sleeper places his soul in God's keeping, confessing that lying down is a kind of dying and that only God's hand will raise him. And on waking, the Prophet ﷺ taught:

> *"Praise be to God who gave us life after He had caused us to die, and to Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312; Muslim 2711)

— *al-ḥamdu lillāhi alladhī aḥyānā baʿda mā amātanā wa-ilayhi al-nushūr*: the morning itself is a liturgy, the waking is a prayer, and the words "to Him is the resurrection" tie the daily waking to the final one explicitly. The Prophet ﷺ thus taught his community to live the sign of this verse every night and every morning — to go to sleep as one who dies, and to wake as one who is resurrected, so that the great Resurrection, when it comes, finds a people for whom it is the completion of a familiar rhythm rather than a foreign terror.

**Sleep, Rest, and the Modern Exhaustion**

The verse's first-layer meaning — sleep as *repose*, as a gift of rest — has a sharp contemporary application that the sūrah's original audience would have felt differently than we do. The Qur'an presents sleep as a *mercy*: God *made* it, deliberately, as part of the design — *"And We made your sleep for rest"* (78:9), and elsewhere *"And We made your sleep a cessation"* and *"And of His mercy He made for you the night and the day, that you might rest therein..."* (28:73). A culture that treats sleep as an enemy of productivity, that prides itself on sleeplessness, that fills the night with screens and noise and anxiety, has quietly inverted the sign: it takes the mercy and turns it into a liability. The sūrah's word is a corrective: sleep is not the failure of the self to keep going but the provision of the Maker to restore the self. To receive sleep with gratitude — to lie down trusting that the soul's return is in God's hand — is itself an act of faith, and the nightly surrender is a daily training in the larger surrender that faith requires. The believer who sleeps well sleeps *religiously*: he has placed his selfhood in trust, confessed that he cannot hold even his own consciousness together through the night, and accepted that he will be returned — if returned at all — by the One who never sleeps and never dies.

The verse, then, does far more than list sleep among the world's wonders. It converts the most ordinary fact of a human day into a standing proof of the chapter's central claim, and it hands the believer a nightly and morning liturgy in which that proof is personally re-enacted. Between the cradle-earth of verse 6 and the garment-night of verse 10, the sleep of verse 9 is the hinge of the human creature's whole existence — the daily death that teaches the final death, the daily rising that teaches the final rising, and the daily rest that teaches, to anyone who will notice, that the whole arrangement is a mercy from a Maker who does not forget His sleeping servants.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:10

> **and make the night a garment,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Darkness Worn Like Clothing**

The fifth question continues the sūrah's tour of the human day, moving from sleep to its setting: *wa-jaʿalnā al-layla libāsā* — "and [did We not] make the night a garment?" The image is one of the most compressed and beautiful in the whole chapter. The night — that vast, impersonal darkness that covers half the earth at every moment — is described as a *libās*, a garment, a piece of clothing. The word *libās* is from the root *l-b-s*, which in Arabic gives both *libās* (clothing) and *iltibās* (confusion, obscurity, the mixing of things so that they cannot be told apart). Both senses are alive in the verse. The night is a garment because it *covers*: it is drawn over the world as a robe is drawn over a body, hiding what it covers from sight. And it is a garment because it *confuses* or *obscures*: in the night, things that the day kept distinct — near and far, friend and stranger, path and pit — become indistinct. The single word thus names both the night's comfort and its danger, and the commentators unfold both without choosing between them, because the Qur'an intends both.

The Qur'an uses the same image in the same sense elsewhere, and the commentators read the passages together. In Sūrah al-Furqān:

> *"And He it is Who made the night a garment (libās) for you, and sleep for rest, and made the day a rising."* (Qur'an 25:47)

The three items — garment, sleep, rising — are the same cluster the sūrah is here unfolding, and the sequence is instructive: the night is the *covering*, sleep is the *cutting off*, and the day is the *rising*. The night is the garment in which sleep is worn; the day is the waking in which the garment is removed. The human creature's entire existence is, in this Qur'anic picture, a matter of being covered and uncovered, hidden and revealed, by a rhythm God designed.

**The Garment as Protection and as Concealment**

The commentators develop the garment image in two directions, and both are essential. The first is *protection and comfort*. A garment shields the body from cold and exposure; the night shields the world from the full blaze of the sun and gives the earth, the plants, the animals, and the weary human body their season of cool and rest. The Qur'an says explicitly elsewhere that God made the night for rest — *"He it is Who made the night for you that you might rest therein"* (10:67), and *"He has made the night for repose"* (6:96). The night is thus a mercy in the form of a covering: the darkness is not a threat but a *shade* — a daily shelter drawn over the laboring world, so that the toil of the day can be followed by the repair of the night. The garment protects.

The second direction is subtler and more searching: the night as *concealment*. The same covering that shields the sleeper also hides the sinner. The Qur'an does not flinch from this; the night's darkness is, in the moral life, a place where deeds are done in secret — and the sūrah's word *libās*, with its echo of *iltibās* (confusion, obscurity), hints at it. The darkness that lets the weary rest also lets the thief move and the slanderer whisper; the covering that is mercy to the innocent is cover for the guilty. The Qur'an elsewhere swears by the night precisely in its character as a concealer — *"By the night when it covers"* (92:1) — and the oaths of the early sūrahs trade constantly on this double nature: the night is both God's mercy and the scene of man's hidden deeds. The commentators draw the moral: the night's concealment is a *test*, because what a person does when no one sees is the truest index of what he is. The covering that hides him from human eyes cannot hide him from the One who made the covering; the darkness is transparent to its Maker. The night is thus the daily arena of *taqwā* — the fear of God in secret — and the Qur'an reserves its highest praise for those whose deeds survive the test of concealment:

> *"...who fear their Lord in secret..."* (Qur'an 21:49; cf. 35:18; 67:12)

**The Night as Spiritual Retreat**

There is a third, and for the sūrah's purposes climactic, sense in which the night is a garment: it is the *cloak of the worshipper*. The same darkness that hides the sinner hides the devotee, and the tradition made much of this. The night vigil — *qiyām al-layl* — is the prayer performed under the cover of darkness, and its entire value, the Qur'an says, lies in what the covering makes possible: solitude, sincerity, the unobserved conversation between the servant and his Lord. The sūrah's companion passage in Sūrah al-Muzzammil praises the night for exactly this:

> *"Truly the vigil of the night is firmest in tread and most upright for speech."* (Qur'an 73:6)

The darkness strips away the audience; the covering removes the stage; and what remains is prayer that no one applauds and no one sees. Ibn ʿArabī, in his Makkan Openings, speaks of the "people of the night" (*aṣḥāb al-layl*) for whom the night is a cloak precisely because it covers them from the eyes of others and builds them a private retreat (*khalwah*) with God. The garment of night is thus, in its highest use, the garment of *intimacy*: the believer wraps himself in the darkness not to hide sin but to find solitude, and the covering that conceals him from the world reveals him to his Lord. The night, which the heedless use as cover for disobedience, the devout use as cover for devotion; the same *libās* is the thief's mask and the worshipper's veil.

**The Garment and the Giver: What the Covering Teaches**

As a sign, the night teaches the same two lessons as every sign in this sūrah: *power* and *care*. It is a display of power because nothing is more overwhelming than the daily descent of darkness over half a planet — an event so vast and so regular that humanity has built its entire existence upon its reliability, and no human power has ever delayed it by a moment. It is a display of care because the darkness is *for* us: it is measured to our needs, drawn over the world at the exact rhythm that living things require, so that the garment fits the sleeper as a robe fits the one it was made for. The Qur'an's rhetorical question — "Did We not make the night a garment?" — gathers both: the One who draws the covering is the One who sized it, and the One who sized it is the One who will, in the sūrah's final movement, draw a different covering over the world — the opened sky of the Day of Division. The garment of night is, once again, a seed the sūrah will harvest later: the covering that is drawn each evening will be pulled back on the Day, and what the darkness hid will be revealed.

There is also a lesson in *sequence* here that the commentators note. The sūrah's order — earth, mountains, pairs, sleep, night, day — is not arbitrary but a *narrative of the human day and the human home*. The earth is the bed, the mountains its pegs, the pair its inhabitants, sleep its rest, the night its covering, and (in the next verse) the day its arena of labor. The whole passage is a single picture: a home prepared for a family, and a rhythm arranged for its life. The night is the home's *roof of darkness*, lowered each evening over the resting household. No element is decorative; each is load-bearing. And the cumulative effect, by the time the cascade reaches its end, is that the denier has been shown his entire lived environment as a constructed mercy — and asked, of each element in turn, whether he really believes it made itself.

**The Modern Night and the Lost Garment**

The verse has a particular poignancy for the modern reader, because the modern world has, in a real sense, *lost the night as a garment*. Electric light has abolished darkness in the cities; the glow of screens has invaded the bedroom; and the covering that once wrapped the world in rest is now thinned, postponed, and at war with the very rhythms it was made to protect. The Qur'an's question — "Did We not make the night a garment?" — reads, in this context, as a gentle rebuke of a civilization that has forgotten the purpose of the covering it still, stubbornly, receives. The night remains; its darkness still falls; but its intended uses — rest, concealment for the honest, retreat for the devout — are increasingly crowded out by a manufactured day that never ends. The believer is invited by this verse to *reclaim* the garment: to let the night be night, to receive its darkness as a mercy, to use its cover for the two things it was made for — the repair of the body and the solitude of the soul. In an age of sleepless brightness, the verse's sign is a summons back to the rhythm: put on the garment, lie down under the covering, and let the One who drew it over you hold your soul until the rising.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:11

> **and the day for livelihood?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rising and the Arena of Work**

The sixth question completes the night's pair: *wa-jaʿalnā al-nahāra maʿāshā* — "and [did We not] make the day for livelihood?" The word *maʿāsh* is built on the root *ʿ-y-sh*, "to live," and it means *the means of living, the arena of livelihood, the time and place in which life is earned*. The day, the sūrah says, is the *maʿāsh* — the space of activity in which human beings go out and seek what sustains them. The contrast with the preceding verses is exact and deliberate: the night was a *garment* (covering, rest, concealment), and the day is a *livelihood* (uncovering, labor, pursuit). The two verses together are the Qur'an's account of the human economy of time: darkness for rest and concealment, light for work and earning; the garment for the body's repair, the arena for the body's provision. The sūrah is describing the *rhythm of life* itself, and it is asking the denier whether he really believes this rhythm — on which his every day has always depended — is an accident.

The commentators connect this verse to the same Qur'anic passages that illuminate verse 9, because the two belong to one movement. In Sūrah al-Anʿām, God says He takes the souls by night and *raises them* by day — and the word for "raise" in that verse is the word of resurrection, as we saw. The full verse reads:

> *"And He it is Who takes your souls by night and knows what you commit by day; then He raises you up therein, that a term appointed may be fulfilled; then to Him is your return..."* (Qur'an 6:60)

The commentators read this and the sūrah's verses 9–11 as a single teaching: night is the taking, day is the raising, and the alternation of the two is the daily enactment, in miniature, of death and resurrection. The sleeper dies into the night and is raised into the day; the day is the *resurrected* interval, the stretch of light in which the raised soul goes about its work. The sūrah's "day for livelihood" is thus not a separate thought from "sleep as repose" and "night as garment" but the completion of them: the whole cycle — garment, sleep, rising, labor — is one continuous sign of the same Maker and the same final Rising.

**The Logic of Light and Labor: Why the Day Is for Earning**

Why is the day suited to livelihood? The commentators' answer is plain and rooted in observation: the day is *light*, and light is the condition of action. In the light, a person sees — sees the path, the tool, the trade, the danger, the opportunity. Livelihood is the pursuit of what one needs, and pursuit requires vision; the day is the provision of vision on a planetary scale. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the connection between light and earning explicit in the same breath as it makes the connection between darkness and rest:

> *"Say: Have you considered, if God were to make the night perpetual over you until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God could bring you light? Will you not then hear? ... And if God were to make the day perpetual over you until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God could bring you night, that you might rest therein? Will you not then see? And of His mercy He made for you the night and the day, that you might rest therein and seek of His bounty, and that haply you may give thanks."* (Qur'an 28:71–73)

That passage is the fullest statement of the sūrah's teaching here, and it names the three purposes of the alternation: *rest* (in the night), *seeking bounty* (in the day), and *thanks* (as the response to both). The day is the arena of *seeking* — the Qur'an's own word is *tabtaghū min faḍlihi*, "that you might seek of His bounty" — and the sūrah's *maʿāsh* is the same arena named by another word. Livelihood, in the Qur'anic vision, is not merely permitted in the day; it is the day's *purpose*, the reason the light was made.

This is an important point against a certain religious misunderstanding. The Qur'an nowhere denigrates earning a living; on the contrary, it presents the day's labor as part of the design. The sūrah's rhetorical question — "Did We not make the day for livelihood?" — includes earning among the *signs*, among the mercies, alongside the earth and the mountains and the pairs and the sleep. Work is not the enemy of the spiritual life; it is one of its God-given rhythms. The Prophet ﷺ affirmed the dignity of labor in the strongest terms. In a report recorded by al-Bukhārī:

> *"No one has ever eaten food better than that earned by the work of his own hands; and indeed the Prophet of God, David, used to eat from the work of his own hands."* (al-Bukhārī 2072)

The day's livelihood is thus a *blessing to be pursued*, not a distraction to be endured — provided it is pursued within the limits God set and returned to God in gratitude. The sūrah's placement of the verse — after sleep and night, before the heavens and the rain — is itself a theology of work: labor stands in the middle of the created order, between rest and provision, as natural and as ordained as the sky above it.

**The Alternation as the Engine of Everything**

The commentators draw attention to the *alternation* itself — the fact that night and day do not merely exist but *succeed one another* — as the deeper sign. A perpetual day would burn the earth and exhaust its creatures; a perpetual night would freeze it and starve them. The livability of the world depends not on light alone or darkness alone but on their *measured rotation*. The Qur'an states this as a deliberate mercy in more than two dozen places, and it always ties the alternation to the purpose of knowledge and gratitude:

> *"God alternates the night and the day. Truly in that is a lesson for those possessed of sight."* (Qur'an 24:44)

The "lesson" is the sūrah's whole point: a rhythm is a *design*, and a design is a *sign*. The rotation of night into day and day into night is the most reliable event in any human life — the one thing a person can count on with total confidence — and its reliability is not self-explanatory but *sustained*. The Qur'an's word is that God *turns* the night into the day and the day into the night (cf. 22:61; 31:29; 35:13; 57:6) — the alternation is an ongoing act, a continuous providence, not a mechanism wound up once and left to run. The day that dawns is not a consequence of yesterday; it is a fresh gift, reissued each morning. And the sūrah's question — "Did We not make the day for livelihood?" — is an invitation to receive the day as exactly that: a fresh gift, sized for the day's work, from the One who will one day bring the alternation to an end.

**The Day as a Sign of the Final Rising**

The eschatological edge of the verse, noted by the commentators who read it with 6:60, deserves full statement, because it is the sūrah's argument in its most concentrated form. Every morning is a *resurrection*: the soul is returned, the body is raised, the world is relit, and the raised creature goes out to seek its livelihood. The disbeliever who denies the great Resurrection has, on this reading, *practiced* it every day of his life: he has died a thousand small deaths into sleep and risen a thousand small risings into the day, and the One who performed the small risings will perform the great one. The day itself is the standing refutation of the denial: a person who wakes is a person who has just been resurrected, and the only question is whether he will read the sign or sleep through it. The sūrah's cascade of questions is designed to produce exactly this waking: to turn the denier's attention to the daily miracle he performs without noticing, until the Resurrection stops being a foreign claim and becomes the completion of a rhythm he has known since birth.

The practical teaching follows directly. If the day is *for livelihood*, then the day has a purpose, and a purpose implies an accounting. The Qur'an's ethics of the day is consistent: earn, but earn lawfully; work, but do not let work make you forget the return; use the light, but remember the One who made the light. The sūrah will close, in verse 40, with the single human being beholding *what his hands have sent forth* — and that phrase, "what his hands have sent forth," is the language of the day's labor: the hands that worked the *maʿāsh* are the hands that sent ahead the record. The day's livelihood, spent in the light, becomes the seed of the Hereafter's account; the arena of earning is the arena of *sending forward*. The verse thus binds the sūrah's middle (the signs) to its end (the reckoning): the day was made for livelihood, the livelihood is the record, and the record is the reckoning. To work the day without remembering the Day is to build a livelihood on a foundation one has forgotten is temporary.

**Gratitude as the Bridge Between Day and Day**

Finally, the verse's place in a series of *questions* teaches the response the whole series is building toward. Each question — earth, mountains, pairs, sleep, night, day — expects the answer "Yes," and the accumulated "yes" is meant to issue in one word: *gratitude*. The Qur'an's own statement of the alternation's purpose, quoted above, ends with *"that haply you may give thanks"* — the rhythm of rest and work is arranged so that the human creature might *thank* the Arranger. The believer's day, on this teaching, is a continuous act of thanks: waking with the Prophet's prayer of praise, going out to the *maʿāsh* as a trust, earning lawfully, and returning the day's provision to its Giver in gratitude and charity. The disbeliever lives the same rhythm and reads nothing in it; the believer lives it and reads everything. The difference is not in the facts — both wake, both work, both sleep — but in the *recognition*. And the sūrah's question, asked of both alike, is the offer of that recognition: the day is a sign, the sign is a gift, and the gift is from the One who will ask, on the Day of Division, what you did with the light He gave you to seek by.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:12

> **And [did We not] build above you seven firmaments,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Roof That Is Also a Sign**

The seventh question turns the hearer's gaze upward for the first time: *wa-banaynā fawqakum sabʿan shidādā* — "and [did We not] build above you seven firmaments?" After six verses spent on the earth and the human creature's daily rhythms, the sūrah now points to the sky, and the change of direction is itself part of the argument: the same question that began under the hearer's feet now arches over his head. The earth was spread below; the heavens are built above; and between them stands the human being, enclosed by signs in every direction. The word *banaynā* — "We built" — is the language of construction, of deliberate raising, and it is chosen with precision: the heavens are not said to have *happened* or *condensed* or *appeared*; they are said to have been *built*, as a builder raises a structure. The choice of the verb is the verse's first theological statement: the sky is an edifice, and an edifice has an architect.

The number seven, *sabʿan*, has generated a long and careful discussion in the classical commentaries, and it deserves to be set out plainly. The Qur'an speaks in multiple places of "seven heavens" — *sabʿ samāwāt* — and the commentators are agreed that the phrase is not a casual figure. It appears in passages that treat the heavens as *stacked* layers of creation:

> *"It is He Who created for you all that is on the earth; then He turned to the heaven and fashioned it into seven heavens. And He knows all things."* (Qur'an 2:29)

> *"He it is Who created seven heavens in layers (ṭibāqan)..."* (Qur'an 67:3)

> *"And We built above you seven firmaments."* (Qur'an 78:12 — this very verse)

The word *shidād* in the present verse is the plural of *shadīd* — strong, firm, solid — and it describes the heavens as *rigid and unbroken*, without fissure, not subject to decay. The commentators, al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī among them, note the force of this: the heavens are built, and built *strong*, so that they hold their structure without sagging, leaking, or falling — and they are held, as the Qur'an says elsewhere, *without visible pillars*:

> *"God it is Who raised the heavens without pillars that you see..."* (Qur'an 13:2)

The sky is thus presented as the supreme display of *structural power*: a roof of immense span, raised and sustained, and yet hanging with no support the eye can detect — the daily miracle above every head, so constant that no one looks up.

**What the Seven Heavens Mean: Layers, Not Numbers**

The deepest question the commentators address is what the "seven" signifies, and their answers fall into two families that are best held together. The first family takes the number literally, as the count of *actual, distinct heavens* — seven created skies, one above another, each a realm of its own. The tradition transmitted from the Prophet ﷺ and the Companions fills these with detail: the first heaven is the nearest sky, adorned with stars; above it, in ascending order, stretch further heavens, each separated from the next by a distance known to God alone; and the seventh is the highest, beyond which lies the Kursī (the Footstool) and the Throne (cf. the Throne Verse, 2:255, and the discussions of the Prophet's ﷺ night journey, in which he ascended through the heavens one by one, meeting a prophet at each level, until he reached the Lote Tree of the Utmost Boundary). This is the picture of the *miʿrāj*, the ascension, recorded in al-Bukhārī and Muslim at length, and it treats the seven heavens as real places traversed in a real journey.

The second family takes the number as carrying a significance beyond mere counting, and here the tradition's reflections are richest. The number seven in Arabic — as in many cultures — is the number of *completeness and multiplicity*: it is used for a large, complete, or repeatedly counted quantity, as in the Qur'an's own idiom, *"Were the sea ink for the words of my Lord, the sea would be exhausted before the words of my Lord were exhausted, even if We brought the like of it as replenishment"* — where seven is the idiom of inexhaustibility (cf. 18:109; 31:27). On this reading, "seven heavens" is the Qur'an's way of saying *heavens beyond count* — the full, complete, layered vault of the cosmos, whatever its actual structure, which the human eye cannot enumerate and the human mind cannot hold. The two readings are not in conflict but in emphasis: the number is real, and it is also *pointing* — to a structure whose vastness is beyond the hearer's measurement. What both readings insist upon is the verse's central claim: the heavens are *built*, they are *layered*, they are *strong*, and they are *above you* — enclosing the human creature in a structure he did not raise and cannot rival.

**The Sign of the Unpillared Roof**

The commentators' deepest use of this verse is as an argument of power, and it runs parallel to the argument of the earth in verse 6. The earth below is a *cradle* — a sign of care; the heavens above are a *roof* — a sign of power. The two together frame the human creature in the two attributes the sūrah is weaving throughout: beneath his feet, mercy; above his head, might. And the argument against the resurrection-deniers is the same as before, only raised to a higher register: the One who built the seven firmaments and holds them without pillars is not a God whose power has limits; the same might that raised the roof can raise the dead. The Qur'an makes the argument explicit in the very passage that the sūrah's later verses echo — Sūrah Yā Sīn's answer to the man who asks who can revive decayed bones:

> *"Say: He will revive them Who originated them the first time; and He knows every creation."* (Qur'an 36:79)

and in Sūrah Qāf, where the argument from the heavens is stated outright:

> *"Have they not looked at the heaven above them, how We built it and adorned it, and it has no fissures? ... And the earth, We spread it out, and cast in it firm mountains, and caused to grow in it every kind of beautiful pair — as an insight and a reminder for every penitent servant."* (Qur'an 50:6–8)

That passage is almost a summary of the sūrah's own middle section — heaven built, earth spread, mountains cast, pairs grown — and its conclusion names the purpose of it all: *"as an insight and a reminder for every penitent servant."* The signs are not for the proud but for the *penitent* — the one who turns. The heavens are built so that the one who turns might see, in their strength, the strength of the Builder.

**The Firmament and the Modern Sky**

The verse's word *shidād* — strong, firm, without fissure — has an unexpected resonance for the modern reader, because the modern sky has been shown to be vaster and stranger than the seventh century imagined, and the verse's claim has only deepened. The "firmaments" above us — the atmosphere that shields the surface, the layers of the sky, the deep space beyond, the fabric of spacetime itself — are, in the precise language of modern physics, held in a structure of extraordinary integrity, and the more precisely we measure, the more the word "built" seems apt. The universe is not a heap; it is an *arrangement*, tuned and ordered at every scale. The sūrah's audience needed no telescope to feel the force of "We built above you seven firmaments" — they had only to look up at night. But the modern reader, who has looked up through instruments the seventh century could not dream of, finds the verse's claim *amplified* rather than embarrassed: the more of the roof we see, the more it looks raised by design. The sūrah's method — asking questions about what the hearer already sees — is thus future-proof: it builds its case from the one piece of evidence no era can outgrow, which is the sky itself.

There is also a modern *posture* the verse corrects. The heavens, in the Qur'an's presentation, are not an empty backdrop but a *sign*; the sky is not dead space but a *revelation*. A civilization that has learned to measure the sky but forgotten to *read* it — that computes its distances and never hears its testimony — has the data without the meaning. The sūrah's question — "Did We not build above you seven firmaments?" — is an invitation to restore the reading: to look up, not only as an astronomer but as a creature, and to let the unbroken roof above remind the heart of the Builder beneath whose hand it stands. The sky that science measures and the sky that faith reads are one sky; the verse claims that the measuring and the reading, rightly joined, lead to the same door.

**Above and Below: The Enclosure of the Signs**

Finally, the verse's position in the sūrah's structure deserves one more note, because it is the pivot of the whole middle section. The questions began *below* — earth, mountains — rose to the human creature *in the middle* — pairs, sleep, night, day — and now ascend *above* — the heavens, and (in the coming verses) the radiant lamp and the descending rain. The movement is a deliberate *vertical tour*: from the cradle beneath the feet to the roof above the head, with the human being stationed at the center, the intended beneficiary of both. And this vertical structure is itself the argument: a creature enclosed, above and below, by prepared signs, is a creature *intended* — made for, addressed, awaited. The denier who feels himself an accident in an accidental world is being shown, layer by layer, that he stands in the middle of a *built and aimed* cosmos, with a cradle under him and a roof over him and a purpose in front of him. The seven firmaments are not the sūrah's astronomy; they are its embrace — the upper arm of the argument that will close, in the sūrah's final movement, when the same heavens are opened into gates and the enclosure is dissolved into the presence of the Builder.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:13

> **and make a radiant lamp,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Blazing Light Hung in the Built Sky**

The eighth question moves from the structure of the heavens to the light set within them: *wa-jaʿalnā sirājan wahhājā* — "and [did We not] make a radiant lamp?" The word *sirāj* is the ordinary Arabic word for a lamp — the oil-lamp of the ancient household, the small vessel of fire that turns night into visibility — and the Qur'an's use of it here is a deliberate domestication of the sun: the great burning star that lights the world is described in the language of the lamp a family lights in its tent. The word *wahhāj* then intensifies it: it is from the root *w-h-j*, which means to blaze, to flame up, to burn with fierce heat, and *wahhāj* is the intensive pattern — *blazing, fiercely radiant, pouring out light and heat together*. So the sun is *a lamp that blazes* — a household light raised to the scale of a star, or a star brought down to the scale of a household light, so that the hearer might measure the one by the other and see, in both, the same act of provision.

The commentators are essentially unanimous that the radiant lamp is the sun, and they read the verse together with its parallels. The closest is Sūrah Nūḥ, where the same imagery is given in full:

> *"And made the moon therein a light, and made the sun a lamp?"* (Qur'an 71:16)

There the sun is *sirāj* — a lamp — and the moon *nūr* — a light — and the distinction is precise and famous: the sun is a *source* of light, self-blazing, while the moon is a *reflector*, a light without a flame of its own. The present verse, with its *wahhāj*, presses the same point with greater force: the sun is not merely luminous but *blazing* — a furnace of light and heat, and yet a furnace that is *for us*, hung in the built sky as a lamp is hung in a home. Some of the commentators, the source notes, read the radiant lamp as a reference to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself, on the strength of the Qur'an's description of him as a *luminous lamp*:

> *"O Prophet! Truly We have sent you as a witness, a bearer of glad tidings and a warner, and as one who calls to God by His leave, and as a luminous lamp (sirājan munīran)."* (Qur'an 33:45–46)

Both readings are honored in the tradition, and they are not finally opposed: the sun is the lamp of the *world*, and the Prophet ﷺ is the lamp of the *soul*; one lights the path of livelihood, the other the path of guidance. The sūrah's own argument rests on the first, but the second is a legitimate extension the text will bear, and the commentators keep both alive.

**The Lamp in the Built House: Completing the Dwelling**

The verse's position in the sūrah's structure is exact, and it completes a picture the earlier questions have been assembling. The sūrah has built a *house*: the earth is the floor (v. 6), the mountains its pegs (v. 7), the human pair its inhabitants (v. 8), sleep and night its rest and covering (vv. 9–10), the day its arena of work (v. 11), the seven firmaments its roof (v. 12) — and now the sun is the *lamp* hung from that roof (v. 13). The cascade of questions is, in effect, a tour of a dwelling prepared by God for the human family, and each verse adds the next necessary furnishing: floor, pegs, family, rest, roof, and now light. The lamp is the element that makes the whole house *usable*: without it, the roof would be a lid over darkness. The sūrah's word *sirāj* is thus not merely a metaphor for the sun but the completion of the house-metaphor — the blazing fixture by which the family goes about its life.

This dwelling-logic is also the argument-logic. A house with a floor, pegs, a roof, and a blazing lamp is not an accident; it is a *home*, and a home implies a householder. The denier who looks at the sun and sees only a burning ball of gas has, the sūrah suggests, looked at the lamp and missed the house: the sun is *sized* to the house, tuned to the needs of its inhabitants — its heat to warm, its light to see by, its distance measured to the fragility of life. The lamp is the most personal of the signs so far, because light is the most personal of provisions: it is the thing a parent provides first for a child's room, the thing that turns fear into safety and blindness into sight. "We made a radiant lamp" is the voice of the householder showing the family the light He hung for them — and asking, in the question's form, whether they can really believe the lamp lit itself.

**The Sun as a Sign: The Physics and the Meaning**

The classical commentators were not physicists, but their reading of the sun as a *sign* is deepened, not contradicted, by what later ages learned about it. The sun is a *wahhāj* in a sense the seventh century could not measure: a sustained thermonuclear furnace, converting mass into light and heat at a rate the mind cannot grasp, pouring energy into space in every direction — and the earth intercepts precisely the thin, exactly-right sliver of that outpouring that life requires. The distance, the intensity, the spectrum, the stability over billions of years — every one of these is a thread in the provision the sūrah compresses into two words. The Qur'an's method is vindicated by the very discoveries that might seem to displace it: it did not describe the sun's physics, because the physics is not the point; it described the sun's *purpose* — the fact that the blazing furnace is a *lamp*, aimed at the household, and that the aiming is the work of the One who built the house. Modern astronomy has made the sun more astonishing, and therefore made the verse's question more pointed: a light this vast, this stable, this precisely tuned to the life beneath it, and we are to believe it is here by chance?

There is also a precise theological point in the word *sirāj* that the commentators preserve. A lamp is a *dependent* thing: it burns fuel, it can be extinguished, it is lit by someone. By calling the sun a lamp, the Qur'an denies it any divinity — the sun is not a god, as the pagans of Arabia and the ancient world had held, but a *created instrument*, a burning vessel in the hand of its Maker. The sūrah's audience included those who worshipped or venerated the sun, and the word *sirāj* is a quiet demolition of that worship: the thing you bow to is a lamp, and a lamp is a tool. The same move is made elsewhere in the Qur'an, in the story of Abraham, who watched the stars, the moon, and the sun and said of each, "This is my Lord" — until each set, and he turned from the setting things to the One who does not set (cf. 6:76–79). The radiant lamp is a *sign*, not an object of worship; its blazing is borrowed, and the Borrower alone is God.

**Light as the Language of Guidance**

The Qur'an's use of the lamp-image does not stop at the physical sun, and the sūrah's verse opens onto the richest symbolism in the whole of the Qur'an: light as the language of *guidance*. The Qur'an calls itself a light — *"And thus We have revealed to you a Spirit of Our command... and We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants"* (42:52) — and it calls God Himself the light of the heavens and the earth (24:35), in the celebrated Light Verse, which the commentators read as the fullest unfolding of the sun's meaning: the physical light is a pointer to the uncreated Light that guides hearts. The Prophet ﷺ, as noted, is a *luminous lamp*; the believer's own face is said to run with light on the Day of Judgment (cf. 66:8); and the Qur'an describes the believer's movement from darkness to light as the whole meaning of God's sending the Messenger (cf. 2:257; 5:16; 14:1). The radiant lamp of verse 13 is thus, in the sūrah's economy, not only the sun but the *whole order of illumination* — the blazing star that lights the day, and the blazing Book and Messenger that light the soul — and the question "Did We not make a radiant lamp?" is asked on both levels at once: did We not light your world, and did We not light your path? The denier who rejects the guidance has, the sūrah implies, looked at the lamp of the sky and refused the lamp of the soul — accepted the light that shows him his livelihood and rejected the light that shows him his Lord.

**Light, Darkness, and the Coming Day**

Finally, the verse plants another seed for the sūrah's ending, and it is the most dramatic reversal of all. The sun is introduced here as the *wahhāj* — the blazing lamp of the world, the light by which everything is seen. But the sūrah's final movement describes a Day on which this lamp will be *extinguished* and replaced by a different light — the light of judgment, in which every deed is made visible. The Qur'an's apocalyptic passages describe the sun being folded up (81:1), darkened, and the world lit instead by the presence of its Lord and the record of every soul. The lamp that lit the day of livelihood will give way to the light that lights the day of reckoning; the *sirāj* of this world is a temporary fixture, and the sūrah's whole argument is that the household should live by its light *now* in a way that prepares for the light *then*. The believer who reads verse 13 in the light of verse 40 — the man beholding what his hands sent forth — understands that the sun is a mercy with a term, and that the truest use of its light is to see, and do, and send forward what the uncreated Light will one day reveal.

The verse's practical call is therefore simple and daily: look at the sun, and let the looking do its work. The same question the sūrah asks of the earth and the mountains and the night it asks of the light itself — "Did We not make a radiant lamp?" — and every dawn re-asks it, and every sunset answers it, and every human being who has ever worked, planted, traveled, or simply seen by its light has, whether he noticed or not, been living inside the answer. The lamp is lit; the house is built; the household is provisioned; and the only question the sūrah leaves open is the one it has been asking from the first verse — whether the household will read the house, thank the householder, and prepare for the Day the householder has announced.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:14

> **and send down water pouring from the rain clouds,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Descent That Completes the Provision**

The ninth question completes the chain that runs from sky to earth, and it is the verse from which the sūrah takes one of its names: *wa-anzalnā mina al-muʿṣirāti māʾan thajjājā* — "and [did We not] send down, from the rain-clouds, water pouring?" The verb *anzalnā* — "We sent down" — is the Qur'an's word for revelation as well as for rain: the same verb describes the descent of the Book and the descent of the water, and the commentators did not miss the kinship — both are *sent down* from above as mercy, one to revive the earth and the other to revive the hearts. The water is not said to "fall" or "happen"; it is *sent down* — a deliberate descent from a Sender, the same grammar of providence the sūrah has used for the earth, the mountains, the sleep, and the lamp. And the two adjectives bracket the sentence with meaning: the source is *al-muʿṣirāt*, and the water is *thajjāj* — and both words carry a depth the commentary must unfold.

The word *thajjāj* first. It is from the root *th-j-j*, which means to pour *abundantly and continuously* — the gushing, streaming, unceasing flow of water in full flood. *Thajjāj* is thus not a drizzle but a *pouring* — water that comes in abundance, wave upon wave, the rain that fills the rivers and soaks the soil. The commentators pair this verse with the Qur'an's other descriptions of rain as a *measure*: the water is sent down *in measure* (cf. 23:18; 43:11) — *bi-qadar* — so that the pouring is not a flood that destroys but a provision that nourishes. The abundance and the measure are one mercy: enough, and not too much; pouring, and yet governed. The word *thajjāj* praises the generosity; the word *qadar* elsewhere praises the wisdom; and the rain, in the Qur'an's vision, is the meeting point of both.

**The Muʿṣirāt: Rain-Clouds, Winds, or Heavens?**

The word *muʿṣirāt* has generated one of the classical commentaries' most instructive discussions, because its root allows several true readings. The root is *ʿ-ṣ-r*, which means to *squeeze* — to press out, as one presses grapes for wine or wrings water from a cloth. A *muʿṣir* is a thing that *presses out*; the plural *muʿṣirāt* is *the pressers*. The majority reading, adopted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī in their main transmission, takes *muʿṣirāt* as *the clouds* — the rain-clouds that are *pressed* until they release their water, the sky's vessels wrung out over the earth. The image is exact and physical: the cloud is a held fullness, and the wind or the divine command *presses* it, and the water pours. A second, well-attested reading takes *muʿṣirāt* as *the winds* — the winds that *press* the clouds, squeezing the water from them and driving them to the land that needs them. Al-Ṭabarī records this reading, and it too is grounded in the root: the winds are the pressers of the clouds. A third, minority reading takes it as *the heavens* — the skies that are pressed to yield their rain. The three readings are not rivals but *layers of one process*: the heavens hold, the winds press, the clouds yield, and the water pours — the root *ʿ-ṣ-r* (to squeeze) describing, at whichever point one fixes the image, the single act of pressure that turns held water into falling rain.

This root-level image is the verse's hidden argument. Rain does not merely happen; it is *squeezed out* — a stored fullness released by a directed pressure. The Qur'an's word invites the hearer to see the whole apparatus: the sea that yields its vapor, the wind that carries and presses, the cloud that is wrung, the water that descends — a complete system, all of it *sent down* (*anzalnā*) by the One who built the sky and lit the lamp. The denier who watches rain and sees only weather has, the sūrah implies, watched a *providence* and seen a *process* — has described the squeezing and missed the Squeezer. The verse's question, "Did We not send down...?", is the correction: the rain is not an event but a *sending*, and every drop is a signature.

**The Water Cycle as a Sign of the Resurrection**

The commentators' deepest use of this verse — and the one that ties it directly to the sūrah's central argument — is the parallel between rain and resurrection. The Qur'an states the parallel explicitly in many places, and it is one of its favorite arguments: the dead earth, revived by water, is the standing proof that the dead will be revived by their Lord. The most concentrated statement is in Sūrah Qāf, immediately after the passage on the heavens already quoted:

> *"And We send down from the sky blessed water, and cause to grow thereby gardens and the grain of the harvest... as provision for the servants; and We give life thereby to a dead land. Thus is the coming forth."* (Qur'an 50:9–11)

"Thus is the coming forth" — *kadhālika al-khurūj* — the word *khurūj*, "coming forth," being the Qur'an's word for the resurrection itself. The argument is complete and devastating in its simplicity: *you have watched the dead earth come to life a thousand times; you call it spring; the same power that does this every year will do it once more, finally.* The disbeliever who says the dead cannot be revived stands, the Qur'an points out, on ground that was itself dead and is now alive — on soil that was dust and is now a field — and the rain that did it fell from the same sky he is now using to deny its Maker's power. The sūrah's placement of the rain-verse immediately before the grain-verse (v. 15) is the deliberate staging of this argument: water first, growth second, resurrection as the conclusion the whole sequence implies.

The Prophet ﷺ and the Companions lived inside this symbolism. The prayer for rain (*istisqāʾ*) treated the sky's water as a sign of the Lord's mercy; the early Muslims would go out to pray for it, and the Prophet ﷺ would turn his cloak and raise his hands, teaching his community that the water's descent was a *descent from God* to be *requested* — not a mechanism to be merely observed. The rain was, in their practice, a liturgical event: its coming was a mercy to be thanked, its withholding a trial to be borne, and its revival of the dead earth a sermon preached by the sky every growing season. The sūrah's verse is the Qur'anic text of that sermon.

**Water as the Basis of Life: The Physical and the Metaphysical**

There is a further layer the commentators preserve, and it deepens the verse's reach. The Qur'an says that God made every living thing from water:

> *"...and We made from water every living thing. Will they not then believe?"* (Qur'an 21:30)

The rain of this verse is thus not merely one provision among others but the *basis* of them all: the water that pours from the clouds is the substance of life itself, the common element of every creature the sūrah has mentioned — the human pair, the sleeping household, the grain and the gardens. The descent of water is, in the Qur'an's physics, the descent of the very material of life; and the One who sends down the material of life is the One who *began* life, and the One who began it can *begin it again*. The verse thus carries the sūrah's resurrection-argument at its deepest level: life itself — the thing the denier takes most for granted, the thing he is — is a *sending-down*, a gift that descends like rain, and the Giver of the gift is not bound by its term. As the Qur'an says of the final revival, using the same water-imagery, the earth on the Last Day will be *shaken and brought to life* by the rain of the resurrection (cf. 41:39; 22:5; 30:50).

And there is the metaphysical parallel the commentators loved: as water revives the dead earth, so revelation revives the dead heart. The same verb *anzalnā* governs both, and the tradition read the two descents as one mercy in two forms. The Qur'an calls itself *water* in its own imagery — the rain of guidance sent down from the sky of revelation, reviving hearts the way the rain revives soil. The sūrah's verse, read at this level, is asking a double question: "Did We not send down water for your bodies? Did We not send down guidance for your souls?" — and the denier who rejects the second descent while accepting the first is, in the Qur'an's accounting, a man who drinks the rain and denies the Sender.

**The Modern Reading: From the Cloud to the Conclusion**

For the modern reader, the verse's argument is, if anything, stronger than it was for its first hearers, because the modern reader knows — as the seventh century did not — the full intricacy of what the Qur'an compresses into *anzalnā mina al-muʿṣirāti māʾan thajjājā*. The water cycle is now a catalogued wonder: evaporation from the seas, the transport of vapor on the winds, condensation in the clouds, the precipitation of rain, the drainage into rivers and aquifers, and the return to the sea — a closed, self-renewing, precisely balanced system on which every terrestrial life depends. Each stage of the system is a thread in the "squeezing" the root *ʿ-ṣ-r* names. The modern reader can trace the thread and marvel at the mechanism; the sūrah's question is whether he will also read the *sending*. A system this exact, this reliable, this essential, and this *generous* — pouring abundance without measure's failing — is, the verse insists, a sign, and the sign's meaning is the same in every era: the water is sent, the Sender is real, and the Sender has announced a Day.

The practical teaching closes the circle the verse began. The rain is a mercy to be *received* — and the reception is gratitude. The believer's response to rain is not indifference but thanks, and the thanks takes form in the use the water is put to: cleanliness, life, growth, and the remembrance that the same water that feeds the body points the soul to its Source. The verse, placed last among the sky-signs and immediately before the earth's yield, is the hinge of the sūrah's whole middle section: it is the point where the built sky and the blazing lamp *give* — where the architecture above descends into the soil below and becomes food. And in that hinge is the sūrah's deepest word about its Maker: the One who built the heavens does not remain aloof above them; He *sends down*. The God of the roof is the God of the rain; the Builder is the Provider; and the descent of the water is the standing proof that the next descent — the final sending-down of the Day of Division — is not only possible but promised.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:15

> **that We might produce grain and plants thereby,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purpose of the Pouring**

The tenth question carries the rain to its intended end: *li-nukhrija bihi ḥabban wa-nabātā* — "that We might produce thereby grain and plants." The particle *li-* at the head is the particle of *purpose* — "in order that, so that" — and it is the verse's whole theological weight. The rain was not sent down to fall and be done; it was sent down *in order that* it might produce. The water of verse 14 has a *destination* in verse 15: it enters the soil, and the soil answers, and what comes out is *ḥabb* (grain, the seed-bearing crops — wheat, barley, and every seed that feeds humanity) and *nabāt* (plants, vegetation — every green thing that grows, whether for food, fodder, or beauty). The verse is the sūrah's statement of *final causality*: the whole apparatus of the previous verses — the built sky, the blazing lamp, the pressed clouds, the pouring water — exists *in order to* produce this. The purpose is named, and the naming of purpose is the naming of the Purposer.

The commentators were at pains to clarify one point that the verse's grammar raises, because it is the hinge of the whole argument: *who* produces the grain? The Arabic says "that *We* might produce" — *li-nukhrija* — and the "We" is God. The rain does not produce the grain; the soil does not produce the grain; the seed does not produce the grain. *God* produces it, *by means of* the rain — the *bi-* in *bihi* is the preposition of *instrument*. The source commentary preserves exactly this point: God remains the primary cause in all of it, and it is a standing error of heedlessness to credit the *means* with the *result*. The farmer who says "the rain made the crop grow" has, in the Qur'an's accounting, mistaken the instrument for the agent — the same error as crediting the pen for the poem or the lamp for the light. The rain is the *instrument*; the growth is the *act*; and the Act is God's. The verse's "We" is the correction of every such confusion, planted at the center of the sūrah's argument: the signs are not self-running; they are *produced*, continuously, by the One who is being described.

**The Root of Extraction: What Growth Actually Is**

The verb *nukhrija* — "We bring out, We extract" — is from the root *kh-r-j*, "to go out, to come forth," and it is the Qur'an's chosen word for *both* the sprouting of plants *and* the resurrection of the dead. This is not a coincidence; it is the deliberate joining of the two ideas through a single root. The Qur'an says of the grain: *"We cause the grain to come forth"* — and of the dead: *"He will bring you forth (yukhrijukum) from the earth"* (cf. 71:17–18; 20:55). The same word, *khurūj*, is used in the celebrated verse already quoted — *"Thus is the coming forth (al-khurūj)"* (50:11) — where the reference is at once to the sprouting of the seed and the rising of the dead. The root's double duty is the Qur'an's argument in miniature: the sprouting of a seed from dark soil is the *same kind of event* as the rising of a body from the grave — a coming-forth, an extraction, a bringing-out of life from the place of lifelessness — and the One who performs the first, visibly, every spring, is the One who will perform the second, finally, on the Day. The sūrah's choice of *nukhrija* for the grain is thus already looking forward to verse 40 and the resurrection; the seed and the soul are both *brought out* by the same Hand.

This is why the verse, quiet as it is, is one of the sūrah's most important. It is the point where the *signs of creation* become the *argument for resurrection*: the grain that comes forth from the earth is the resurrection made ordinary. A person who has seen a field sprout has seen the principle of the Resurrection at work — life extracted from the ground by water and power — and has only to *generalize* what he has seen: if God can call grain from soil every year, He can call bodies from graves once. The disbeliever's question in verse 1 — about the Resurrection — is answered, here, not by a syllogism but by a *seed*: the thing you deny is the thing you eat every day, because every loaf is a resurrection, every harvest is a raising of the dead earth, and the eater of the bread is the witness of the truth he denies.

**Grain and Plants: The Provision Named**

It is worth pausing on what the verse actually names, because the naming is a catalog of mercy. *Ḥabb* — grain — is the staple of human life: wheat, barley, rice, the seed-crops that have fed every civilization; the commentators note that *ḥabb* is mentioned *first* because it is the *food of humanity*, the staff of life that bread is made from. *Nabāt* — plants, vegetation — is the wider world of green: the pastures that feed the animals, the herbs that heal, the trees that shade and bear, the whole kingdom of growth that sustains every other creature. The two words together cover the *food-chain of the world*: grain for the human being, vegetation for the animal, and through them the entire living order provisioned. The verse is the sūrah's summary of *rizq* — the Qur'anic word for provision — and its message is that provision is not a given but a *gift*: the bread in your hand is the end of a chain that began in the built sky, passed through the blazing lamp, was squeezed from the clouds, poured into the soil, and extracted as grain — and every link in the chain was forged by the One who is now asking, "Did We not...?"

The commentators add a further note that deepens the point: the verse's grammar attributes the production *directly* to God — "We produce" — and this attribution is the Qur'an's standing correction of human self-sufficiency. The farmer who plows and sows is a *partner in the means*, never the *author of the growth*; he prepares, but God produces. The Qur'an states this with an image that the sūrah's verse implies:

> *"Have you considered what you sow? Is it you who cause it to grow, or are We the Growers?"* (Qur'an 56:63–64)

The question is unanswerable except by confession: the farmer can sow, water, and tend, but he cannot make the seed *live* — the life in the seed is not his to command. The verse thus humbles the human agent at the very moment it feeds him: every meal is a dependent act, every harvest a receipt of what no human hand can produce. And this humility is the sūrah's strategic goal, because the denier of the Resurrection is, at bottom, a person who has forgotten his dependence — who has credited himself or the means with what only God produces, and who must be led back, through the grain in his own fields, to the Producer of both the grain and the Day.

**The Chain Completed: From Sky to Soil to Table**

The verse's position in the sūrah deserves a final structural note, because it closes the sūrah's great vertical circuit. The middle section began at the earth (v. 6) and ascended through the human creature (v. 8) to the heavens (v. 12), the sun (v. 13), and the rain (v. 14); now, in verses 15–16, it descends again to the earth's produce — the grain and the gardens. The movement is a *complete cycle*: earth → sky → earth, the full round of the world's economy, in which the sky's gifts return to the soil and become the soil's bounty. The sūrah has, in ten verses, traced the entire circuit of human sustenance — from the cradle beneath the feet to the roof above the head to the rain between them to the grain that results — and in doing so it has left no corner of the denier's lived world untouched by a sign. The grain of verse 15 is the *harvest* of the whole passage: the point where all the previous verses converge, and the proof, laid on every table, that the world is a provision arranged by a Provider.

And the Provider's provision points to the Provider's promise. The same word that brings the grain from the soil will bring the bodies from the graves; the same purpose that aims the rain at the field aims the world at the Day. The verse's *li-* — "in order that" — is thus the sūrah's quietest and most powerful particle: it declares that nothing in the created order is pointless, that the rain has a reason and the grain has a reason and the whole world has a reason, and that the reason is *you* — the creature provisioned, and the creature accountable. The grain is for your body, the sign is for your soul, and the Day is for your accounting. This is the verse's full message: the harvest is a mercy, the mercy is a sign, the sign is an argument, and the argument ends in the resurrection the sūrah is announcing.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:16

> **and luxuriant gardens?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Sign, and the Word That Names Paradise**

The eleventh and final question of the cascade is the shortest of them all, and it gathers everything the middle section has built: *wa-jannātin alfāfā* — "and gardens, luxuriant?" The word *jannāt* is the plural of *jannah* — a garden — and it is the very word the Qur'an uses, throughout, for *Paradise*. The connection is not accidental; it is the sūrah's final structural gift. The same word that here describes the gardens the rain produces — the orchards and groves of the earth — is the word that, twenty verses later, will describe the reward of the reverent (78:31–32): *"Truly the reverent shall have a place of triumph — gardens and vineyards."* The earthly garden is the *sign* of the heavenly one; the orchard the rain calls from the soil is the *foretaste* of the Garden the Lord calls from the grave. The sūrah has been building to this identification from its first verse: the world's provision, seen aright, is a *preview* of the Hereafter's reward.

The word *alfāf* is the verse's precise and beautiful choice, and it deserves its full unfolding. It is the plural of *laff* or *lifāf*, from the root *l-f-f*, which means to *wrap, fold, intertwine* — the same root from which comes *lifāfah*, a wrapping, an envelope. An *alfāf* garden is a garden whose trees are *intertwined* — branches folded into branches, foliage wrapped in foliage, growth so dense and interwoven that one tree's shade runs into the next and no gap shows the soil between. The word pictures *abundance beyond measure*: not a sparse planting but a *luxuriance* — the crowded, tangled, overflowing fullness of vegetation in its richest state. The commentators gloss it as *mul taffah* — "intertwined, entwined" — and the image it conveys is of a garden so thick with life that it has become one continuous body of green. This is the rain's final result: not mere survival but *overflow* — the pouring water of verse 14 ending in the entwined abundance of verse 16.

**From Grain to Garden: Necessity to Abundance**

There is a deliberate progression from verse 15 to verse 16 that the commentators mark. Verse 15 gave *grain* — the staff of life, the bare necessity, the food that sustains. Verse 16 gives *gardens* — the abundance beyond necessity, the beauty, the shade, the fruit, the pleasure. The two verses together are the Qur'an's statement that God's provision is *both* sufficient *and* generous: He provides the bread that keeps life, and then He provides the orchard that makes life sweet. The human being is not fed at a bare minimum but *lavished* upon — given not only the grain that answers hunger but the garden that answers joy. The sūrah's cascade of questions thus ends on a note of *overflow*: the Provision ends in abundance, the mercy ends in beauty, and the world, seen through these eleven questions, is not a ration but a *feast*.

This progression from necessity to abundance is itself the pattern of the Hereafter the sūrah is about to describe. The disbelievers' punishment is pictured, in the coming verses, as a *minimum of torment* — extremes of heat and cold, and nothing else (vv. 24–25). The believers' reward is pictured as an *abundance of delight* — gardens, companions, overflowing cups, and peace (vv. 31–36). The same gap between the grain and the garden is the gap between Hell and Paradise: the one is a bare subsistence of punishment, the other an overflowing of reward. The earthly garden of verse 16 is thus the sūrah's *bridge*: it teaches the eye to recognize abundance when it sees it, so that when the sūrah turns to the heavenly garden, the recognition is already prepared. The luxuriant garden is the lexicon in which the sūrah will describe Paradise; the hearer who has learned to read the one is ready to read the other.

**The Garden as the Sign of the Resurrection, Completed**

The garden also completes the sūrah's resurrection-argument, and it does so with the argument's most powerful single image: the *dead ground made alive*. A garden in full leaf is, in the strictest sense, the *reversal of death* — soil that was bare and lifeless, now wrapped in intertwined life, because water was sent down upon it. The Qur'an returns to this image again and again as its proof of the resurrection, and it always uses the garden's own vocabulary:

> *"And you see the earth barren, but when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells and puts forth every kind of lovely pair."* (Qur'an 22:5)

The same verse continues, in its full form, to the very argument the sūrah is making:

> *"...That is because God is the Truth, and because He gives life to the dead, and because He is powerful over all things, and because the Hour is coming, about which there is no doubt, and because God will raise those who are in the graves."* (Qur'an 22:5–7)

The luxuriant garden is, in the Qur'an's hands, the *visible form of the promise*: it is the resurrection performed annually, the dead earth raised, the lifeless soil wrapped in life — and the denier who walks through an orchard and denies that the dead can be raised is, in the Qur'an's accounting, a man who has walked through the proof and refused to read it. The sūrah's final question — "and gardens, luxuriant?" — is therefore the *capstone* of its whole argument: it is the point where the cascade of questions has delivered the hearer to the very threshold of the Hereafter, with the earthly garden as the last step before the heavenly one.

**The Root of Wrapping: The Earth's Mantle and the Sūrah's Meaning**

The root *l-f-f* — to wrap — deserves one more reflection, because it ties the sūrah's ending to its beginning in a way the commentators' love of roots makes visible. The night, in verse 10, was a *garment* — a wrapping drawn over the world. The garden, in verse 16, is a *wrapping* of growth drawn over the soil. Both are *coverings*: the night wraps the world in rest, and the garden wraps the earth in life. The sūrah's world is a world of *divine wrappings* — the covering of darkness, the covering of foliage — and each wrapping is a mercy from the One who wraps. And the sūrah's final movement will add the last and greatest wrapping: the Day on which the sky is *unwrapped* (cf. 81:11, "when the sky is stripped away") and the earth *brings out* what it has wrapped — the bodies it has held in the grave (cf. 99:2). The wrapping and the unwrapping are one act of God: He wraps the world in night and green, and He will unwrap the graves and the sky, and the whole sūrah is the announcement that the unwrapping is as certain as the wrapping the hearer has watched all his life.

**The Modern Orchard and the Ancient Promise**

For the modern reader, the verse's invitation is the same as for the first hearer, and it needs no translation: *look at a garden.* The luxuriance of verse 16 is still being performed, every growing season, everywhere on earth — the intertwined abundance that the root *l-f-f* names is as available to a person in a modern city park as to a Bedouin in the seventh-century Hijaz. The sūrah's question — "and gardens, luxuriant?" — is a question that every orchard, every garden, every window-box and every field is asking on God's behalf, and it is a question the modern world, for all its knowledge of botany and hydrology, has not outgrown: the garden is still the visible form of the impossible thing — life from lifelessness — and the impossibility is still performed every spring. The verse's last word, *alfāf*, is thus the sūrah's *benediction* on its own argument: the world ends its tour of signs not with a bare fact but with a *fullness* — a tangled, overflowing, intertwined abundance — and it hands that fullness to the hearer as the final evidence that the One who wraps the earth in gardens will wrap His servants in the Garden, and that the coming Day is the harvest of all the days.
