With this verse the middle section of the sūrah closes, and the tone changes again. The questions are finished; the signs have been laid out; the hearer has been walked through the whole prepared house of the world, from the cradle-earth to the entwined garden. And now the sūrah turns, in its final movement, from the signs *of* the Maker to the Day *of* the Maker — from the world that points to the Hereafter, to the Hereafter itself.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:17

> **Truly the Day of Division is a Moment Appointed,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn from the Signs to the Day**

With verse 17 the sūrah changes register a second time. The opening verses were rebuke; the middle verses were the cascade of questions and signs. Now the tone shifts to *declaration* — no longer "Did We not...?" but "*Truly...*" The verse opens with *inna*, the particle of emphasis and confirmation, the Arabic "verily, indeed, truly," and with it the sūrah announces its subject without qualification: *inna yawma al-faṣli kāna mīqātā* — "Truly the Day of Division is a Moment Appointed." The *inna* answers the whole of what has come before: the questioning (v. 1), the differing (v. 3), the doubled warning (vv. 4–5), and the eleven signs (vv. 6–16) all converge on this single, emphatic statement. The signs were the argument; the Day is the conclusion. And the conclusion is stated not as a hope or a threat but as a *fact* — *kāna mīqātā*, "it *is* a moment appointed," with the verb *kāna* (the verb of being, here in its perfect form) lending the statement the weight of something already fixed and settled, as though the Day's appointment were an accomplished matter merely awaiting its arrival.

The name *yawm al-faṣl* — "the Day of Division" — is the verse's first great word, and it must be unfolded carefully. The root is *f-ṣ-l*, which means to *separate, sever, divide* — to cut one thing from another with precision. From it comes *faṣl* (a division, a decisive judgment), *fiṣāl* (the weaning of a child, the separation of the nursling from the breast), *mafṣil* (a joint, the place where the body is articulated), and *faṣīl* (a young camel already weaned). The Day is called the Day of *Faṣl* because it is the day of *decisive separation*: the day when truth is severed from falsehood, the believer from the denier, the right from the wrong, and the destiny of each soul from every ambiguity. The Qur'an uses the name repeatedly for the Resurrection, and always with this sense of final, surgical division:

> *"This is the Day of Division (yawm al-faṣl); We have gathered you and the ancients."* (Qur'an 77:38)

The sūrah's own title-sūrah, al-Mursalāt, had just used the name in its closing verses, and al-Nabaʾ takes it up and makes it the hinge of its final movement. The commentators note that the Day has many names in the Qur'an — the Hour, the Day of Judgment, the Day of Rising, the Overwhelming, the Striking, the Deafening, the Day of Mutual Disillusionment — and that each name fixes on one aspect of the event. *Yawm al-Faṣl* fixes on the *division*: the Day is not primarily a display of power or a passage of time but a *sorting*, a *separation*, the final and irreversible sorting of the human race into its two destinations. The world of the present, in which truth and falsehood are mixed, the believer and the denier indistinguishable in the crowd, and every claim contestable, is a world of *intermixture*; the Day of Division is the end of the mixture.

**A Moment Appointed: The Word Mīqāt and the Certainty of the Term**

The verse's second great word is *mīqāt* — "a moment appointed" — and it is the sūrah's answer to every doubt about the Day's *certainty*. *Mīqāt* is built on the root *w-q-t*, "time," and it means a *set time, a fixed term, an appointed rendezvous* — the specific hour fixed for a meeting, the deadline fixed for a payment, the time fixed for an event that *will* occur. The word is used in the Qur'an for the appointed times of pilgrimage (2:189), for the tryst God fixed with Moses at the mountain (7:142–143; 20:40), and here for the Day of Division: the Day is a *mīqāt* — a rendezvous that has been *set*, a term that has been *fixed*, a moment that is *scheduled* and therefore certain. The commentators draw the lesson from the very grammar: the Day is not "perhaps coming" or "likely to occur"; it is *appointed*. An appointment does not argue; it simply *is*, and the one who has made the appointment keeps it. The sūrah's word thus carries the same assurance as the earlier *sa-yaʿlamūn* — "they will know" — and the same grammar of the settled future: the Day is a fact of the calendar of eternity, fixed before the world began, and no human disbelief can move it by an hour.

The certainty of the *mīqāt* is a direct answer to the disbelievers' oldest objection, which the Qur'an records in their own words: *"When will this promise be, if you are truthful?"* (10:48; 21:38; 27:71; 34:29; 36:48; 67:25). The question "when?" is, in their mouths, a device of evasion — as though the Day's *postponement* were evidence of its *falsehood*. The sūrah's answer is the word *mīqāt*: the Day is *appointed* — its time is fixed, and the fixing of the time is not the denial of the event but the very proof of it, for only a real event can have a real time. The God who fixed the *mīqāt* of Moses' meeting at the mountain is the God who fixed the *mīqāt* of the resurrection; the appointment is kept in both cases, and the only difference is the scale. The disbeliever who mocks the Day's delay is, the sūrah implies, like a man who denies that dawn is coming because the night is still dark: the darkness is not the absence of the dawn but the very condition of its arrival.

**The Logic of Division: Why the Day Must Separate**

The verse's two words together — *Day of Division* and *Moment Appointed* — contain a complete logical argument that the commentators unfold. The world, as it now stands, is a place of *mixture*: the truthful and the false are interwoven, the oppressor and the oppressed share the same city, the good deed and the evil deed are both done under the same sun, and no final verdict is rendered. If the world were all there is, this mixture would be the last word — and justice, which the human heart knows to be owed, would be a permanent casualty. The Day of Division is the *resolution* of the mixture: it is the point at which the woven threads are separated, each to its own destiny, and the accounts that the world left open are closed. The commentators put it simply: the name *yawm al-faṣl* is the Qur'an's guarantee that the moral confusion of the present is not final, that the distinction between truth and falsehood is not a human convention but a divine reality, and that the reality will be *enforced* — visibly, publicly, and finally — on the appointed Day. The logic is the logic of justice itself: a world made by a just Maker cannot end in mixture; the division is the justice, and the appointment is the certainty that the justice will arrive.

And the *mīqāt* completes the logic by answering the question of *timing*. Why is the Day not now? Why the interval of mixture, the long history of wrongs unrighted and truths contested? The commentators' answer is rooted in the Qur'an's own: the interval is the *term of testing* — the span in which souls choose freely, deeds are sent forward, and the mixture is allowed to persist precisely so that the division, when it comes, can be a *verdict* rather than a *force*. The Day is appointed, not immediate, because appointment is the condition of *meaningful choice*: the tryst is set, the term is known to God, and within the term every soul writes the record that the Day will read. The delay is not indifference; it is *opportunity*. The sūrah's word *mīqāt* is thus, like the doubled *kallā* earlier, a mercy in the form of a fact: the Day is coming, and it is not here yet, and the space between the coming and the arriving is the space in which repentance is still possible.

**The Tryst That Cannot Fail**

The commentators connect the *mīqāt* here to the Qur'an's own promise that God does not fail the tryst, and the connection is the verse's deepest reassurance. The Qur'an says, in the voice of warning and promise both:

> *"Our Lord, You are the Gatherer of mankind to a Day about which there is no doubt. Truly God does not fail the tryst (al-mīʿād)."* (Qur'an 3:9)

The *mīʿād* — the tryst, the promise, the appointment — is the same root-family as *mīqāt*, and the two words together are the Qur'an's theology of certainty: the Day is a *mīqāt* (a set time), and the setting of it is a *mīʿād* (a promise), and God *does not fail the promise*. The disbeliever's error, at bottom, is to suppose that God's patience is God's forgetfulness — that because the Day has not come, it will not come. The sūrah's verse corrects the supposition with a single word: the Day is *appointed*. What is appointed is not forgotten; what is promised is not abandoned; and the One who fixed the time is the One who keeps it. This is the verse's pastoral power for the believer as well as its warning for the denier: the believer who suffers the world's mixture — who sees truth mocked and falsehood enthroned, who bears wrongs unrighted in this life — is given the *mīqāt* as his consolation. The mixture is temporary; the division is appointed; and the tryst will be kept. The sūrah, having spent eleven verses on the signs, now hands its hearer the *certainty* the signs were building toward, and turns to describe, in the verses that follow, exactly what the appointed Day will bring.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:18

> **a day when the trumpet is blown and you come forth in throngs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Horn That Ends the World and Begins the Judgment**

The verse opens the description of the appointed Day with its first event: *yawma yunfakhu fī al-ṣūri fa-taʾtūna afwājā* — "a day when the trumpet is blown, and you come forth in throngs." The word *ṣūr* is the horn, the trumpet, and the blowing of it is the Qur'an's chosen image for the *summons* that ends the world and begins the judgment — the single sound that no creature can refuse, the call that raises the dead from their graves and gathers them before their Lord. The verb is in the passive voice: *yunfakhu fī al-ṣūr* — "the trumpet *is blown*" — and the passivity is itself meaningful: the verse does not yet name the blower, because the emphasis falls wholly on the *sound* and its *effect*. The trumpet is not an ornament of the scene but the *mechanism of the gathering*: at its blast, everything the disbelievers called impossible — the return of the dead, the assembly of all humanity, the end of the world's mixture — is performed in an instant. The sūrah's hearer, who has just been walked through the signs of the world's order, is now shown the one sound that will dissolve that order and begin the next.

The classical tradition fills in the picture of the trumpet with details the sūrah's brevity implies, and the commentators draw them from the Qur'an's fuller statements elsewhere. The trumpet is blown by an angel — the tradition names him Isrāfīl (Seraphiel), one of the great angels entrusted with this single task — and it is blown *twice*, or in two stages. The first blast is the blast of *terror and death*: the Qur'an says of it that *"whosoever is in the heavens and the earth will swoon, save whom God wills"* (39:68), and that the mountains will be crumbled and the cosmos convulsed. The second blast is the blast of *rising*: at it, the dead come forth from their graves and are gathered. The Qur'an states the sequence in full in Sūrah al-Zumar:

> *"And the trumpet will be blown, and all who are in the heavens and the earth will swoon, save whom God wills. Then it will be blown again, and behold, they will be standing, looking on."* (Qur'an 39:68)

The sūrah's verse may refer to both blasts together, or to the second blast in particular — the one at which "you come forth in throngs" — and the commentators discuss both possibilities, since the Arabic of this verse is brief enough to carry either. What is not in doubt is the verse's central image: a *blown horn* as the instrument of the resurrection, the sound that calls the dead as a shepherd's horn calls a scattered flock, and the dead answer by rising.

**The Trumpet and the Logic of the Summons**

Why a trumpet? The image deserves reflection, because it is chosen with the same precision as every image in this sūrah. A trumpet is a *summons* — in the ancient world, the horn was the instrument of the call: the call to war, the call to assembly, the call of a king to his subjects, the call of a shepherd to his flock. The sound of the horn is the sound of *authority being exercised* — one blast, and an army forms, a city gathers, a scattered people assemble. By describing the resurrection as the blowing of a trumpet, the Qur'an is saying that the end of the world is a *summons*: the dead are not being rebuilt like machines but *called* like subjects, and the call is from the King. The image also carries the logic of *obedience*: no creature can ignore a summons from its Maker; the blast is the command, and the rising is the compliance. The disbeliever who asks "how can the dead be raised?" is answered by the image itself: the dead are raised the way subjects answer a summons — by the authority of the One who calls. The question "how" dissolves into the reality of "by whose command."

The commentators add a further note on the *range* of the sound. The trumpet's blast reaches *everything* — the Qur'an says the first blast makes all who are in the heavens and the earth swoon (39:68), and the second raises all who ever lived. The horn is the Qur'an's image of *universality*: a sound that leaves nothing out. The gathering the sūrah describes is not local or partial but total — every human being from the first to the last, every community, every generation — and the trumpet is the image of that totality, because a sound does not select; it reaches. The "throngs" of the second half of the verse are the visible result: the dead, scattered across time and space, answered the call and came — not singly but in *crowds*.

**Throngs: The Resurrection of the Communities**

The word *afwāj* — "throngs" — is the verse's second great image, and it too carries a precise meaning. *Afwāj* is the plural of *fawj*, a *group, a company, a crowd* — the word for a mass of people moving together, as distinct from an individual or a mere pair. The resurrection, the sūrah says, will not be a trickle but a *flood*: "you come forth in throngs" — group after group, wave after wave, the nations of the dead streaming from the earth like water released. The Qur'an's fuller statements picture this thronging precisely: in Sūrah Qāf, the dead come forth *"as locusts spreading"* (54:7) and *"with sight restored, like scattered locusts"* (cf. 54:7; 101:4); in Sūrah Yā Sīn, the single blast brings them all forth *"and behold, they are all brought before Us"* (36:53). The throng is the Qur'an's image of *multitude*: the resurrection is not the return of a few but the assembly of *all* — every soul that ever lived, summoned at once, and the sheer mass of the crowd is itself a sign of the power that gathered it.

The commentators read *afwāj* with a further, more specific meaning that the tradition preserves: the throngs are the *communities* — each nation gathered as its own crowd, following its own prophet. The Qur'an says of this:

> *"A day when We shall call every people by their leader (imām)..."* (Qur'an 17:71)

And the tradition transmitted from the Prophet ﷺ — recorded in the collections of hadith in connection with this very word — pictures his own community rising in distinct groups. The source commentary preserves the report: when asked about the *afwāj*, the Prophet ﷺ said that on the Day of Resurrection his community would be resurrected in *ten throngs*, which he then enumerated, distinguishing the various states in which people would rise — some in the condition of their faith, others in the condition of their sins, each group marked by what its members had made of themselves in the world. The point of the enumeration is not the exact number but the *principle*: the resurrection preserves the moral distinctions the world obscured — people rise *in throngs*, grouped by what they were, each company a visible statement of the lives its members lived. The mixture of the world is already being *sorted* into its throngs; the Day of Division begins with the division of the crowds.

**The Psychology of the Summons: No Refusal, No Delay**

The verse's effect on its hearer is meant to be immediate, and the commentators preserve the emotional force of the image. The trumpet is the *end of postponement*. Every previous postponement — the disbeliever's "when will this promise be?" — is answered by the blast: *now*. The sound leaves no room for argument, negotiation, or delay; it is the event that no one schedules and no one declines. The sūrah's earlier warning — "they will know" — is here shown in its fulfillment: the knowing begins with the hearing of the horn. And the word *fa-taʾtūna* — "and *you* come forth" — with its sudden shift to the second person, is a deliberate address: the verse turns from describing the scene to *including the hearer in it*. It is not "they come forth" but "you come forth" — the reader, the listener, the questioner of verse 1, is told directly that he will be among the throngs. The sūrah's rhetoric has been building to this inclusion: the "they" of the opening becomes the "you" of the Day, because on that Day there is no third-person — everyone is a participant, and the crowd is made of the very people who are now reading the description.

This is the verse's pastoral function for the believer and its warning for the heedless alike. For the believer, the trumpet is not a terror but a *summons home*: the blast that ends the world ends also the world's sorrows, and the throng that rises includes the ones he loves and the Prophet ﷺ he followed, and the gathering is the first moment of the reunion the faith has promised. For the heedless, the trumpet is the end of every deferral: the life he was going to repent in, the prayer he was going to begin, the account he was going to set right — all of it is closed by the blast, and the "later" he counted on is revealed to have been borrowed time. The verse thus does in a single image what the sūrah has been doing throughout: it collapses the distance between the future and the present, so that the hearer lives *now* in the awareness of the sound that is coming. To hear of the trumpet and to feel, for a moment, that one is among the throngs it raises, is to have received the sūrah's message in its fullest form.

**From the Summons to the Sorting**

The verse also sets the stage for what follows, and its two halves — the blast, then the throngs — are the sūrah's transition from the *event* to the *consequences*. The trumpet gathers; the Day then divides. The throngs that come forth are not the end but the beginning of the judgment: once gathered, they are separated, as the next verses describe, into the two destinies — Hell lying in wait for the rebellious, and the gardens awaiting the reverent. The verse's *afwāj* is thus the sūrah's hinge between the resurrection and the recompense: the crowds are assembled, the sorting begins, and the Day of Division, announced in verse 17, proceeds to its verdict. The hearer who has followed the sūrah from the question of verse 1 now stands, in imagination, among the risen throngs of verse 18 — and the rest of the chapter will tell him what the throngs are divided into, and on what basis, and with what result. The trumpet has been blown; there is no going back; and the only remaining question is the one the sūrah has been asking from the first line: which side of the division will you stand on?

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:19

> **and the sky is opened as if it were gates,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Roof of the World Split into Doors**

The verse continues the description of the Day, and its image is the unmaking of the very sign the sūrah built in verse 12: *wa-futiḥati al-samāʾu fa-kānat abwābā* — "and the sky is opened, and becomes as if it were gates." The word *al-samāʾ* is the same word the sūrah used for the firmaments God *built* above the hearer — the strong, unbroken roof of verse 12, the *shidād* that held no fissure. Now, on the Day, that same sky is *opened* — *futiḥat* — and the unbroken becomes broken, the seamless becomes seamed, the single firm surface becomes a field of *gates*. The contrast is deliberate and devastating: the sky whose solidity the sūrah held up as a sign of power is now shown to be *openable* — a door after all, not a wall, and the One who built it closed is the One who opens it. The Day of Division is thus, in its first cosmic act, the *reversal of the world's stability*: the roof that held for a lifetime is opened in a moment, and what it held back — or held up — comes pouring through.

The grammar carries the theology. The verb *futiḥat* is in the passive — "is opened" — and the passive is the Qur'an's way of directing attention to the *event* rather than the *agent*, as though the opening is so total that the Opener needs no introduction; every hearer knows who opens. And the phrase *fa-kānat abwābā* — "and it becomes gates" — uses the verb of being to describe a *transformation*: the sky is not merely cracked or pierced; it is *converted* into gates, its entire substance turned into openings. The plural *abwāb* — gates, doors — multiplies the image: not one opening but *many*, the whole vault of heaven perforated with doors, so that there is no direction in which the sky is still a wall. The commentators note the echo of the Qur'an's other apocalyptic descriptions — the sky *rent asunder* (25:25; 84:1), *split* (55:37), *folded* (21:104), *stripped away* (81:11), *becoming like molten brass* (70:8) — and the sūrah's word here, *gates*, adds its own precise note: the opening is not a ruin but a *passage*. Gates are made for entering and exiting; the sky becomes gates because *something is coming through them*.

**The Descent of the Angels: What the Gates Are For**

What comes through the opened sky? The commentators are substantially agreed: the *angels*. The gates of the sky are opened for the descent of the angelic hosts, who come down for the judgment. The Qur'an states the connection explicitly in Sūrah al-Furqān:

> *"The day when the sky is split open with clouds, and the angels are sent down, descending."* (Qur'an 25:25)

And the sūrah's own later verse — verse 38 — will complete the picture: *"the Spirit and the angels stand in rows."* The opened sky of verse 19 is the *door of the descent*: the angels, who in the world were the hidden keepers and recorders of human deeds, now come down visibly, filling the opened heavens with their ranks, and the gates of the sky are the passages of their coming. The image reverses the normal order of things: in the world, the sky was a *barrier* — the roof that kept the unseen realm unseen; on the Day, it is an *entrance* — the opened threshold between the world and its Lord, through which the hosts of heaven pour down to attend the judgment. The sūrah's hearer, who has looked up all his life at a closed sky, is told that the closure is temporary and that the Day is the moment the closure ends.

The commentators develop the point with the Qur'an's own fuller picture. In Sūrah al-Fajr, the angels come *rank upon rank* (89:22) — and in Sūrah al-Zumar and elsewhere, the judgment is attended by the angels who carry the Throne, the angels who encircle it, and the angels of the ranks, all of them present at the final assize. The opened sky is the *architecture* of this presence: the heavens are opened precisely so that the unseen order of the world — the angels who recorded, guarded, and bore witness — can become visible at the moment of verdict. The sūrah's word *gates* is thus not merely a poetic image but a *functional* one: gates are what the sky becomes when the judgment requires the descent of its witnesses, and the opening is the first act of the court's assembly.

**The Sign of the Sky Completed: From Building to Opening**

The verse's deepest resonance is with the sūrah's own verse 12, and the connection deserves full statement because it is the heart of the sūrah's method. In verse 12, the sky was the *sign of power*: "Did We not build above you seven firmaments?" — the strong, unbroken roof that held without pillars, the standing proof of the Builder's might. Now, in verse 19, the same sky is *opened* — and the opening is the proof of the same might in its other aspect. The power that *built* the sky closed is the power that *opens* it; the Builder and the Opener are one. The sūrah is thus completing its own argument: the disbeliever was asked to consider the sky as a sign of the power that made it; now he is shown the sky being *unmade* by that power, so that no one can say the sky is more permanent than its Maker. The world's most stable thing — the roof that outlasted every generation — is shown to be *held*, not self-sustaining; and the holding is withdrawn, and the roof opens like a door. The lesson is the sūrah's central one, stated in its most cosmic form: *nothing in the created order is stable of itself; stability is a gift, and the Gift is recalled on the Day.*

There is also a profound inversion in the word *abwāb* — gates — that the commentators' love of language brings out. In the world, the sky was *closed* — and its closure was a mercy, the roof that protected the household beneath it. On the Day, the sky is *opened* — and its opening is the end of the protection, because the household is no longer being protected but *summoned*. The same sky that was a roof of mercy becomes a gate of judgment; the same closure that sheltered becomes an opening that summons. The sūrah's hearer is thus taught to read the sky in two tenses at once: *now*, it is the closed roof of God's sheltering; *then*, it will be the opened gate of God's gathering. And the reading of the two tenses together is the whole of faith — to live under the closed sky in gratitude, and to prepare for the opened sky in fear and hope.

**The Opened Sky and the Modern Imagination**

The image of the opened sky speaks to the modern reader with undiminished force, and perhaps with greater force than the sūrah's first hearers could have felt, because the modern reader has a keener sense of the sky's *vastness*. The heaven above is no longer the low dome of the ancient imagination but a universe of measureless extent, and the sūrah's claim that it will be *opened like gates* is therefore, to the modern ear, a claim of measureless power — the entire vault of space, with all its galaxies and distances, turned into a passage, because the judgment it serves is greater than everything it contains. The verse's cosmology is not an embarrassment to the modern mind but a *challenge* to it: it insists that the physical universe, however vast, is a *creature* — a made thing, held in being, and ultimately *opened* — and that the human being, however small against the sky, is the *purpose* for which the sky is opened. The gates are opened for *us*: the cosmos is unsealed not to reveal a deeper astronomy but to convene the judgment of the creatures who lived beneath it. This is the sūrah's staggering reversal of perspective: the sky that dwarfs us is *for* us, and on the Day it will be opened *because of* us — because the reckoning of a single human soul is weightier, in the scale of eternity, than the vault that contains all the stars.

**The Verdict Begins at the Threshold**

Finally, the verse's function in the sūrah's narrative must be marked. The trumpet (v. 18) *summoned*; the opened sky (v. 19) *admits*; the moving mountains (v. 20) *dissolve*; and then, with the cosmos unmade, the sūrah turns from the scenery to the *sentence* — Hell lying in wait for the rebellious (v. 21). The opened sky is the *threshold* of the judgment: it is the moment the world's protective architecture is withdrawn and the court is thrown open. The hearer who has been carried from the question of verse 1 through the signs of verses 6–16 and into the Day of verses 17–19 now stands at the edge of the verdict, with the sky open above him and the judgment beginning below. The sūrah has not yet pronounced a word about his fate — it will, in the verses that follow, divide the throngs into the two destinies — but it has placed him, unmistakably, at the threshold, under the opened gates, with no roof between him and the descending hosts. And that placement is the sūrah's final warning and its final mercy: the gates are not yet open *now*; the sky still stands; the roof still shelters; and the interval between the closed sky and the opened one is the interval of choice.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:20

> **and the mountains are set in motion as if they were a mirage.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pegs Pulled from the Earth**

The verse completes the unmaking of the world with the most dramatic reversal the sūrah has staged: *wa-suyyirati al-jibālu fa-kānat sarābā* — "and the mountains are set in motion, and become as if they were a mirage." The word *al-jibāl* — the mountains — is the same word the sūrah used in verse 7, where the mountains were the *awtād*, the pegs driven into the earth to hold it steady. There, the mountain was the very image of permanence — the anchor of the world, the thing that could not be moved. Here, the same mountains are *set in motion* — the verb *suyyirat* is the passive of *sayyara*, "to make to travel, to set moving" — and the pegs are pulled, the anchors are loosed, and the immovable is shown to be, in the grip of its Maker, no more stable than dust in the wind. The sūrah's argument could not be stated more forcefully: the thing it held up, eleven verses ago, as the world's firmest point, is on the Day shown to be a *mirage* — and the contrast between the two verses is the chapter's whole theology of the created order.

The word *sarāb* — mirage — is the verse's final and most precise image, and it must be unfolded with care. A *sarāb* is the desert illusion: the shimmering appearance of water on the horizon that the traveler sees and hurries toward, and that vanishes as he approaches — a *nothing* that looks like a *something*, a vision with no substance. The lexicographers note the word's kinship with *saraba*, "to flow away," and the image is of something that *slips through the grasp*: the mirage is the perfect symbol of that which seems solid and is not. By saying the mountains *become a mirage* on the Day, the sūrah is saying something far more than that they are destroyed; it is saying that their *apparent solidity was always, in the final accounting, a seeming* — that what looked like the most substantial thing in the world is revealed, when its Maker withdraws His holding of it, to be no more substantial than the desert's false water. The Qur'an states the same truth in Sūrah al-Naml with the same image, and the commentators read the two passages together:

> *"And you see the mountains, supposing them solid, while they pass away like the passing of clouds — the work of God, Who perfects all things."* (Qur'an 27:88)

"Supposing them solid" — *taḥsabuhā jāmidatan* — is the precise diagnosis: the solidity of the mountains is a *supposition*, a judgment of the eye, and the Day corrects the eye. The mirage is the truth of all worldly permanence.

**The Reversal of the Sūrah's Own Argument**

The verse's power lies almost entirely in its *placement*, and this is the point the commentary must make most forcefully. The sūrah has been building its argument from the *stability* of the world: the earth spread as a cradle (v. 6), the mountains driven as pegs (v. 7), the heavens built as a firm roof (v. 12), the sun hung as a blazing lamp (v. 13). Every sign depended on the *solidity* of the created order — the reliability of the ground, the permanence of the mountain, the unbrokenness of the sky. The disbeliever was asked to consider these stabilities and infer, from them, the power of their Maker. Now, in verses 19–20, the sūrah *withdraws* the stabilities one by one — the sky opened (v. 19), the mountains dissolved (v. 20) — and the withdrawal is the completion of the argument: the same power that established the stabilities is the power that dissolves them, and the dissolution proves that the stability was *held*, not self-owned. The mountain that anchored the world was anchored *by* God; the peg was driven *by* God; and the Day is simply God's hand withdrawing from the peg. The sūrah's method is thus revealed in its full brilliance: it built up the world's solidity in the middle section *precisely so that* it could dissolve it in the final section, and the contrast between the built and the dissolved is the strongest possible statement of the world's dependence on its Maker.

This is the Qur'anic teaching on the *contingency of the world* — the truth that everything other than God exists by His sustaining, and that the sustaining can be withdrawn. The sūrah's images are the Qur'an's most vivid pedagogy of this truth: the mountain, the sky, the sun — the things human beings have always taken as the very type of permanence — are shown to be *borrowed* permanence, held in being by the One who made them. The commentators connect the verse to the Qur'an's general statements of this teaching:

> *"God holds the heavens and the earth, lest they cease..."* (Qur'an 35:41)

The mountains *pass away* because they never stood of themselves; the sky *opens* because it never closed of itself; and the Day of Division is, in its cosmic dimension, the moment the borrowed solidity of the world is called in, and only God remains — as the sūrah will say in its closing movement, the Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them (v. 37). The mirage is the image of every created permanence; the Day is the moment the mirage is exposed.

**A Warning Against the Worship of the Solid**

The verse also carries a sharp spiritual warning that the commentators draw out, and it is aimed at every age including our own. The human heart, the Qur'an teaches, is prone to *trust the solid* — to rest its weight on the things of the world precisely because they seem permanent: wealth, position, strength, the mountain-like institutions and powers that appear immovable. The verse's image of the mountain becoming a mirage is the Qur'an's demolition of this trust: the things that seem most solid are the things that will be shown most hollow, and the weight a person rests on them is weight rested on a mirage. The sūrah's hearer is being taught to *redistribute* his trust: not to the pegs of the world, which will be pulled, but to the One who drives the pegs and will pull them. The Qur'an states this as a general law in its treatment of the world's vanities:

> *"Know that the life of this world is play and diversion and adornment and boasting among you and rivalry in wealth and children — like the example of rain whose growth pleases the tillers, then it dries and you see it turn yellow, then it becomes chaff."* (Qur'an 57:20)

The mountain and the field share one fate: both are *held*, both are *shown*, and both are *withdrawn*. The believer is the one who learns, from the sūrah's image, to lean on the only thing that does not pass — and the sūrah's own later verse will name that thing: the *Face* of the Lord (cf. 55:26–27, "All that is upon it passes away, and there remains the Face of your Lord, Possessor of Majesty and Bounty").

**The Psychology of the Reversed World**

There is a further, more experiential level to the verse that the commentators preserve: the effect of the Day's reversal on the *human psyche*. A person's entire sense of reality is built on the stability of the world — the ground that holds, the sky that stands, the mountain that does not move. On the Day, this sense of reality is *taken apart*: the ground is convulsed, the sky is opened, the mountain flows like a mirage — and the human being, who has spent a lifetime calibrating himself to a stable world, is suddenly in a world where nothing holds. The Qur'an describes this psychic dislocation precisely — *"On the Day when the earth and the mountains shake, and the mountains become a heap of shifting sand"* (73:14), and the human beings are *"like scattered moths"* (101:4), their composure shattered with the world they trusted. The verse is thus not only a statement about the mountains but a *warning about the self*: the person who has built his peace on the world's stability will lose his peace with the world's stability; the person who has built his peace on God will find, on that Day, that his peace was the one thing that did not pass. The mirage of the mountain is the fate of every worldly foundation; and the sūrah's whole purpose is to move its hearer's foundation *now*, while the mountain still stands, from the passing to the Permanent.

**The Mirage and the Modern Sense of Solid Things**

For the modern reader, the verse's image lands with a force that has only grown, because the modern world has discovered, at the scale of physics, the very truth the verse states at the scale of image: the solidity of matter is, in the deepest analysis, a *seeming*. The table, the stone, the mountain — the things that feel most dense — are, at the atomic level, mostly empty space, held in their apparent solidity by forces the eye cannot see. Modern physics has not made the mountain *less* real, but it has made the *appearance* of solidity newly strange: the mountain's massiveness is, in a sense the seventh century could not have imagined, a *holding-together* rather than a *given*. The sūrah's claim — that the mountain's solidity is a *supposition*, and that its Maker can dissolve it into a mirage — reads, in the light of modern science, less like poetic hyperbole and more like a precise intuition of the world's dependence. The believer and the physicist, from different directions, arrive at the same humility: the solid world is *held*, and the holding is not the world's own.

The verse's practical call, then, is the sūrah's whole call in its most concentrated form: *do not build on the mirage.* Whatever in your life feels like a mountain — your strength, your wealth, your position, your plans, the institutions and powers you trust — is a held thing, and the holding will be withdrawn. Build instead on the Holder. The sūrah has taken its hearer through the signs of the world in order to lead him, finally, to this single conclusion: the world is a *gift*, not a *ground*; a *sign*, not a *stay*. The mountain that anchors the earth will be moved; the sky that roofs the world will be opened; and the only permanence is the One who moves and opens. The Day of Division is the day this is made visible to every eye — and the sūrah's mercy is that it has made it visible *now*, in words, while the mountain still stands and the choice is still open.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:21

> **Truly Hell lies in ambush,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Watcher at the End of the Road**

With verse 21 the sūrah turns from the cosmic scenery of the Day to its *destination*, and the image it chooses is one of the most chilling in the entire Qur'an: *inna jahannama kānat mirṣādā* — "Truly Hell is an ambush." The word *mirṣād* is from the root *r-ṣ-d*, which means to *watch, lie in wait, observe* — the root of *raṣad* (an observation post) and *tarassada* (to lie in ambush for). A *mirṣād* is a *place of watching* — a lookout, an ambush-point, the position where one waits for the prey that must pass by. The verse says Hell *is* a *mirṣād*: it is the watcher posted on the road, the ambush laid at the crossing, the place that *waits* for those who are coming to it. The verb *kānat* — the perfect of "to be" — lends the statement the same settled weight as the earlier "the Day is an appointed moment": Hell *has been* an ambush, is an ambush, from before the world began — the trap is set, the watch is posted, and the question is only who walks into it.

The image must be felt precisely, because its force is in its *passivity of waiting*. An ambush does not pursue; it *waits*. The prey walks into it. The *mirṣād* of the verse is Hell's relationship to the rebellious: it does not hunt them down; it *lies in wait for them* on the road they have themselves chosen, and their arrival is the completion of their own direction. This is the sūrah's — and the Qur'an's — deepest teaching about Hell's justice: Hell is not an arbitrary prison into which God throws people, but the *end of a road the traveler has walked*, the ambush that was always at the end of the path of rebellion. The commentators state it plainly: Hell lies in ambush for the *rebellious* (the next verse names them) — those who turned against the truth — and it will take them *when they die*, because they had been heedless of it all their lives. The heedlessness is the walking; the death is the arrival; the ambush is the destination they never looked for because they never looked.

**The Root of Watching: What the Mirṣād Sees**

The root *r-ṣ-d* deserves its full unfolding, because the Qur'an's choice of it is exact. The word family is the language of *surveillance*: a *raṣad* in classical Arabic is a watch-post, a place from which the movements of others are observed; the verb describes the patient, attentive watching of one who will not be seen and cannot be escaped. The commentators note the deliberate irony in calling Hell a *mirṣād*: the rebellious spent their lives as though they were *unwatched* — doing in secret, forgetting the Observer, assuming that the road ahead was empty. The verse corrects the assumption with a single word: the road was never empty; the end of it was always *watching*. Hell's very nature, on this image, is *attentiveness* — it does not sleep, it does not miss, it does not forget; the *mirṣād* is the opposite of heedlessness, and it is set against the heedless. The sūrah's earlier word for the deniers' condition was exactly heedlessness — they "anticipated not a reckoning" (v. 27) — and the *mirṣād* is the precise opposite term: they did not watch, but it did.

There is also a subtle point in the Qur'an's broader use of the root that the commentators preserve. God Himself is described in the Qur'an as *observant* of His servants — *"Truly your Lord is ever watchful (bi-al-mirṣād)"* (89:14) — and the same word that names Hell's watching names God's. The two watchings are one reality seen from two sides: God watches over all things, and His watching, for the rebellious, takes the form of the ambush that awaits them. The *mirṣād* of Hell is thus not a rival power but an *instrument* of the divine watchfulness — the form that God's observation of the unrepentant takes at the end of their road. The verse's image is therefore not of a demonic trap but of a *divine appointment*: the watcher at the end of the path is the same justice that watched the path being walked.

**The Psychology of the Ambush: Why the Image Warns**

The choice of the ambush image has a precise psychological purpose, and the commentators' treatment of it reveals why the sūrah chose this picture over any other. An ambush is the punishment that arrives *where you are going*, not where you are. The rebellious person's error, throughout the world, is to *separate the deed from its destination*: the sin is here, its consequence is nowhere; the rebellion is now, its reckoning is never. The ambush image destroys this separation: it declares that the consequence is *already placed* at the end of the rebellion, that the road and its end are one thing, and that every step of rebellion is a step *toward* the waiting trap. The heedless person feels safe not because there is no danger but because the danger is *ahead* of him, and the sūrah's image drags the danger into view: the trap is not hypothetical, not postponed, not uncertain — it is *set*, and it is *watching*, and you are walking toward it at the speed of your own days. The image converts the future punishment into a present reality: to know that Hell is a *mirṣād* is to know, now, that the road has a watcher at its end.

This is why the verse is a mercy in the form of a warning. The purpose of telling the heedless that the ambush is set is not to gloat over the trap but to *turn the traveler aside before he reaches it*. The whole function of the warning is its timing: the *mirṣād* is ahead, and the hearer is not yet there. The sūrah's description of Hell — here and in the verses that follow — is the Qur'an's way of posting, in words, the sign that the ambush itself will not post: a *warning of the watcher*, delivered while the road can still be changed. The Prophet ﷺ's entire mission is described in the Qur'an as exactly this — a *warner* (nadhīr) sent before the punishment — and the sūrah's *mirṣād* is the content of the warning: the trap is real, it is set, and you are being told of it now so that you may not walk into it.

**Hell's Waiting and the Hereafter's Certainty**

The verse also contributes a distinct note to the sūrah's doctrine of the Hereafter: Hell is not *created* on the Day of Judgment; it *exists*, and has existed, and *waits*. The verb *kānat* — "it has been" — is the Qur'an's assertion of Hell's pre-existence, and the classical theology built on it the doctrine that the Garden and the Fire are *already created*, awaiting their inhabitants. The commentators connect this to the Prophet's ﷺ accounts of the *isrāʾ* and *miʿrāj*, the night journey, in which he was shown both the Garden and the Fire as existing realities, and to the Qur'an's own statement that the Garden has been *prepared* for the reverent (cf. 3:133) and the Fire *prepared* for the disbelievers (cf. 2:24; 3:131). The *mirṣād* is thus not a threat that may or may not materialize but a *standing fact*: the destination is already built, the watcher is already posted, and the world's drama is being played out *in front of* a set that is already complete. This is the verse's deepest realism: the Hereafter is not a future possibility but a *present reality awaiting its moment* — and the only thing still undecided is which of the two prepared destinations each traveler is walking toward.

This realism is the verse's gift to the believer as well as its warning to the heedless. The believer who reads "Truly Hell is an ambush" reads it with the gratitude of one who has been *shown the trap*: he knows the road of heedlessness ends in the watcher, and he has, by the mercy of the warning, kept to the other road — the road the sūrah's final movement will describe, whose end is not an ambush but a garden. The verse's place in the sūrah's structure makes this clear: the *mirṣād* is followed, in verses 31–36, by the description of the reverent's reward, and the two destinations stand side by side as the two ends of the two roads. The sūrah is not describing Hell for its own sake but as the *dark half* of a double picture whose bright half is coming. And the hearer, standing between the two halves, is being asked the question the whole chapter exists to ask: *which road are you on, and what waits at its end?*

**The Ambush and the Everyday Road**

Finally, the verse's image can be brought home to the everyday in a way that preserves its force. Every life is a road, and every road has an end; the sūrah's claim is that the ends are *fixed* — the ambush for the rebellious, the garden for the reverent — and that the fixing of the ends is the condition of the road's meaning. A person does not know, in the world, which road he is on, because the two roads look alike in the walking: both are lived in the same sun, the same work, the same days. But the sūrah insists that the roads are *distinguishable* — by the direction of the steps, the content of the deeds, the state of the heart — and that the distinction, invisible to the world, is fully visible to the Watcher at the end. The verse's word *mirṣād* is the promise of that visibility: the watcher sees which road is being walked, and the ambush is set only on one of them. To live with "Truly Hell is an ambush" in the heart is to live with the question of the road always present — not in terror, but in the clarifying awareness that the steps matter because the end is fixed. The sūrah, having shown the watcher, will now show the road it watches for — the rebellious — and the drink that waits at the ambush's end.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:22

> **a place unto which the rebellious return**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Name of Those the Ambush Waits For**

The verse completes the sentence begun in verse 21: Hell is an ambush, *li-al-ṭāghīna maʾābā* — "for the rebellious, a place of return." The word *ṭāghīn* is the active participle of the root *ṭ-gh-y*, and it names the precise class of people the ambush is set for. The root *ṭ-gh-y* means to *exceed the bound, overflow, transgress* — the same root from which comes *ṭughyān*, "rebellion, transgression, the overstepping of limits," and *ṭāghūt*, "the tyrant, the false god, anything worshipped besides God." A *ṭāghī* is thus not merely a sinner but a *transgressor* — one who has crossed the boundary God set, who has exceeded his measure, who has refused the limit and overrun it. The commentators are careful about this precision: the ambush of Hell is not for humanity in general, nor for the weak, nor for those who sinned in frailty and repented; it is for the *ṭāghīn* — those who *rebelled*, who knew the bound and crossed it, who made a direction of their life out of exceeding. The word carries the whole Qur'anic diagnosis of the human ruin: ruin is not a matter of this or that sin but of *ṭughyān* — the standing refusal to accept a limit.

The root's image is drawn from water: *ṭaghā al-māʾ* is said of water that *overflows its banks* — the flood that will not stay in its bed. The rebellious are, on this image, a *flood*: a force that was given a channel and refused it, that rose over its bounds and swept everything in its path. The Qur'an uses the same root for Pharaoh, the archetype of rebellion — *"Pharaoh was tyrannical (ṭaghā) in the land"* (20:24; cf. 79:17) — and for every power that has exceeded its measure. The *ṭāghīn* of this verse are the human beings who have done in their own lives what Pharaoh did in his: refused the bound God set, exalted themselves beyond their measure, and made their own desire the law. The ambush of verse 21 is set for exactly this: the flood's end is the sea of fire that *returns* it — and the word *maʾāb* is the key to how the sūrah frames it.

**The Place of Return: The Irony of Maʾāb**

The word *maʾāb* is the verse's second precision, and it carries an irony the commentators preserve. *Maʾāb* is from the root *ʾ-w-b*, "to return," and it means a *place of return, a homecoming, a refuge* — the word is used in the Qur'an for the *good* return, the return to God's mercy and the Garden, as when David is told *"and truly for him is nearness to Us and a goodly return (maʾāb)"* (38:25), and when the believers are promised *"an excellent place of return"* (cf. 38:40, 49). The word's native meaning is *warm* — a *maʾāb* is where one comes *home*. And here, in a deliberate reversal, the Qur'an applies the word of homecoming to the rebellious and their destination: Hell is their *maʾāb* — their *place of return* — the home they were, all along, walking toward. The irony is the point: the human being was created for a good return — the return to his Lord in mercy — and the rebellious have *exchanged* the return for this one. The *ṭughyān* was their choice of direction, and the *maʾāb* is simply the direction's end: a person returns to what he was walking toward. The flood returns to the sea it came from; the rebellious return to the rebellion's end. The word *maʾāb* thus completes the *mirṣād* of the previous verse: the ambush is a *homecoming* — the rebel's true home, reached at last, the destination he spent his life unknowingly seeking.

This framing is the sūrah's — and the Qur'an's — deepest statement of Hell's *justice*. Hell is not an alien place the rebellious are dragged to against the grain of their lives; it is the *return* — the completion, the coming-home — of the direction they themselves chose. The Qur'an states this logic of return in many places: *"Every soul is held in pledge for what it has earned"* (74:38), and the wrongdoers are told that the punishment is *"not but the recompense of what you used to do"* (cf. 32:17, 45:33). The *maʾāb* is the same truth in spatial form: the rebellious return to the rebellion's destination because the destination was always inside the direction. A life of *ṭughyān* is a life aimed at a *maʾāb*, and the aiming is what makes the arrival just.

**Who the Ṭāghīn Are: The Profile of Rebellion**

The verse names the inhabitants of the ambush, and the commentators fill in the profile from the Qur'an's fuller descriptions, so that the warning is not abstract but personal. The *ṭāghīn* are those who: *rejected* the truth after it came to them (cf. 2:39; 7:36); *denied* the signs of God (v. 28 of this very sūrah); *anticipated no reckoning* (v. 27); and, in the Qur'an's most concentrated portrait, those who *preferred the life of this world* and made its pursuit their measure (cf. 79:37–39). The rebellion the verse names is not one sin but a *posture* — the posture of the creature who will not accept creaturehood, who lives as though the bounds of God were optional and the reckoning a fiction. The sūrah's next verses will fill the portrait further — the *ṭāghīn* "anticipated not a reckoning" and "denied Our signs with utter denial" (vv. 27–28) — and the cumulative picture is of a life lived in *heedless refusal*: not ignorance, but the refusal of knowledge; not weakness, but the will to exceed.

But the commentators are equally careful about the *boundary* of the term, because the Qur'an's justice is precise. *Ṭughyān* is not the same as ordinary human sin, and the Qur'an's door of repentance stands open precisely to those who *sinned and then turned* (cf. 4:110; 39:53). The *ṭāghī* is the one who *persisted* — who made the transgression a way of life and died in it. The distinction matters for the sūrah's hearer: the verse is not describing an arbitrary damned class but a *direction*, and a direction can be changed as long as the traveler lives. The very naming of the *ṭāghīn* is an act of mercy, because a named disease is a treatable disease: to hear the profile of rebellion described is to be able to check oneself against it, and to turn, while there is time, from the road of the flood to the road of the return that is good.

**The Two Returns: The Structure of the Sūrah's End**

The word *maʾāb* also performs a structural function that binds the sūrah's ending together, and it is worth marking. The rebellious have a *maʾāb* — a return — and it is the ambush. The reverent, in the sūrah's later verses, have their own destination — the *mafāzā*, "a place of triumph" (v. 31), the gardens and the overflowing cups. The sūrah is built on this *double return*: every human being is on a road, and every road has an end, and the two ends are the two *maʾābs* — the return of the rebel to the ambush, and the return of the reverent to the garden. The Qur'an states the double return explicitly in its summary of the two ways:

> *"So whosoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whosoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

The *maʾāb* of verse 22 is the evil half of that double seeing; the sūrah's later verses are the good half. And the hearer is placed, by the sūrah's structure, exactly between them — having been shown the rebel's return, and about to be shown the reverent's, with the choice of direction as the only open question.

**The Warning Turned Inward**

Finally, the verse's practical address must be made explicit, because it is the sūrah's whole purpose in naming the rebellious. The first hearers of the sūrah — the Quraysh who "questioned one another" — were the *ṭāghīn* being described, and the verse's naming of them was a direct address: the "they" of the opening verses is the "rebellious" of verse 22, and the ambush set for the rebellious is set for *them*. But the sūrah's address is not confined to its first audience, and the verse's warning turns inward for every reader: the *ṭughyān* it names is a direction, not a genealogy, and no reader is immune from checking his own road. The question the verse presses is personal and immediate: *is there any bound of God that I am currently overrunning — any limit I treat as optional, any reckoning I live as though it would not come?* If so, the sūrah says, I am on the road of the *ṭāghīn*, and the ambush at its end is not a metaphor but a *maʾāb* — a homecoming already prepared. And the sūrah's mercy is the timing of the telling: the road can still be changed, the flood can still return to its banks, and the return that is *good* — the *maʾāb* of the reverent — is still open to every traveler who turns before the road ends.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:23

> **to tarry therein for ages.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Duration of the Ambush's Hold**

The verse fixes the *length* of the rebellious' stay in their return-place: *lābithīna fīhā aḥqābā* — "tarrying therein for ages." The word *lābithīn* is the active participle of *labitha*, "to remain, to tarry, to stay" — and it pictures the inhabitants of Hell as *stayers*, those who are not passing through but *remaining*. The word *aḥqāb* is the plural of *ḥuqb*, a *long period of time*, an *age* — and its plural has generated one of the classical commentaries' most discussed questions, because the duration of Hell's punishment is a matter of the deepest doctrinal weight. The verse's grammar is stark: the rebellious will *tarry in it for ages* — period after period, age after age — and the commentators' discussion of what the *ages* mean is a window into how the Islamic tradition handles its hardest and most consequential questions with honesty and care.

The plain and dominant reading is the one adopted by the vast majority of commentators: the *aḥqāb* indicate *endless* duration. On this reading, the plural of *ḥuqb* is not a count but an *intensification* — "ages upon ages," one period following another without end, so that the word *aḥqābā* is the Qur'an's way of saying "forever" in the idiom of succession. The commentators who take this view — and they are the overwhelming majority — note that the Qur'an elsewhere states the eternity of Hell explicitly, with words that leave no room for temporariness: *"they will not have it lightened, nor will they be given respite"* (2:162; 3:88), *"they will abide therein forever (khālidīn fīhā abadan)"* (4:169; 33:65; 72:23), and *"they will not be brought out of it"* (cf. 5:37). On this reading, *aḥqābā* is one more expression of the same truth: the ages succeed one another without terminal, and the tarrying has no end.

**The Minority Reading: The Ages as a Term**

There is, however, a second reading, preserved with scholarly honesty by the commentators even while the majority rejected it, and it deserves careful statement because it shows the tradition's breadth. A minority — the source commentary notes them — read *aḥqābā* as indicating a *limited* duration: "ages" in the sense of a *definite, if unimaginably long, span*, so that Hell, though endless in relation to human reckoning, would have a limit in the absolute sense. This reading drew support from the word's grammar — *aḥqāb* is a *plural of paucity or succession*, and could be read as "a number of ages" — and from a strand of theological reflection, associated with some early figures, that emphasized God's mercy as ultimately prevailing over His wrath in a way that might, in the absolute sense, bound the punishment. The majority of the tradition, however, rejected this reading, and the rejection itself is instructive. The commentators point out, first, that the Qur'an's own explicit statements of eternity (the *khālidīn fīhā abadan* verses) are decisive and cannot be set aside by a debatable reading of one plural; second, that the context of the verse — the *ṭāghīn* who denied the signs and anticipated no reckoning — is the context of those for whom the Qur'an elsewhere closes the door absolutely; and third, that the reading of *aḥqāb* as "a limited span" imports a meaning the text does not require and the surrounding verses do not support.

The tradition's handling of this question is a model of method: it states the minority view fully, weighs it against the whole of the Qur'an, and lets the weight of the text decide. And it holds the two things in tension that the question touches: the *justice* of God, which the eternity of Hell serves — the unrepentant rebel has no claim on a mercy he refused — and the *mercy* of God, which the famous ḥadīth of intercession (quoted in the source commentary and preserved in al-Bukhārī and Muslim) displays in its fullness even on the Day of Judgment, when God commands that whoever has a mustard-seed's weight of faith be brought out of the Fire. The two truths are not contradictory: Hell's eternity is for those in whose heart there was *no* faith at all — the *ṭāghīn* of this verse — while the mercy of extraction is for those in whom some faith remained. The *aḥqāb* of the verse belong to the first class, not the second.

**The Psychology of the Word "Ages"**

Why does the Qur'an express Hell's duration as *ages* rather than simply saying "forever"? The commentators' answer opens the verse's rhetorical purpose. The word *aḥqāb* works on the imagination in a way the bare word "eternity" does not. "Forever" is an abstraction the mind cannot picture; "ages upon ages" is a *succession* the mind can begin to feel — one age, then another, then another, each one full of the same punishment, and no age the last. The plural's force is *temporal weight*: the hearer is made to feel the duration as a *piling up* of periods, each one complete and yet not final, so that the horror is not in any single age but in the *succession* — the knowledge that the age now ending is followed by another that will also end, and another, without any of them being the end. The commentators note that this is the Qur'an's characteristic way with the unrepresentable: it does not say what cannot be pictured; it *stages* the unpicturable in a rhythm the mind can follow. The *aḥqāb* are the rhythm of eternity, and the hearer who follows the rhythm to its implied end feels, without being told in the abstract, what "no end" means.

This is also why the verse is placed where it is: immediately after the naming of the rebellious and their return-place, and immediately before the description of what they will taste there (vv. 24–25). The sūrah's order is the order of a *warning escalating*: the ambush (v. 21), its inhabitants (v. 22), its duration (v. 23), its content (vv. 24–25), its justice (v. 26). Each verse adds a heavier stone to the warning, and the *aḥqāb* is the stone of *duration* — the one that converts the punishment from an event into a *condition*, from a moment into an *age*, from something that might be endured into something that cannot be outlasted. The hearer who has followed the sūrah this far is being made to feel, with the full force of the Qur'an's rhetoric, that the road of the rebellious ends not in a punishment but in an *age of punishment*, and that the age has no successor but another age.

**The Duration and the Deeds: Proportion in the Reckoning**

A question the commentators also address, because the verse's word *aḥqāb* raises it, is the *proportion* between the duration and the deeds. How can a finite life of rebellion merit an unending punishment? The classical answer, preserved in the theological tradition and stated in the commentaries, is that the proportion is not between the *time* of the sin and the *time* of the punishment but between the *object* of the sin and the *gravity* of the punishment. The rebellion of the *ṭāghīn* was a rebellion against the *infinite* — against the Lord of the heavens and the earth — and a sin against the infinite carries a weight the finite span of a life cannot bound. Moreover, the theologians add, the rebellious did not merely sin for a finite time and then cease; they *persisted* — the direction of *ṭughyān* is a *standing* direction, a rebellion that would have continued forever had the life continued, and the punishment corresponds to the *direction*, not merely the *deeds*. The *aḥqāb* are the duration of a *standing refusal*, and the standing refusal has no natural end. The verse thus rests, in the final analysis, on the same principle the sūrah has been teaching throughout: the Hereafter is the *unveiling of what was always true* — the rebel was always a rebel, the heedless was always heedless, and the *ages* of Hell are the unveiling of a direction that had no intention of turning.

**The Warning's Single Purpose: Turn Before the Ages Begin**

The verse's practical force, finally, is all in its *timing*, and the sūrah never lets the hearer forget it. The *aḥqāb* are *ahead* — the ages have not begun, the ambush is still only watching, and the tarrying is still only *described*, not *entered*. The entire weight of the warning is aimed at the present moment: the hearer who reads "tarrying therein for ages" is being told, while he still has time, what the end of his road is — so that he may change the road. The Prophet's ﷺ mission, as the Qur'an frames it, is precisely this: *"a warner, that he may warn... and that those who perish may perish by a clear proof, and those who live may live by a clear proof"* (8:42). The description of the ages is the *clear proof*; the living and the perishing are both, now, choices; and the sūrah's warning is the mercy that makes the choice possible. To read "for ages" and to feel, for a moment, the weight of an unending succession, is to have received the warning in its full seriousness — and the seriousness is itself the gift, because the alternative is to feel it first when the ages have begun and the time for feeling has ended.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:24

> **They taste therein neither coolness nor drink,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Denial of the Two Relieves**

The verse moves from the duration of Hell to its *content*, and it begins with a negation that the next verse will complete: *lā yadhūqūna fīhā bardan wa-lā sharābā* — "They taste therein neither coolness nor drink." The verb *yadhūqūn* — "they taste" — is the Qur'an's chosen word for *direct experience*: it is the verb of the palate, of the first-hand savoring of a thing, and it is used throughout the Qur'an for both the punishment and the reward — *"so taste"* (the sūrah's own v. 30), *"that they may taste the punishment"* (4:56), and, for the believers, *"that they may taste of His mercy"* (cf. 30:46). The word's irony here is deliberate: the rebellious *taste* — they experience directly — but what they taste is a *denial*: neither *bard* (coolness) nor *sharāb* (drink). The two words name the two things a parched, burning body most longs for — the *cool* that relieves heat, and the *drink* that relieves thirst — and the verse declares both *absent*. The punishment of the *ṭāghīn* is thus introduced not as a positive infliction but as a *withholding*: the removal of every relief, the absence of every comfort, the taste of *nothing that helps*.

The commentators unfold the two words with precision, because each carries a distinct meaning. *Bard* is *coolness, coldness* — the opposite of heat, the relief of shade and breeze, the thing a creature in fire would crave above all. The verse denies it: no coolness, no shade, no relief from the heat — and the denial is itself a dimension of the punishment, because heat without the hope of cool is heat doubled by despair. *Sharāb* is *drink* — the generic word for anything potable, water above all — and the verse denies it too: no water, no relief for the throat, no quenching of the thirst that the fire's proximity must cause. The two denials together are the Qur'an's way of describing the *absolute minimum of comfort* as an absolute *absence*: the rebel who had everything in the world and thanked nothing is reduced to *nothing* — not even the cool and the water that the world gives freely to every creature. The verse's structure — "neither X nor Y" — is the grammar of total privation.

**The Thirst and the Heat: The Body's Two Cries**

Why coolness and drink, of all things? The commentators' answer is rooted in the *body's* experience, and it reveals the Qur'an's psychological precision. The two most elemental bodily cries are the cry for *temperature* (to be neither burned nor frozen) and the cry for *water* (to be quenched). Everything else a body needs is, in extremity, subordinate to these two: a person can endure hunger longer than thirst, and can endure most deprivations if the heat is tolerable and the water available. By denying precisely these two — the cool and the drink — the verse strips the punishment down to the body's *most basic, most inescapable* needs, and declares them unmet. The image is of a creature in *permanent thirst and permanent heat*, with no relief of either — and the commentators note that the Arabic pairing of the two words is itself a rhetorical completeness: *bard* (cool) and *sharāb* (drink) are the two *liquids of relief*, the cold and the wet, and to deny both is to deny relief itself. The verse is the Qur'an's most economical description of *absolute discomfort*: nothing cool, nothing to drink, forever.

The next verse will supply the positive content — what they *do* taste in place of the denied coolness and drink — and the commentators note the deliberate structure of the two verses together: verse 24 states the *privation* (no coolness, no drink), and verse 25 states the *substitution* (boiling liquid and a cold murky fluid). The punishment is thus framed as an *exchange*: the two relieves are withheld, and in their place are given two *torments* that *mock* the relieves — a liquid that burns instead of quenching, and a cold that freezes instead of cooling. The sūrah's method is the method of *inversion*: the very things the body craves are *parodied* in the punishment, so that the craving itself becomes the torment. The verse's "neither coolness nor drink" is the first half of the inversion; the next verse completes it.

**The Denial as Recompense: What They Did With Their Cool and Drink**

The verse's deepest meaning, however, is not physiological but *moral*, and the commentators draw it out from the sūrah's own logic. The rebellious had, in the world, the *coolness and the drink* — the shade, the water, the endless comforts that the sūrah's middle section described: the earth as a cradle, the rain sent down, the grain and the gardens produced. They received these and *did not thank*; they were given the provisions and *anticipated no reckoning*; they took the mercies and *denied the signs* (v. 28). The verse's denial is therefore the *mirror* of their own denial: as they denied the Giver of the cool and the drink, so the cool and the drink are denied them; as they refused to read the provisions as signs, so the provisions are withdrawn. The Qur'an states this principle of *mirrored recompense* throughout — *"the recompense of an evil is an evil like unto it"* (42:40) — and the verse is its most concrete form: the rebel's punishment is to be given back, in the next world, the *emptiness* he made of this one. He had the cool and the drink and treated them as nothing owed to anyone; he is given the fire and the thirst and taught, at last, what the mercies were worth.

This is also why the verse begins with *taste* — the verb of *first-hand knowing*. The rebellious denied the Hereafter because they had not *tasted* its reality; the verse promises that they will *taste* — directly, bodily, unmistakably — the reality they denied. The word *yadhūqūn* is the sūrah's answer to the opening *sa-yaʿlamūn* ("they will know"): the knowledge promised in verse 4 arrives, in verse 24, as *taste* — knowledge so direct that it is no longer arguable but *experienced*. The rebel who asked "when will this be?" will have his answer not in words but in the palate; the denial of the cool and the drink is the *form* the knowledge takes. The sūrah's arc from "they will know" to "they taste" is the arc of the whole Hereafter: from the warning to the fulfillment, from the description to the experience, from the word to the *taste*.

**The Two Denials and the One Lesson**

Finally, the verse teaches, through its very negation, the positive truth the sūrah has been building toward: the coolness and the drink are *gifts*. The rebel learns their value only by their absence — the cool denied, the drink withheld — and the lesson of the verse is that the believer must learn it *now*, in their presence. The shade of a tree, the cold of a glass of water, the rain of verse 14 — these are the *signs* the middle section described, and the believer's task is to receive them as gifts while they are given, to thank the Giver while the giving lasts, and to know that the same Giver who gives the cool and the drink can withhold them, and will withhold them, from those who took them without thanks. The verse is thus, in its dark way, a *celebration* of the ordinary mercies: it is by picturing their absence that the sūrah teaches their worth. The believer who reads "neither coolness nor drink" and then lifts a glass of cold water to his lips has received the verse's whole teaching: the water is a mercy, the mercy is a sign, the sign is from the Lord, and the Lord is the One who will ask, on the Day, what the water was used for and whether it was thanked.

The commentators add one final, sobering note that completes the verse's warning: the denial is *total* and *final*. The verse does not say the rebellious will have little coolness or delayed drink; it says *neither... nor* — the grammar of absolute exclusion. There is no relief, no interval, no cooling breeze, no single cup — the privation is as complete as the *aḥqāb* of the previous verse are long. The two verses together form the sūrah's most severe description of Hell's *condition*: endless in duration (v. 23), total in privation (v. 24), and — as the next verse will show — mockingly complete in its torments (v. 25). And the severity is, as always in this sūrah, the *warning's* severity: the description exists to be read *now*, to be felt *now*, to turn the traveler *now* — while the cool and the drink are still his to receive with thanks, and the road to the Garden is still open.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:25

> **save boiling liquid and a cold, murky fluid,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Drinks That Mock the Two Cravings**

The verse supplies the content that verse 24 withheld, and it does so with the sūrah's characteristic method of *inversion*: *illā ḥamīman wa-ghassāqā* — "save boiling liquid and a cold, murky fluid." The particle *illā* — "except, save" — completes the previous verse's negation: they taste *nothing* except these two. And the two things named are a deliberate, terrible *parody* of the two things denied. Verse 24 denied *coolness and drink* — the cold and the wet that relieve. Verse 25 supplies, in their place, *ḥamīm* and *ghassāq* — two liquids that *mock* the very craving they are given to: one a *boiling* liquid that burns instead of cooling, and one a *cold* fluid whose coldness is a *torment* rather than a relief. The punishment is thus designed with an exacting irony: the rebel is given the *form* of what he craves — liquid, temperature — but the form is filled with the *opposite* of relief. The drink is a burn; the cool is a freeze; and the two torments bracket the whole spectrum of temperature so that there is no refuge anywhere in it.

The word *ḥamīm* first. It is from the root *ḥ-m-m*, which means to *heat*, and *ḥamīm* is *intensely hot water* — water at the extreme of heat, boiling, scalding, at the maximum temperature that liquid can reach. The Qur'an uses the word throughout its descriptions of Hell, and always with this sense of the *extreme*: the inhabitants are given *ḥamīm* to drink (6:70; 10:4; 37:67; 38:57; 40:72; 44:46–48; 47:15; 56:42, 54), and the word's intensity is part of the punishment — not warm water but water at the *limit* of heat, the maximum the word can express. The commentators note a further precision: some read the word's root as also carrying the sense of *closeness, intimacy* — *ḥamīm* in Arabic also means a *close friend, an intimate* — and there is a bitter irony the commentators preserve: the word that in the world means the *dearest companion* means, in Hell, the *scalding liquid*, so that the rebel's punishment is, at the level of the very vocabulary, the *inversion of intimacy* — the drink that is the opposite of the friend.

**The Ghassāq: The Cold That Burns**

The second word, *ghassāq*, is one of the most discussed terms in the Qur'an's description of Hell, and the commentators record its meanings with care. The root *gh-s-q* carries the sense of *darkness and cold* — *ghasaq* is the *darkness of night*, the *gloaming* — and *ghassāq* is read by the majority of commentators as an *intensely cold, foul, murky fluid* — a liquid of freezing cold and unbearable stench. Ibn Kathīr transmits the description from the early authorities: the *ghassāq* is what gathers from the *pus, sweat, tears, and wounds* of the people of the Fire — the effluent of their tormented bodies, collected into a liquid of icy cold and intolerable stench, and given them to drink. The word thus combines three horrors at once: *cold* (the freezing extreme), *foulness* (the stench of corruption), and *origin* (it is the waste of their own suffering, returned to them). The *ghassāq* is the punishment's most revolting image — the drink that is distilled from the drinker's own torment — and the commentators note its function: it is the *opposite* of the pure, sweet, flowing water the Qur'an promises the believers in Paradise (cf. 47:15), the *anti-water*, the liquid form of everything the Garden's water is not.

The two words together, then, map the *two extremes of temperature* — *ḥamīm* the extreme of heat, *ghassāq* the extreme of cold — and the sūrah's point, the commentators observe, is the *totality* of the punishment: the inhabitants are placed between boiling and freezing, with nothing in between, no moderate zone, no relief. The source commentary captures this precisely: Hell is characterized not only by fire and heat but by *extremes at both ends of the spectrum*, and all the tension such extremes entail — whereas the Garden is described as a place of perfect *moderation* and tranquility. The contrast is the sūrah's deepest statement about the two destinies: *torture lies in the extremes; peace lies in the mean.* The rebel, who in the world made his own desire the measure and knew no bounds — who was, in the root sense of *ṭaghā*, a creature of *excess* — is given, in Hell, the *reign of excess*: temperature without moderation, sensation without relief, the extremes he lived by returned to him as his dwelling.

**The Drink as the Deed's Own Form**
