The commentators' most searching insight into this verse is that the punishment is *the deed's own form returned to the doer* — a principle the Qur'an states in many places and this verse embodies. The *ḥamīm* and the *ghassāq* are not arbitrary tortures invented by a wrathful imagination; they are the *liquified form* of what the rebel's life was. The rebel's wealth was *scalding* — earned and spent in excess, burning those it touched; and his deeds were *foul* — the corruption of the signs he denied, the pus and sweat of a life lived in heedless self-indulgence. The drink he is given is his own life *distilled*: the boiling is his excess, the foulness is his corruption, and the two together are the *taste* of what he made. The Qur'an states the principle generally — *"the recompense of an evil is an evil like unto it"* (42:40), and *"you are only recompensed for what you used to do"* (cf. 37:39) — and this verse is its most physically vivid expression: the punishment is not imposed *on* the rebel but *extracted from* him, from the very materials of the life he lived. The *ghassāq*'s origin in the sufferer's own wounds is the symbol of the whole: Hell's drink is the self's own output, returned.

This is the verse's deepest teaching about *justice*: the punishment is *fitting* (the next verse, v. 26, will name it exactly that — *a fitting recompense*). The rebel is not wronged; he is *repaid* — repaid in the currency of his own deeds, given to drink the taste of his own life. And the justice is precisely what makes the punishment bearable to contemplate, because it is *intelligible*: it is not arbitrary fury but *proportion* — the excess returned as excess, the foulness returned as foulness, the denial of the cool and the drink returned as the denial of the cool and the drink. The sūrah's whole doctrine of the Hereafter rests on this intelligibility: the Day of Division is not a display of raw power but the *application of a principle* — *"that each soul may be recompensed for what it has earned"* (cf. 14:51; 45:22) — and the *ḥamīm* and *ghassāq* are the principle made liquid.

**The Two Extremes and the One Mean: A Lesson for the Living**

Finally, the verse's lesson for the living is the same lesson the sūrah has been teaching in every register: *moderation*. The rebel's ruin was excess — the *ṭughyān* of a creature who would not stay within bounds — and his punishment is the *reign* of excess: the boiling and the freezing, the extremes without relief. The believer's safety is the *mean* — the moderation the Qur'an prescribes and praises, the balance of the life lived within God's limits. The sūrah's image of the two drinks is thus, in its dark way, a *celebration of the middle*: the cool and the drink that were denied (v. 24) are the *moderate* mercies — the shade that is not freezing, the water that is not boiling — and the believer is the one who learns, from their absence, to treasure the moderation he is given. The Garden, which the sūrah will describe in a few verses, is the *realm of the mean* — the place of neither excess nor privation, where the cups overflow but nothing intoxicates, where every pleasure is complete and none is extreme. The rebel traded the mean for the extreme in the world; Hell is the extreme's own country; and the sūrah's warning is the invitation to *return to the mean* — to the bounds of God, the moderation of the commanded life, and the cool and the drink that are, for now, still flowing.

The verse thus closes the sūrah's description of the punishment's *content* with the same double movement as its opening: a dark picture whose purpose is the *light* — a description of the extremes whose lesson is the *mean*, a portrait of the rebel's drink whose warning is the *believer's thanks*. The *ḥamīm* and the *ghassāq* are the last word on what the ambush holds; the next verse will say the single word that makes the whole picture just — *a fitting recompense* — and then the sūrah will turn, in its great second movement, from the dark half of the picture to the bright.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:26

> **a fitting recompense.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Single Word That Justifies the Whole**

The verse is the sūrah's briefest statement and, in a sense, its most important doctrinal sentence: *jazāʾan wifāqā* — "a recompense, fitting." Two words in the original, and they carry the entire theology of divine justice on which the sūrah's picture of Hell rests. *Jazāʾ* is the Qur'an's word for *recompense* — the return of a deed to its doer, the payment of what was earned — and *wifāq* is from the root *w-f-q*, which means to *correspond, match, be in proportion to*. A *jazāʾan wifāqā* is a recompense that *matches* — a payment that *corresponds* to the deed it pays for, a punishment that *fits* the crime the way a key fits a lock. The verse is the sūrah's answer to every possible charge of injustice against the punishment just described: the boiling and the freezing, the ages and the privation, the ambush and its inhabitants — all of it is *wifāq*, *matching*, *proportionate*. The rebel is not wronged; he is *repaid*, and the repayment is exactly, precisely, what his deeds called for.

The grammatical form of the verse reinforces its meaning. Both words are in the accusative — the case of the *circumstantial complement* — so that the phrase stands as a kind of divine *footnote* to everything that came before: the ambush, the ages, the denied drink, the boiling liquid — all of it is glossed, in one breath, as "a fitting recompense." The sūrah pauses in the middle of its description of Hell to append this verdict, and the pause is deliberate: it is the Qur'an's way of insisting that the picture just painted is not an outburst of wrath but the *application of a law*. The commentators read the verse exactly so: it is the sūrah's own certification of the punishment's justice, placed at the center of the description so that no hearer can mistake the severity for cruelty. The severity is *proportion*; the proportion is *justice*; and the justice is the verse's whole content.

**The Root of Correspondence: How the Punishment Fits**

The root *w-f-q* deserves its full unfolding, because the sūrah's choice of it is theologically exact. The root gives *muwāfaqah* (agreement, concord), *tawfīq* (divine enabling, the making of a thing to *succeed* and correspond), and *wifāq* (matching, correspondence). The idea throughout is of *fit* — two things made to answer each other, a measure and its matching measure. The verse's *wifāq* is therefore the Qur'an's precise word for *proportional justice*: the recompense *matches* the deed, not in an arbitrary or approximate way but in the way that corresponding things correspond — the way the shadow matches the shape, the way the echo matches the sound. The commentators connect the word to the Qur'an's broader statements of the same principle, and the connection is the sūrah's own theology in summary:

> *"The recompense of an evil is an evil like unto it (mithluhā)..."* (Qur'an 42:40)

> *"...and your Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

> *"...God wrongs not His servants."* (Qur'an 3:182)

The *wifāq* is the meeting point of these verses: the recompense is *like* the deed (42:40), and the likeness is the proof that no one is *wronged* (18:49; 3:182). A punishment that matched nothing would be an injustice; a punishment that matches exactly is the very definition of justice. The sūrah's two words are thus a complete defense of the divine decree: whatever the rebel receives, he receives *because it corresponds to what he sent forward*, and the correspondence is the guarantee that the scale is true.

**Why the Verse Matters: The Justice of the Day as the Foundation of Everything**

The verse's importance extends far beyond the description of Hell, because it grounds the sūrah's entire doctrine of the Hereafter — and, with it, the moral intelligibility of the world. The Day of Division, announced in verse 17, is a day of *judgment* — and a judgment is only as meaningful as its *justice*. If the recompense were arbitrary, the Day would be a spectacle of power; if it were partial, it would be a scandal; if it were harsh beyond correspondence, it would be a cruelty. The verse's *wifāq* is the sūrah's guarantee that the Day is none of these: it is a *court*, and the court's verdicts are *fitting* — each soul repaid in exact proportion to what it earned. The commentators state the consequence plainly: the justice of the Hereafter is the *ground of the believer's hope and the denier's fear alike*. The believer trusts the Day because he trusts that no good deed will be lost and no wrong will go unrighted; the denier is warned of the Day because he is warned that no evil deed will be forgotten and no rebellion unpaid. Both trusts rest on the single word *wifāq*: the recompense is fitting, and therefore the reckoning is real.

The verse is also the sūrah's answer to a question that must press on any reader of the preceding verses: *how can this be just?* The answer is not to soften the description — the sūrah does not retreat from the boiling and the freezing — but to *ground* it: the punishment is fitting because the deeds were fitting of it. The rebel's life was a life of *ṭughyān* — of excess, denial, and heedlessness — and the *aḥqāb* of Hell are the *fit* recompense of a standing rebellion; the *ḥamīm* and *ghassāq* are the *fit* recompense of a life lived at the extremes. The proportion is not between the *time* of the sin and the *time* of the punishment, as the previous commentaries noted, but between the *nature* of the sin and the *nature* of the punishment: the rebellion was against the Infinite, and the punishment's infinity is its *fit*. The verse's two words thus do what no long argument could: they locate the justice of Hell not in God's wrath but in the *deed's own shape*, and they leave the hearer with the unanswerable conclusion that the rebel's punishment is the rebel's own life, returned to him in its true form.

**The Verse as a Hinge: From the Dark Half to the Bright**

Structurally, the verse is the sūrah's *hinge*, and its placement is exact. It closes the description of Hell's condition — the ambush, the ages, the drinks — with the one word that makes the whole picture bearable to contemplate: *justice*. And it prepares the turn the sūrah is about to make: the same *wifāq* that justifies the punishment of the rebellious will justify the reward of the reverent, when the sūrah reaches them in verses 31–36. The commentators note this symmetry explicitly: the recompense is *fitting* in both directions — the rebel receives what matches his rebellion, and the reverent receive what matches their reverence — and the sūrah's structure embodies the symmetry by placing the word of justice exactly between the two halves. The *jazāʾan wifāqā* of verse 26 is the *scale's fulcrum*: on one side the rebel's fitting punishment, on the other the reverent's fitting reward, and the single word of correspondence holding both in balance. This is the sūrah's deepest statement of the Day's meaning: the Day of Division is a *scale*, and the scale is *true*, and the truth of the scale is the same for the saved and the lost.

The source commentary notes a further, elegant point about the word *wifāq* that the tradition preserves: it can also be read in relation to the *deeds' correspondence with themselves* — the good returning as good, the evil returning as evil, each soul meeting exactly what it sent forward. The Qur'an states this in the celebrated verse of the atom's weight:

> *"So whosoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whosoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

The *wifāq* is the principle of that verse stated as a single noun: the recompense *corresponds* — nothing added, nothing subtracted, nothing lost, nothing invented — and the correspondence is the believer's comfort and the denier's terror in equal measure.

**The Word of Justice and the Work of Hope**

Finally, the verse's practical teaching is the sūrah's most consoling and most sobering at once. For the believer, *jazāʾan wifāqā* is the guarantee that *nothing is lost*: the good done in secret, the patience borne in silence, the wrong endured without redress — all of it is *held* against the Day, and the Day will return it *fittingly*, in exact and overflowing proportion, because the God of the scale does not let a single atom's weight fall. The believer's hope is not a vague optimism but a *doctrinal certainty*: the recompense is fitting, and therefore the good will be seen. For the heedless, the same two words are the sobering truth that *nothing is forgotten*: the rebellion, the denial, the heedlessness — all of it is on the scale, and the scale is true, and the recompense will match. The verse thus leaves no reader unmoved, and its two words do the work of a whole sermon: *the scale is true.* Live now in the light of the scale — send forward what you wish to meet, withhold what you cannot bear to see returned — and the fitting recompense, when it comes, will be the *mercy* the sūrah's bright half is about to describe, rather than the *severity* its dark half has just painted.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:27

> **Truly they anticipated not a reckoning,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Root Cause of the Rebel's Ruin**

Having pronounced the punishment fitting, the sūrah now turns to the *cause* — the inner state that made the punishment deserved: *innahum kānū lā yarjūna ḥisābā* — "Truly they anticipated not a reckoning." The verse is the sūrah's diagnosis of the rebel's whole disease, and it is placed with surgical precision: after the description of the punishment (vv. 21–25) and its justification (v. 26), the sūrah asks *why* the rebellious came to such an end, and answers: because they *did not expect a reckoning*. The Arabic word is *ḥisāb* — the *account, the reckoning, the settling of the ledger* — and it is the Qur'an's central word for the judgment of deeds: the Day is the *yawm al-ḥisāb* (the Day of Reckoning, cf. 14:41; 38:16, 26, 53; 40:27), and every soul will be *taken to account* for what it did. The verse says the *ṭāghīn* "anticipated not" this — the verb *yarjūna* carrying the full sense of *expecting, hoping for, taking seriously* — and in that single negation the sūrah locates the root of their ruin.

The verb *yarjūna* deserves careful attention, because it is not the ordinary word for "believe" but the word for *hope and expectant regard*. The root *r-j-w* gives *rajāʾ* — *hope* — and the verse's *lā yarjūna* means they did not *hold the reckoning in expectation*: they did not live toward it, did not prepare for it, did not fear it or hope in it, did not let it weigh on a single decision. The commentators unfold the full range of the negation: they did not expect the reckoning, and therefore they did not *fear* it, and therefore they did not *avoid* it by taking themselves to account, and therefore they lived as though no account existed. The sūrah's earlier word for their condition was *heedlessness* — and this verse gives the heedlessness its precise content: heedlessness is the *absence of the expectation of the account*. The rebel's sin is not, at its root, any particular deed; it is the *posture* of a life lived without the reckoning in view — the life of one who spends without a ledger, acts without a consequence, and walks without a destination. All the particular sins follow from this one absence, as branches follow a root.

**The Logic of the Ledger: Why Expectation Governs Conduct**

The verse's teaching rests on a psychological principle the commentators make explicit, and it is one of the most practical insights in the sūrah: *conduct is governed by expectation.* A person's behavior is shaped, far more than by any abstract belief, by what he *expects to follow* from it. The student who expects an examination studies; the one who does not expect it does not. The merchant who expects an audit keeps honest books; the one who does not expect it lets the books drift. The traveler who expects a destination watches his road; the one who does not expect it wanders. The *reckoning* of the Hereafter is the ultimate instance of this principle: the person who *expects* the account lives in its light — weighing deeds, restraining desires, preferring the lasting to the passing — while the person who does not expect it lives in its shadow's absence — spending freely, because in his heart nothing is being recorded and nothing will be collected. The verse's diagnosis is thus a diagnosis of *human nature itself*: the ruin of the rebel is not a mystery but the predictable outcome of the removal of expectation. Remove the reckoning, and the conduct follows the removal.

The Qur'an states this principle in its full form in many places, and the commentators read them together with this verse. The most direct is the verse's own echo in Sūrah al-Qiyāmah, where the same denial of the reckoning is linked to the same ruin:

> *"Does man think that We shall not gather his bones? ... Does man think that he will be left aimless?"* (Qur'an 75:3, 36)

"Left aimless" — *sudā* — is the precise consequence of "anticipating not a reckoning": a creature who expects no account lives as though he were *left aimless*, and the aimlessness is the ruin. And the Prophet ﷺ stated the principle as the foundation of wisdom itself, in the report already cited:

> *"The wise person is the one who takes himself to account and works for what is after death; and the incapable is the one who lets his soul follow its desires and yet hopes in God."* (al-Tirmidhī; cf. Ibn Mājah)

The "wise person" of the report is the exact opposite of the *ṭāghīn* of this verse: he *takes himself to account* (ḥāsaba nafsahu) precisely *because* he expects the great reckoning (*ḥisāb*), while the incapable lets the desire run and the expectation lapse. The sūrah's verse and the Prophet's maxim are one teaching: the expectation of the account is the root of wisdom, and its absence is the root of ruin.

**The Double Meaning of Ḥisāb: The Account and the Accounting**

The word *ḥisāb* also carries a double sense that the commentators preserve, and it deepens the verse's teaching. In Arabic, *ḥisāb* means both the *reckoning* (the settling of accounts on the Day) and *sufficiency* (as in "a sufficient amount" — the same root gives *ḥasb*, "enough"). The two senses meet in the Qur'an's use of the word for the Day: the reckoning is the day when every soul is *settled* — given exactly what suffices, exactly what it earned, nothing more and nothing less. The sūrah's own verse 36 will use a related form (*ḥisāban*) for the believer's reward — "a gift abounding" or "a recompense sufficient" — and the commentators note the deliberate echo: the rebel anticipated no *ḥisāb* (no reckoning), and the reverent receive a *ḥisāb* (a full, sufficient, abounding recompense) from their Lord. The same root that the rebel dismissed becomes the believer's fullness; the account the heedless ignored becomes the sufficiency the reverent inherit. The sūrah's word-play is its theology: the reckoning is not a threat but a *scale*, and the scale's verdict is either the rebel's ruin or the reverent's fullness, according to whether the account was *expected* and *prepared for*.

This double meaning also illuminates the verse's placement. The sūrah has just said the punishment is *fitting* (v. 26); it now says the cause was the *unexpected account* (v. 27); and it will say, in the next verse, that they *denied the signs* (v. 28). The sequence is a chain of cause and effect: because they anticipated no reckoning, they denied the signs; because they denied the signs, they lived heedlessly; because they lived heedlessly, they arrived at the ambush; and the ambush's punishment is the *fitting recompense* of the whole chain. The verse is thus the *first link* — the root of the entire ruin — and the sūrah's insistence on naming the root is its mercy: a disease whose root is named can be treated, and the treatment of this one is the *expectation* the verse itself is planting in its hearer. To read "they anticipated not a reckoning" is to begin, at once, to anticipate it.

**The Reckoning as Mercy: Why the Warning Is a Gift**

It is essential to see the verse's warning as *mercy*, because the sūrah's whole tone depends on it. The verse does not merely *condemn* the heedless; it *diagnoses* them — and the diagnosis is offered precisely so that the hearer might avoid the disease. The reckoning, in the Qur'an's vision, is not a terror to be fled but a *mercy to be embraced*: it is the guarantee that the world is not aimless, that deeds matter, that the oppressed will be righted and the good rewarded, that no life is *sudā* — left without meaning. The rebel's ruin is that he *rejected this mercy* — he preferred the aimlessness, because the aimlessness let him do as he pleased. The verse's warning is thus an invitation to accept the reckoning *as a gift*: to live with the account in view, not as a cowering fear but as the *clarifying light* that gives every day its weight. The believer's expectation of the reckoning is not a burden but a *freedom* — the freedom of the student who studies, the merchant who keeps honest books, the traveler who watches his road — the freedom of a life that has a direction because it has a destination.

The commentators preserve a beautiful instance of this reversal in the tradition's treatment of the word *yarjūna*. The same verb that here describes the rebel's *failure to expect* is, throughout the Qur'an, the verb of the believer's *hope*: *"those who hope for the meeting with their Lord"* (cf. 2:218; 18:110), *"they hope for a commerce that never fails"* (35:29). The believer *hopes* in the reckoning — because the reckoning is the day his hope is fulfilled — while the rebel *refuses* to expect it — because the reckoning is the day his heedlessness ends. The single verb, *yarjūna*, marks the dividing line between the two roads: the road of those who *live toward* the account, and the road of those who *live away* from it. And the sūrah, by placing the verb in its negated form here, holds up the negation as the *mirror* in which the hearer can see which road he is on.

**The Everyday Reckoning: From the Verse to the Ledger of the Self**

Finally, the verse's practical teaching issues in a discipline the tradition made central: *the taking of oneself to account before one is taken to account.* The Companion ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb (d. 23/644) stated it in words that became proverbial:

> *"Take yourselves to account before you are taken to account, and weigh your deeds before they are weighed for you."*

The discipline is the direct application of this verse: because the great reckoning is certain, the believer runs a *daily reckoning* — a review of the day's deeds, a settling of the self's small ledger, a turning back from what was sent forward wrongly and a hastening to what was left undone. The person who lives this way is the exact opposite of the *ṭāghīn*: he has *anticipated* the reckoning, and the anticipation has reorganized his life. The sūrah's verse is thus not only a diagnosis of the rebel's ruin but a *prescription* for the hearer's safety: expect the account; live toward it; take yourself to account before it comes. The heedless anticipated nothing and arrived at the ambush unprepared; the believer anticipates everything and arrives, as the sūrah's bright half will show, at the Garden — *a fitting recompense* in the other direction, the fullness of the account he spent his life expecting and preparing for.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:28

> **and denied Our signs with utter denial.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Link in the Chain of Ruin**

The verse completes the sūrah's diagnosis of the rebel with its second and crowning count: *wa-kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā kidhdhābā* — "and they denied Our signs with utter denial." The verb *kadhdhabū* is the intensive form of *kadhaba*, "to lie" — *kadhdhaba* means not merely to lie but to *call a thing a lie*, to *declare false*, to *give the lie to* — and its object here is *āyātinā*, "Our signs." The word *āyāt* is the Qur'an's term for *signs* in the fullest sense: the verses of revelation, the evidences of nature, the proofs of God's power and providence — everything, in short, that the sūrah's middle section laid before the hearer, from the cradle-earth to the entwined garden. The rebel's ruin, the verse says, was completed by this: he *gave the lie to the signs* — he met the entire order of divine evidence with *denial*. And the final word of the verse, *kidhdhābā*, is one of the Qur'an's most emphatic forms: it is a *verbal noun of intensification* — "a great denial," "an utter giving-the-lie" — so that the verse's structure is denial *intensified*: they denied, and their denial was *utter*.

The word *kidhdhāb* has generated a discussion among the reciters and commentators that is worth recording, because it shows the tradition's care with the text's every syllable. The word can be read in more than one way. Read as *kidhdhāb* — with a doubled *dhāl* and a long *ā* — it is the intensive verbal noun, "a great denying," and this is the reading that gives the sense "with utter denial." Read as *kudhdhāb* — with a short first vowel — it is a *plural*: "as deniers," meaning "they denied Our signs, being deniers." And read as *kudhdhāb* in the singular it can be a *name of emphasis*: "as does a great denier," as though denial were their profession and title. The commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī among them) record all three, and the tradition's preservation of the variants is itself a lesson: the verse's meaning is stable across them all — the denial is *total, habitual, and defining* — while the exact shading is left to the richness of the revealed text. The rebel is a *denier* — not someone who doubted once, but someone whose denial was *the form of his whole life*.

**What the Signs Are: The Full Range of What Was Denied**

The word *āyāt* is the key to the verse's scope, and the commentators unfold its full range, because the sūrah's middle section has just laid out exactly what was denied. The *āyāt* include, first, the *verses of the Qur'an* — the revelations the Prophet ﷺ brought, which the Quraysh called lies (cf. 6:33, "We know that what they say grieves you; but it is not you they deny, but the signs of God the wrongdoers reject"). They include, second, the *signs of creation* — the earth, the mountains, the pairs, the sleep, the night, the day, the heavens, the sun, the rain, the grain, the gardens — the whole tour of verses 6–16, every one of which the rebel *refused to read* as a sign. And they include, third, the *signs of the Prophet's truthfulness* — the evidences that he was sent by God, which the rebels met with rejection. The verse's "Our signs" thus gathers the entire order of divine evidence into one object, and the rebel's crime is that he *gave the lie to all of it* — not this sign or that sign, but the *whole* of what God laid before him. The commentators note the horror of this totality: the rebel stood inside a world *crowded* with signs — every field, every night, every mountain, every verse — and met the crowding with a single, standing denial. The *kidhdhāb* is the denial of the *whole* by a creature who owed the whole its recognition.

The connection to the sūrah's earlier verses is exact and deliberate. The rebel's first crime, verse 27 said, was that he *anticipated no reckoning*; his second, verse 28 says, is that he *denied the signs*. The two are cause and effect: *because* he anticipated no reckoning, he had no use for the signs — the signs are the *pointers to the reckoning*, and a man who does not expect the destination has no interest in the signposts. The sūrah's middle section was the laying out of the signposts; the rebel's denial is the *refusal to follow them*; and the ambush of verse 21 is the destination reached *without* the signposts' guidance. The chain is now complete, and the sūrah's diagnosis is total: the rebel's ruin is not a matter of bad luck or divine caprice but of *two inner facts* — no expectation, and no acceptance — and the two together are the *direction* of a life that walked away from every pointer God set along its road.

**The Psychology of Denial: How the Signs Were Refused**

The verse's deepest insight, unfolded by the commentators, is into the *psychology* of denial itself. The rebel's *kidhdhāb* was not the denial of a person who had examined the signs and found them wanting; it was the denial of a person who *needed* them to be false. The signs point to the reckoning; the reckoning would require the rebel to change his life; and the rebel, rather than change, *denied the pointers*. The Qur'an states this psychology with unsparing clarity elsewhere, and the commentators read it as the gloss on this verse:

> *"Nay, but man desires to persist in sin before him. He asks: 'When is the Day of Resurrection?'"* (Qur'an 75:5–6)

The denial is the *servant of the desire*: the rebel's wish to persist produced the denial as its justification, so that the "utter denial" of this verse is revealed as the *intellectual form* of a moral refusal. This is why the sūrah's argument against the deniers took the form it took: it did not meet their denial with syllogism (the denial was not intellectual) but with *signs* — the cascade of questions that made the denial *felt* as the absurdity it was. The sūrah understood that the rebel's problem was not his mind but his *will*, and it aimed its evidence at the will through the senses — the ground beneath, the sky above, the rain that falls — so that the denial, confronted with the undeniable, might *break* and release the denier into belief. The verse's *kidhdhābā* — "utter denial" — is thus the sūrah's naming of the *depth* of the disease it has been treating, and the naming is the final step of the diagnosis before the sentence.

There is also a specific subtlety the commentators preserve about the *object* of the denial. The verse says they denied *Our signs* — the signs *of God* — and the Qur'an elsewhere makes the precise point that the denial of the signs is, in reality, the denial of *God's* truthfulness: the rebel is not rejecting an abstract proposition but *calling God a liar*, refusing to credit what God has said. The Prophet ﷺ was told, in the verse already quoted, that it was not *him* they denied but *the signs of God* (6:33) — and the distinction consoles the Prophet while it condemns the denier: the denial's object is the *highest* possible, the speech and signs of the Lord of the worlds, and the *kidhdhāb* is the creature's verdict of falsehood against his Maker. The verse thus measures the denial by its true object, and the measurement is the sūrah's final word on the rebel's guilt: he gave the lie, utterly, to the signs of the One who made him — and the fitting recompense of verse 26 is the fitting answer to that.

**The Denial's Opposite: The Acceptance the Sūrah Seeks**

Finally, the verse's dark description is, like every dark description in this sūrah, the *obverse of an invitation*. The rebel's ruin was the denial of the signs; the believer's salvation is their *acceptance* — and the sūrah's whole middle section was the laying out of the signs precisely so that the hearer might accept them. The verse thus completes the sūrah's diagnosis by showing, in reverse, what the sūrah has been doing from the first question: it has been *offering the signs* — earth, mountains, pairs, sleep, night, day, heavens, lamp, rain, grain, garden — and the hearer's response to the offering is the dividing line of the sūrah's entire argument. The *kidhdhāb* — the utter denial — is one possible response; the sūrah's own structure is the other: the response of *acceptance*, which receives the signs as they are, follows them to their Source, and lives in their light. The believer is, in the sūrah's final accounting, simply the one who *did not deny the signs* — who looked at the cradle-earth and thanked the Maker, who watched the rain and praised the Sender, who read the verse and said *āmannā*, "we believe." The rebel's utter denial and the believer's quiet acceptance are the two halves of the Day of Division, already present in the hearer's own heart; and the sūrah's warning, naming the denial in its full ugliness, is the mercy that makes the acceptance possible while the signs still stand.

The commentators note, in closing, the verse's placement within the sūrah's sentence: verse 27 gave the *inward* cause (no expectation of the reckoning), verse 28 the *outward* act (the denial of the signs), and verse 29 — the next — will give the *record* (everything written in a book). The three verses are the rebel's *indictment*: the posture, the deed, and the documentation — and the sūrah's next verse will turn the indictment into the certainty of judgment by declaring that the documentation is complete and the account is sealed.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:29

> **And everything have We recorded in a book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Indictment Sealed by the Record**

The verse closes the rebel's indictment with the declaration that makes it inescapable: *wa-kulla shayʾin aḥṣaynāhu kitābā* — "And everything have We recorded in a book." The sentence's word order is emphatic: the object *kulla shayʾ* — "everything, every single thing" — is thrust to the front, so that the verse's first word is the *totality* of what is recorded. And the verb *aḥṣaynā* is the precise technical word for this: it is from the root *ḥ-ṣ-y*, which means to *count, enumerate, comprehend* — the same root from which comes *iḥṣāʾ*, the counting and numbering of things one by one. *Aḥṣaynāhu* means "We have enumerated it, We have comprehended it, We have counted it without omission" — and the verse's force is in the verb's *completeness*: nothing has slipped, nothing has been rounded off, nothing has been lost. The counting is total. And the result of the counting is *kitāban* — "in a book," "in writing" — the record fixed and preserved, the ledger written down, the account committed to a document that cannot be altered or erased.

The commentators read the verse at two levels, and both are essential to its meaning. The first level is the *book of deeds*: the record of every human action, written by the recording angels, which each soul will meet on the Day. The Qur'an describes this record throughout — *"And We have fastened every man's record to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a book which he will find spread open"* (17:13) — and it names the angels who write it: *"Noble recorders, who know what you do"* (82:11–12), *"there is no deed but We have it witnessed"* (cf. 43:80; 50:18). The verse's "everything have We recorded" is, at this level, the Qur'an's declaration that no deed — not one, not the smallest — has escaped the record: the rebel's denial of the signs, his expectation of no reckoning, his every act of rebellion, is *written*, and the writing is complete. The second level, also transmitted by the commentators, is the *Preserved Tablet* — *al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ* (85:22) — the primordial record in which God wrote, before creation, the full knowledge of all that would be. At this level, the verse declares that the divine knowledge comprehends everything from eternity: the record is not made up after the fact but *already complete*, because the Knower of all things has known all things from before the beginning. The two levels converge on the verse's single point: *nothing is lost to God* — neither in the writing of the angels nor in the knowledge of the Lord — and the rebel's account is sealed in a book that omits nothing.

**The Root of Counting: The Precision of the Divine Record**

The root *ḥ-ṣ-y* deserves its full unfolding, because the sūrah's choice of it is theologically exact. The root is the language of *enumeration*: to count a thing is to *know it in its measure* — its number, its weight, its exact quantity — and the Qur'an's *aḥṣaynā* says that God has *counted* everything, in this precise sense of knowing it in its measure. The commentators connect the root to the Qur'an's other statements of the same truth, and the connections form a complete doctrine of the record. In Sūrah Yā Sīn:

> *"And We record what they have sent forth and their traces; and everything have We enumerated (aḥṣaynāhu) in a clear register (imāmin mubīn)."* (Qur'an 36:12)

In Sūrah Qāf:

> *"Not a word does he utter but there is with him a watcher, ready."* (Qur'an 50:18)

And in Sūrah al-Kahf, on the Day itself:

> *"And the book will be placed, and you will see the criminals fearful of what is in it; and they will say: 'Woe to us! What is with this book, that it leaves nothing, small or great, but has enumerated it (aḥṣāhā)?' And they will find what they did, present; and your Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

That verse is the sūrah's verse made into a *scene*: the rebels, seeing their book, cry out in astonishment that it has *enumerated* everything — small and great, without omission — and the cry is their own testimony to the record's totality. The root *ḥ-ṣ-y* is the guarantee behind the cry: the counting is complete, and the completeness is the justice — because a record that omitted would be a record that wronged, and the verse's *aḥṣaynā* is the declaration that no one is wronged by omission. The rebel's deeds are all *there*, in the book, in their exact measure — and the "fitting recompense" of verse 26 is the reading out of what the book contains.

**The Book and the Deeds: Why the Record Is the Justification**

The verse's function in the sūrah's argument must be stated precisely, because it is the bridge between the diagnosis (vv. 27–28) and the sentence (v. 30). The sūrah has said that the rebel anticipated no reckoning and denied the signs; it now says that *everything is recorded*; and the next verse will say *"So taste!"* The logic is the logic of the courtroom: the crime is named (vv. 27–28), the evidence is produced (v. 29 — the complete record), and the sentence is pronounced (v. 30). The verse's "everything have We recorded" is the *evidence*: the rebel's punishment is not based on suspicion, accusation, or partial memory but on a *complete record*, written down, enumerating all. The sūrah is thus careful to establish the *evidentiary basis* of the judgment before pronouncing it — and this care is the sūrah's doctrine of divine justice in action: God does not punish on hearsay; He punishes on the *record*, and the record is complete and true. The rebel himself, the Qur'an says, will be *his own witness* — *"rather, man is a witness against himself"* (75:14) — because the book will leave him no room to deny what it contains.

The commentators add a further point about the word *kitāban* that deepens the verse. The word can be read as *"in writing"* — an adverbial — so that the verse says "We have enumerated everything, *in writing*," or as *"in a book"* — a noun — "We have enumerated everything *in a book*." Both readings are transmitted, and both serve the same purpose: the record is *fixed*. A thing that is merely remembered can be forgotten or disputed; a thing that is *written* is settled, stable, available. The book is the image of the record's *fixity*: the deeds are not floating in the air of memory but *written down*, in a document that endures, and the document will be *opened* on the Day — as the Qur'an says, *"and the book will be placed"* (18:49; 39:69; 69:19). The rebel who lived as though his deeds evaporated is told, in one word, that they were *written* — fixed, preserved, awaiting the opening.

**The Two Sides of the Record: Terror and Comfort**

The verse, like every verse in this dark half of the sūrah, is double-edged, and the commentators preserve both edges. For the rebel, the record is *terror*: the book that enumerates everything is the book that contains the denial, the heedlessness, the rebellion — the complete documentation of a wasted life, to be opened and read on the Day. The sūrah's placement of the verse in its warning to the *ṭāghīn* emphasizes this edge: the rebel's ruin is *certain* because the record of it is *complete*. But the same verse is, for the believer, the deepest *comfort*: the book that enumerates everything is also the book that contains the *good* — the sincere deed done in secret, the patience borne in silence, the charity given unobserved, the tear shed in the night — all of it *written*, none of it lost, every atom of good preserved against the Day. The Qur'an's doctrine of the record is the believer's guarantee that no good is wasted: the same *aḥṣā* that terrifies the rebel consoles the reverent, because the counting is *total in both directions*. The sūrah's own bright half — the reward of the reverent in verses 31–36 — rests on this same record: the gardens and the overflowing cups are the *reading out* of a book full of good, and the believer's hope is that his book, opened on the Day, will be the book he is glad to meet.

This double edge is the sūrah's deepest teaching about the *neutrality* of the divine record: the book is not *against* anyone; it is *accurate* for everyone. It records the evil of the evil and the good of the good with the same exactness, and the Day is the *reading* of what each person wrote into it. The verse's "everything" is thus the most just word in the sūrah: it promises the rebel that his denial is on record, and it promises the believer that his patience is on record, and it leaves both to the *reading* — which is the sūrah's final word on the matter, pronounced in the next verse: *So taste.*

**The Record and the Present: Living as One Who Is Being Written**

Finally, the verse's practical teaching is the most immediate in the sūrah, because it concerns the *present moment of reading*. "Everything have We recorded" is not only a statement about the past and the future; it is a statement about *now*: the reader's present deeds — this moment, this thought, this word — are being *written* as they occur, and the book is being filled *as the life is lived*. The Qur'an's image of the ever-present recorders — *"Noble recorders, who know what you do"* (82:11–12) — is the image of a life *lived in writing*: every day is a page, every deed a line, and the book grows with the life. The believer who internalizes this verse lives differently: he lives as one who is *being written*, and the awareness of the writing shapes the writing — the deed done *knowing* it is recorded is a deed done *carefully*, and the care is the verse's gift. The sūrah's warning to the rebel and its comfort to the believer meet here, in the present tense: *everything is recorded — including what you do with this verse.* The reader's response to "everything have We recorded" — the acceptance or the denial, the turning or the persistence — is itself a line in the book, and the book's next entry is being written as the next verse sounds: *So taste.*

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:30

> **So taste! For We shall not increase you in aught but punishment!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Pronounced**

The verse is the sūrah's *sentence*, and it is delivered in the harshest grammar the chapter has yet used: *fa-dhūqū fa-lan nazīdakum illā ʿadhābā* — "So taste! For We shall not increase you in aught but punishment!" The imperative *dhūqū* — "taste!" — is addressed directly to the rebels, and it is the same verb of direct experience that verse 24 used ("they taste therein neither coolness nor drink"): the sūrah has described the punishment in the third person, and now, in one word, it *turns* and *addresses* — the description becomes a command, the scene becomes a sentence, and the hearer is no longer watching the punishment from outside but is *spoken to* from inside it. The command is the Qur'an's characteristic form of the divine verdict — *"Taste the punishment"* recurs throughout the Qur'an's addresses to the people of the Fire (cf. 3:106; 3:181; 4:56; 6:30; 7:39; 8:35, 50; 10:52; 22:22; 32:14, 20; 34:42; 46:34) — and its force is the force of *irreversibility*: the time for warning is over, the time for the *tasting* has begun, and the tasting is *commanded* because the deeds have made it inevitable.

The second half of the verse is the harshest of all, and the commentators mark it as such: *fa-lan nazīdakum illā ʿadhābā* — "For We shall not increase you in aught but punishment." The particle *lan* is the Arabic particle of *perpetual negation into the future* — "never" — and its placement here is the verse's terror: there will *never* be any increase for them *except* punishment. No relief, no diminution, no end, no intermission — the only change their state will ever know is *more*. The source commentary preserves the tradition's own testimony to the verse's severity: according to a ḥadīth transmitted in the commentaries, this verse is *the most severe verse revealed against the people of the Fire* — because every other punishment the Qur'an describes admits, in principle, of some alleviation, while this verse *closes the door on alleviation itself*: whatever comes, it will only be more punishment. The sentence is total, and it is the sūrah's final word on the dark half of the Day.

**The Logic of "Increase in Punishment": What the Verse Negates**

The verse's precision deserves careful statement, because its horror is in exactly what it excludes. The Qur'an elsewhere describes the punishments of Hell with a certain *rhythm*: the skins are burned and *renewed* so that the tasting continues (4:56); the punishment *abates* and is then *increased* (17:97); the boiling water is poured over heads (22:19–22); the fire is *fed* with fuel of men and stones (2:24; 66:6). In all of these, the punishment is described as a *process* — and a process, however terrible, implies *change*. This verse negates the one change that could ever relieve: the change toward *less*. The only change the rebels' state will know is the change toward *more* — *nazīdakum*, "We shall increase you" — and the increase is only in punishment. The commentators unfold the full negation: no coolness will come, no drink will flow, no interval will open, no decrease will arrive — the grammar of *lan... illā* ("never... except") seals every door. The verse is the Qur'an's most absolute statement of Hell's *hopelessness*: it is the place where hope itself — the capacity to imagine relief — is extinguished, because the verse has announced that relief is not coming.

And yet the verse's severity is *justice*, not caprice, and the sūrah has prepared this conclusion meticulously. The rebel's whole life was a *refusal of limits* — the *ṭughyān* that would not accept a bound — and the punishment is the *refusal of relief*: the rebel who would accept no limit in his desire is given no limit in his torment. The "increase" the verse promises is the *fitting recompense* (v. 26) of a life that always demanded more — more pleasure, more wealth, more self — and now receives *more* of the only thing its direction could lead to. The commentators state the correspondence precisely: the rebel's desire was an *endless increase of the self's gratification*; the punishment is an *endless increase of the self's torment*; and the two increases are one reality seen from its two ends. The verse's "We shall not increase you but in punishment" is thus the mirror of the rebel's own "I shall not be satisfied but with more" — the *ṭughyān* returned to its author in its own currency.

**The Verse as the Darkest Point and Its Purpose**

The sūrah has now reached the *darkest point* of its entire forty verses, and it is important to state why the darkness is here, because the sūrah's mercy is precisely in its *placement* of the dark. The verse is not the sūrah's *last* word — the chapter will turn, in the very next verse, to the brightest of its pictures, the reward of the reverent — and the darkness of verse 30 is the *necessary ground* of the brightness to come. The sūrah's method is the method of *contrast*: it paints the rebel's fate in the deepest shadow it can command, so that the believer's fate, when it is painted, will shine against the shadow with all the force of relief. The "taste!" and the "never but more" are the sūrah's *preparation* for the "gardens and vineyards" and the "overflowing cup" — and the hearer who has felt the full weight of verse 30 will feel the full sweetness of verse 31 and the verses after. The dark half exists to make the bright half *felt*; the severity exists to make the mercy *grateful*.

This is also the verse's *warning* function, and it must be stated clearly: the sentence is pronounced *in advance*, in the text, precisely so that it need never be pronounced *in fact*. The command "taste!" is, for the hearer reading these words in the world, a *future* command — the rebel will hear it on the Day — and the sūrah's quoting of it now is the mercy of the *advance notice*: the hearer is told, before the Day, what the Day will say to the unrepentant, so that he might repent before the saying. The verse's horror is the *saving* horror: it is the Qur'an's way of making the punishment *felt in advance* — as a warning — so that the punishment's *actual* arrival might be avoided. The Prophet ﷺ's entire mission, the Qur'an says, is to *warn* before the punishment (cf. 34:46; 50:45), and this verse is the warning in its most concentrated form: the sentence is quoted now, so that the hearer might never hear it addressed to himself. The verse is thus, in the deepest sense, a *mercy* — the mercy of the surgeon who shows the patient the wound before it is incurable, so that the treatment might be accepted.

**The Most Severe Verse: The Tradition's Own Testimony**

The source commentary preserves the tradition's recognition of the verse's rank, and it deserves full statement. The commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī among them) transmit that this verse is held to be *the most severe verse against the people of the Fire* — the *ashadd mā fī al-Qurʾān ʿalā ahli al-nār* — because it removes the one thing that makes any suffering bearable: the *possibility of relief*. The Qur'an's other descriptions of Hell, terrible as they are, leave the imagination some corner of change — the skins renewed, the punishment abated and returned — but this verse *closes every corner*: *lan nazīdakum illā ʿadhābā*, never anything but more. The tradition's singling out of the verse is itself a witness to its power: fourteen centuries of readers have felt, in these five words, the absolute finality of the sentence, and the feeling is the verse's warning doing its work. The commentators pair the verse with its companions in severity — 17:97 (*"every time it abates, We shall increase for them a blazing flame"*) and 4:56 (*"as often as their skins are consumed, We shall replace them with other skins, that they may taste the punishment"*) — and the pairing shows the sūrah's verse as the *climax* of a single Qur'anic theme: the punishment's *inexhaustibility*, which is the exact recompense of the rebellion's *inexhaustibility*.

**The Turn That Follows: From the Dark to the Bright**

The verse also performs the sūrah's most important *structural* work, and it must be marked as the chapter's hinge. The command "taste!" and its perpetual increase are the *last words of the dark half*; the very next verse — *"Truly the reverent shall have a place of triumph"* (v. 31) — begins the bright half with the word *inna*, the particle of emphasis and confirmation, the same word that opened the description of the Day (v. 17) and the ambush (v. 21). The sūrah's three *innas* — verse 17 (the Day), verse 21 (Hell), verse 31 (the reward) — are the three pillars of its final movement, and verse 30 stands at the foot of the third pillar, having sealed the second. The hearer who has followed the sūrah to this point has been taken through the full circuit of the warning — the question, the signs, the Day, the ambush, the drink, the record, the sentence — and now stands at the exact center of the chapter's mercy, where the "never but more punishment" gives way, in a single breath, to the "place of triumph." The sūrah's message could not be clearer: *the two ends are both real, both prepared, both announced — and the announcement of the dark one is made only so that its hearer might choose the bright.* The next verse turns the page, and the sūrah's face brightens into the reward of the reverent — the second half of the Day of Division, and the destination of those who *anticipated the reckoning* and *accepted the signs*.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:31

> **Truly the reverent shall have a place of triumph,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn to the Bright Half**

The verse is the sūrah's great turning point, and it opens the bright half of the chapter with the same emphatic particle that opened its final movement: *inna li-al-muttaqīna mafāzā* — "Truly, for the reverent, there is a place of triumph." After the darkness of the ambush, the ages, the drinks of extremes, and the sentence of "taste!" — the sūrah now turns, in a single breath, to the other half of the Day of Division, and the turn is itself the chapter's mercy made structural: the same Day that holds the rebel's ruin holds the reverent's reward, and the sūrah will not let its hearer rest in the dark half without showing him the bright. The word *inna* — "truly, verily" — is the particle of *emphasis and confirmation*, and its repetition here (after verse 17 and verse 21) marks this as the third of the sūrah's three great declarations: the Day is appointed (v. 17), Hell is an ambush (v. 21), and now — *for the reverent, a place of triumph* (v. 31). The three declarations are the sūrah's whole message in outline: the event, the warning, and the promise.

The verse's two key words each carry a full doctrine, and they must be unfolded one at a time. The first is *al-muttaqīn* — "the reverent," "the God-fearing," "the God-conscious." The word is the active participle of the eighth form of the root *w-q-y*, which means to *protect, guard, shield* — the root of *wiqāyah* (protection) and *taqwā* (God-consciousness, reverence). A *muttaqī* is, literally, *one who has made himself a shield* — one who has placed, between himself and what he fears, a protective barrier — and the word's Qur'anic meaning is precise: the *muttaqī* is the one who shields himself *from God's displeasure* by *obeying God's commands* — who takes the obedience as the shield, the piety as the protection, the remembrance of God as the guard. The word is one of the Qur'an's most important, and the sūrah's use of it here — after the description of the *ṭāghīn*, the rebels who refused all bounds — is the deliberate *opposition* of the two types: the rebel *overflowed* the bounds, and the reverent *guarded* them; the rebel made himself *unshielded*, and the reverent made himself *shielded*; and the two stand at the two ends of the Day. The *muttaqīn* are the *other* throng — the ones the Day will divide *toward* the reward.

**The Place of Triumph: The Word Mafāz and Its Full Meaning**

The second key word is *mafāz* — "a place of triumph" — and it is built on the root *f-w-z*, which means to *win, succeed, attain the goal, escape to safety*. A *mafāz* is a *place of winning* — the destination of the successful, the refuge of the escaped, the goal attained. The root is the Qur'an's word for the believer's ultimate success: *al-fawz al-ʿaẓīm* — "the great triumph" — is the Qur'an's repeated name for the attainment of Paradise (cf. 3:185; 4:13; 5:119; 9:89; 33:71; 37:60; 44:57; 45:30; 48:5; 57:12; 61:12; 85:11), and the *mafāz* of this verse is the spatial form of the same word: the *place* of the triumph, the *destination* of the successful. The commentators unfold the word's double sense, because the root carries both: the *muttaqīn* attain *success* (the winning of the goal) and *escape* (the deliverance from the danger) — and both senses are alive in the verse. The reverent are those who *won* — who attained, after the race of life, the prize they strove for — and those who *escaped* — who passed through the world's dangers and came out, by God's mercy, into safety. The *mafāz* is the *place* where both are realized: the winning and the safety, the attainment and the refuge, joined in the single word for the Garden.

The contrast with the previous verses is exact and deliberate, and the commentators mark it. The rebel's destination was the *mirṣād* — the ambush, the trap that waited at the road's end. The reverent's destination is the *mafāz* — the triumph, the refuge that awaited at *their* road's end. The two words are the two ends of the sūrah's two roads: the ambush for the one who walked heedless, the triumph for the one who walked reverent — and the sūrah's placement of the two destinations side by side is its whole doctrine of the Day. The Day of Division is the day the two roads *arrive*: the road of *ṭughyān* arrives at the ambush, and the road of *taqwā* arrives at the *mafāz* — and the division of the two arrivals is the Day's name made visible. The verse's "place of triumph" is thus not a vague promise but a *precise opposite*: the exact inverse of the "ambush" twelve verses earlier, and the sūrah's hearer is being shown, in the pairing, the two doors between which every life is choosing.

**Who the Muttaqīn Are: The Profile of Reverence**

The word *muttaqīn* is the sūrah's name for the saved, and the commentators fill in its profile from the Qur'an's fuller descriptions, so that the promise is not abstract but a *recognizable way of life*. The *muttaqīn*, the Qur'an says, are those who *"believe in the unseen, and establish the prayer, and spend of what We have provided them"* (2:3) — the opening of Sūrah al-Baqarah, where the word is defined in its fullest sense: belief, worship, and charity as the three pillars of reverence. They are those who *"suppress anger and pardon people"* (3:134), who *"spend in ease and in hardship"* (3:134), who *"remember God much"* (33:35), who *"fear their Lord in secret"* (21:49; 35:18; 67:12). The *taqwā* the word names is not a single virtue but the *comprehensive orientation* of a life lived under God's watchfulness — the shield held up against every occasion of disobedience, the bounds guarded at every point, the reckoning of verse 27 *anticipated* rather than dismissed. The sūrah's own earlier verses define the *muttaqīn* by contrast with the rebels: where the rebels *anticipated no reckoning* (v. 27), the reverent *anticipated* it; where the rebels *denied the signs* (v. 28), the reverent *accepted* them. The profile is thus the mirror-image of the ruin: the saved are simply those who did, in the world, what the sūrah's middle section was urging — they *read the signs*, *expected the account*, and *lived shielded*.

And the commentators are careful about the *door* of this word, because the Qur'an's mercy is in it. *Taqwā* is not a rank reserved for saints; it is the *commanded minimum* of every believer — the Qur'an addresses *all* believers as *"O you who believe"* and calls them to *taqwā* (cf. 3:102; 59:18), and the *muttaqīn* of this verse are the ordinary faithful who kept the bounds as best they could, fell and rose, repented and returned. The word's promise is thus *broad*: the "place of triumph" is not for a rare elite but for *every shielded soul* — every person who took God's limits seriously and lived within them, in fear and hope. The sūrah's hearer is not excluded from the *mafāz* by the verse's word; he is *invited* into it, because the *taqwā* it names is within the reach of every heart that turns. The bright half of the sūrah is not a picture of distant saints but of *the believer himself*, if he will be what the word describes.

**The Triumph and Its Conditions: Why the Reward Is Not Cheap**

The verse also teaches, by the very precision of its word, that the triumph is *earned* — and the earning is the verse's balance. The *mafāz* is *for* the *muttaqīn* — the triumph is *conditional* on the reverence, the refuge is *for* the shielded — and the sūrah does not let the promise float free of its condition. The Qur'an's doctrine of the reward is the same as its doctrine of the punishment: the recompense is *fitting* (v. 26) in both directions, and the *mafāz* is the fitting recompense of a life of *taqwā* just as the ambush is the fitting recompense of a life of *ṭughyān*. The triumph is not arbitrary favor; it is the *attainment* the root *f-w-z* names — the prize of the race, the goal of the striving, the harvest of the sowing — and the sūrah's earlier verses supply the sowing: the reverent *anticipated the reckoning* and *accepted the signs* and *guarded the bounds*, and the *mafāz* is what such a life *wins*. The verse thus holds together the two truths that must never be separated: the reward is *God's gift* (the sūrah will say so explicitly in verse 36 — "a recompense from your Lord, a gift abounding") and the reward is *the reverent's attainment* (the *fawz* of those who strove). The gift and the earning are one: God's mercy gives the race, and the runner's *taqwā* runs it, and the *mafāz* is the finish the runner reaches by the Giver's grace.

**The Turn That Changes the Reader**

Finally, the verse's *rhetorical* function must be felt, because it is the sūrah's pastoral genius at its height. The hearer has just been taken through the darkest passages in the chapter — the ambush, the boiling drink, the "taste," the "never but more punishment" — and the weight of the warning is on him. And then, with no transition but the single word *inna*, the sūrah *turns*, and the tone changes from sentence to *promise*, from the rebel's ruin to the reverent's reward. The turn is the sūrah's whole method made audible: the warning is the *shadow* that makes the promise *shine*, and the promise is the *light* the warning was preparing. The hearer who has been fearing the ambush is now given the *mafāz*; the one who has been warned *away* from the road of heedlessness is now drawn *toward* the road of reverence; and the sūrah's two halves, dark and bright, do together what neither could alone — they *move* the hearer, by fear and hope, toward the choice the chapter exists to produce. The *muttaqīn* of verse 31 are not a distant third party; they are the *address* of the verse — the "for whom" of the triumph — and the hearer is being asked, by the very grammar of the promise, to *be among them*. The next verses will describe, in the sūrah's most beautiful language, what the place of triumph contains — the gardens and the vineyards, the companions and the cups — and the description is the sūrah's final invitation: *this is what is prepared; be of its people.*

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:32

> **gardens and vineyards,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Sight of the Place of Triumph**

The verse opens the description of the *mafāz* with its first contents: *ḥadāʾiqa wa-aʿnābā* — "gardens and vineyards." The word *ḥadāʾiq* is the plural of *ḥadīqah*, a *garden* — and it is not the ordinary word for garden but a specific and beautiful one: *ḥadīqah* is from the root *ḥ-d-q*, which means to *surround, enclose* — the root of *ḥadaqah* (the pupil of the eye, so called because it is surrounded by the white) — and a *ḥadīqah* is a *walled, enclosed garden*: a garden hedged and bounded, protected from the outside, a private paradise within its own borders. The word's root-meaning is the key to its use here: the Gardens of the reward are *enclosed* — surrounded, sheltered, set apart — and the enclosure is their peace: no harm enters, no intruder comes, no outside world breaks in. The commentators note the contrast with the world's gardens, which are open to every wind and every thief; the Gardens of the *mafāz* are *ḥadāʾiq* — enclosed, protected, inviolable — and the enclosure is itself part of the reward.

The second word, *aʿnāb* — "vineyards," the plural of *ʿinab* (grapes) — adds the specific image of the *vine*: the grapevines that, in the Arabian imagination and throughout the Qur'an's own descriptions, were the very emblem of paradise. The vine is the most generous of plants — it climbs, it shades, it fruits in clusters, and its fruit is the sweetest the earth knows — and the Qur'an uses the vineyard repeatedly as the image of the Garden's plenty (cf. 2:266; 13:4; 18:32; 36:34; 80:28). The pairing of *gardens and vineyards* is thus the sūrah's *summary of abundance*: the enclosed garden (the general) and the vine (the specific), the shade and the fruit, the shelter and the sweetness — the two together naming everything a parched, weary, world-traveled soul most longs for. The verse is the first *sight* of the reward, and it is a sight of *cool green plenty* — the exact opposite of the rebel's boiling drink and frozen murk of verses 24–25, the exact fulfillment of the coolness and drink that were there denied.

**The Garden and the World: What the Reward Reverses**

The verse's deepest meaning lies in its *reversal* of the world's conditions, and the commentators unfold it through the sūrah's own imagery. The world, as the middle section described it, is a place of *labor*: the earth is a cradle, but a cradle for a toiling creature; the day is for *livelihood*, for earning by sweat; the night is a *garment* of rest, but a rest that ends in the next day's labor. The world's gardens are *earned* — the farmer plants, waters, tends, and harvests, and the garden of verse 16 (the luxuriant gardens the rain produces) is the *fruit of work*. The Gardens of the *mafāz* reverse all this: they are *given* — "gardens and vineyards" appear in the verse not as something the reverent plant but as something they *receive*; the labor is over, and the reward is the garden *without* the toil. The commentators state the reversal precisely: the world's order is *effort then enjoyment*; the Garden's order is *enjoyment without effort* — the shade without the heat, the fruit without the tending, the enclosure without the guarding. The *ḥadāʾiq wa-aʿnāb* of the reward are the *harvest* of the world's *taqwā* — the seed of the shielded life, come to full, effortless flower.

And the enclosure, the root *ḥ-d-q*, carries a further reversal the commentators mark: the world's reverent were the *guarded* — the *muttaqīn* who shielded themselves with obedience — and in the Garden they are the *guarded ones still*: their reward is itself a *shielding* — an enclosure that protects them from every harm, a wall that keeps out everything that could disturb. The *taqwā* they practiced becomes the *ḥadīqah* they inherit: the shield they held up in the world becomes the wall that surrounds them in the Garden, and the guarding they did becomes the guarding done *for* them. The root-play is the sūrah's theology in miniature: *the shield is returned as the shelter* — the one who guarded himself from God's displeasure is, at the end, enclosed in God's protection, and the enclosure is the *mafāz*'s first and deepest blessing.

**The Vine and the Cup: Looking Forward in the Sūrah**

The vineyard of this verse also looks forward within the sūrah's own sequence, and the commentators note the deliberate chain: the *aʿnāb* (vineyards) of verse 32 lead, two verses later, to the *kaʾs dihāq* (the overflowing cup) of verse 34 — the fruit of the vine and the drink of the vine, the grapes and the cup, the harvest and its consummation. The sūrah is building the Garden's picture in the order of *enjoyment*: first the enclosed garden, then the companions (v. 33), then the cup (v. 34), then the peace (v. 35), then the gift (v. 36). The vineyard is the *source* of the cup — the *aʿnāb* are the vines from which the Garden's drink flows — and the sequence garden → companions → cup is the sequence of a *feast*: the setting, the company, the drink. The sūrah's description of the reward is thus structured as a *banquet of God's giving*, and the vineyard is the banquet's *provision* — the abundance that makes the cup overflow. The hearer is being shown, in verses 32–36, not a list of rewards but a *scene* — the reverent at home in their enclosure, with their companions and their overflowing cups, in perfect peace — and the scene's every element flows from the first: the *gardens and vineyards* of the place of triumph.

The vine also carries, in the Qur'an's broader imagery, a specific *theological* point that the commentators preserve: the Qur'an explicitly contrasts the wine of this world — which intoxicates and corrupts — with the drink of the Garden — which intoxicates in no harmful way (cf. 37:45–47; 56:19). The vineyard of verse 32 is the *source* of that harmless drink: the same plant that, in the world, produces the wine that befuddles, produces in the Garden a drink that is pure delight without the befuddlement — *"crystal-white, a delight to the drinkers, wherein is no intoxication, nor will they be wearied thereby"* (37:46–47). The sūrah's *aʿnāb* are thus the emblem of the Garden's deepest principle: the pleasures of the Hereafter are the world's pleasures *perfected* — the fruit without the rot, the drink without the harm, the delight without the cost — because the Garden is the realm of *moderation's fullness*, the place where every good is complete and every excess is absent. The vineyard points, in two verses, to the overflowing cup, and the cup points to the principle the whole reward embodies: *peace lies in the mean, and the Garden is the mean made permanent.*

**The First Verse of the Reward as the Answer to the Whole Sūrah**

Finally, the verse's placement — as the *first* of the reward's six verses — gives it a climactic role that must be stated. The sūrah began with a question: "About what do they question one another?" — and the question, the commentators said, was about the Resurrection and its rewards. The middle section answered with the signs: the earth, the mountains, the pairs, the rain, the gardens of *this* world. And now the bright half answers with the *reality*: the *gardens and vineyards* of the *next* world — the earthly garden of verse 16 raised to its eternal form, the *luxuriant gardens* of the world's rain become the *enclosed gardens* of the Lord's reward. The sūrah's whole arc is complete in the pairing of verse 16 and verse 32: the world's garden was the *sign*, the Hereafter's garden is the *fulfillment*, and the hearer who read the sign aright is now shown what it pointed to. The *ḥadāʾiq wa-aʿnāb* are the answer to the sūrah's opening question — the "Great Tiding" made good — and the first breath of the reward's description is the sūrah's declaration that the tiding was *true*, and that the questioners who believed it have come, at last, to the gardens that were promised.

The verse's practical gift to the hearer is the *hope* it plants: the world's toil — the heat, the thirst, the labor of the *maʿāsh* — is not the whole of the story; the story's end is a garden, enclosed and vine-hung, where the toil is over and the reward is given. The believer who works his day's labor, guards his bounds, and waits in patience is not waiting for nothing; he is walking toward the *ḥadāʾiq wa-aʿnāb* — and the sūrah's first sight of them is the *strength* for the road. The description will continue — the companions, the cup, the peace, the gift — but the first word of the reward is already the whole promise: *gardens, and vineyards* — the enclosure and the abundance, the shelter and the sweetness, the end of the road for those who walked it reverent.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:33

> **buxom maidens of like age,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Companions of the Garden**

The third item in the description of the reward is the verse that has occasioned the most discussion across the ages, and it deserves to be treated with the care and precision the classical tradition gave it: *wa-kawāʿiba atrābā* — "and buxom maidens of like age." The word *kawāʿib* is the plural of *kāʿib*, which the lexicographers define as *a young woman whose breasts have become full and rounded* — the word names the *prime of youthful maturity*, the age at which a woman has reached her full bodily flowering and is at the beginning of her adult beauty. The word *atrāb* is the plural of *tirb*, which means *a peer, one of equal age* — the companions of a single generation, those who grew up together and share the same years. So the verse's image, read precisely, is of *young women of one age, in the full flower of maturity* — the companions of the Garden, made and kept at the peak of youthful prime. The two words together fix the meaning that the tradition has always drawn from them: the women of Paradise are *at the perfect age* — fully mature in body, and yet untouched by the decay that time brings in the world, because in the Garden there is no aging, no withering, and no end.

The classical commentators unfold the verse with a frankness and a reverence that modern readers often miss. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī all transmit the definition of *kāʿib* plainly, without embarrassment and without prurience, because the verse's imagery is part of the Qur'an's honest description of the Garden's pleasures — and the tradition treats that honesty as itself a sign of the text's confidence. The commentators note, however, the essential point that the *word* carries: the maidens are *atrāb* — "of like age" — and the "like age" is, as the tradition specifies, the age of the prime: the famous teaching, transmitted in the commentaries, that the people of Paradise — men and women alike — will be in the prime of life, commonly given as thirty-three years, the age of full strength and full beauty, at which the body is complete and no decline has begun. The verse's "of like age" is thus the Garden's answer to the world's most painful fact: *time*. In the world, every beauty fades and every prime passes; in the Garden, the prime is *held* — the companions remain at the peak age, forever, because the Garden is the place where the decay the world imposes is *abolished*. The *atrāb* are the eternal peers of a single, never-ending prime.

**The Garden's Honesty: Desire Perfected, Not Abolished**

The verse raises, for every generation, the question of how the Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden's sensual pleasures are to be understood, and the tradition's answer is a complete theology of desire that the commentary must state. The Qur'an does not, in the Garden, *abolish* human desire; it *perfects* it. The world's pleasures — beauty, love, companionship, the body's delight — are, in the Qur'an's vision, not evils to be stamped out but *gifts* to be purified and returned. The verse's frank description of the Garden's maidens is the Qur'an's declaration that the *body's* longing — the longing for beauty, for intimacy, for the companion of one's youth — is a real and legitimate part of the human creature, created by God, and therefore *included* in the reward rather than excluded from it. The tradition's teaching on the Hereafter is thus *maximalist*: nothing that God created in the human being as a good is left out of the Garden — and the verse's *kawāʿib atrāb* is the inclusion of the erotic good, purified of the world's distortions, in the eternal reward. The commentators are explicit: the Garden's pleasures are the world's pleasures *without their defects* — beauty without the fading, intimacy without the wounding, desire without the compulsion — and the verse is the Qur'an's own testimony to this perfection.

At the same time, the tradition is careful about the verse's *frame*, and the care is itself the answer to the modern question. The *kawāʿib atrāb* are not presented as the *central* reward, but as one item in a list that moves, deliberately, from the garden to the companions to the cup to the *peace* (v. 35) to the *gift from the Lord* (v. 36) — a list that *ascends*, as the commentators note, from the sensual to the spiritual, from the created pleasures to the Creator's own gift. The Qur'an's own summary of the Garden's joy places the *greatest* reward above all the rest — *"and the pleasure of God is the greatest"* (9:72) — and the tradition reads this verse in that light: the maidens are a true reward, but the *peak* of the reward is the nearness to God that the sūrah's own later verses will describe (vv. 37–38). The verse's honesty about the sensual is thus held *within* the larger honesty about the spiritual: the Garden satisfies the whole creature — body, heart, and soul — and the satisfaction of the body is neither denied nor made the whole.

**The Companions for All: The Verse's Universality**

A further point the commentators make is essential to the verse's justice and its modern reading: the reward of the Garden is described, in the Qur'an's language, in the terms of its original audience — the men of seventh-century Arabia, who were addressed first — but its *content* is universal, and the tradition is explicit that the women of the Garden receive the corresponding reward in full. The Qur'an's descriptions of the Hereafter repeatedly address *both* the believing men and the believing women — *"the believers, men and women, are allies of one another"* (9:71), and the promise of the Garden is given to *"whoever does righteous deeds, whether male or female, while believing"* (4:124; 16:97; 40:40) — and the tradition's teaching on the Garden includes, for the believing women, the reunion with their spouses and the perfection of their own beauty and youth, exactly as for the men. The commentators note that the verse's *kawāʿib* are the *male* hearer's reward described in his idiom, while the *female* believer's reward is the same perfection in her own terms — and the underlying principle is the same for both: in the Garden, every legitimate human longing is fulfilled, for every soul, in the form that completes it. The verse is thus not a narrowing of the reward to one sex but a *sample* of the reward's fullness, offered in the language of those first addressed, and the tradition's whole teaching is that the fullness is for all.

There is also a subtle teaching in the word *atrāb* that the commentators preserve and that speaks directly to the human heart's deepest wound. The "like age" of the companions is the Garden's healing of *separation*: in the world, the generations are divided by age — the young and the old, the parents and the children, the lovers separated by the years — and time is the great divider. In the Garden, the division is *abolished*: all are *atrāb* — of one age, one prime, one generation — so that the mother and the daughter, the husband and the wife, the friends of a lifetime, are gathered into a single eternal peerhood, with no age between them and no decay ahead of them. The verse's "of like age" is thus, at its deepest, the promise of *reunion without loss*: the companions of the Garden are not strangers but the *restored* — the loved ones of the world, returned at the prime, to be together forever. The *kawāʿib atrāb* are the sūrah's answer to the world's grief over time: in the Garden, time is over, and the prime is forever.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Sequence: From Sign to Fulfillment**

Finally, the verse's place in the sūrah's structure completes its meaning.
