The middle section, verse 8, had described the *pairs* — *"and We created you in pairs"* — the mates made for one another, the affection and mercy placed between them, as a *sign* of God's providence. This verse is that sign's *fulfillment*: the *zawj* of the world — the mate, the companion, the pair — is completed in the *kawāʿib atrāb* of the Garden, the companions of the eternal prime. The sūrah's arc is again the arc from sign to reality: the pairs of the world were the *foretaste*, the companions of the Garden are the *feast*, and the affection and mercy of the first pairing are consummated in the eternal companionship of the second. The hearer is thus shown, in the verse, that the deepest human longing — the longing for the beloved, for the companion, for the one of one's own age and kind — is not a longing to be *renounced* but a longing to be *fulfilled*, and the fulfillment is the Garden's. The *kawāʿib atrāb* are the sūrah's promise that love, like every other created good, has an eternal form — and that the road of the reverent ends not in the renunciation of the heart's desires but in their perfect, purified, and permanent satisfaction.

The verse, read in full and in context, is thus what the sūrah intends it to be: a *reward* — one item in the banquet of the *mafāz*, described in the frank language of the text's own culture, pointing through the body's delight to the perfection of the whole creature, and through the perfection of the creature to the Giver whose gift it all is. The next verse will pour the cup — *and an overflowing cup* — and the sequence will continue its ascent from the garden's abundance to the peace that is the Garden's own voice.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:34

> **and an overflowing cup.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cup Poured to the Brim**

The fourth item in the reward is the most economical and one of the most discussed images in the sūrah: *wa-kaʾsan dihāqā* — "and an overflowing cup." The word *kaʾs* is the Arabic for *cup, goblet* — the drinking-vessel of the feast, the cup passed among companions — and the word *dihāq* is its description, and the description has generated a discussion among the lexicographers and commentators that the commentary must record, because the word's several readings all converge on the same meaning and each adds its own shade. The root is *d-h-q*, and the commentators transmit three main interpretations of *dihāq*. The first and most common is *"completely filled"* — the cup filled to the brim, so full that it overflows, the image of *abundance without measure*. The second is *"continuously filled"* — the cup that is never empty, that is refilled as it is drained, the image of *provision without end*. The third is *"pure"* — the cup whose contents are unadulterated, the image of *delight without defect*. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī transmit all three, and the tradition's preservation of the variants is itself the commentary's point: the word *dihāq* is the Qur'an's single, compressed statement of the Garden's *abundance* — the cup that is full, and stays full, and is pure — the three senses together naming the one reality of the reward's plenty.

The cup's contents are not named in the verse, but the tradition is clear, and the source commentary preserves the early authority on the point: according to the early commentator al-Ḍaḥḥāk (d. 212/827), *every mention of the cup in the Qur'an is a reference to the wine of Paradise* — the pure drink of the Garden that the Qur'an describes in full elsewhere. The cup is thus the *kaʾs* of the Garden's wine — the drink whose nature the Qur'an specifies with a precision that reverses the world's wine entirely:

> *"Round them will be passed a cup of pure wine — white, a delight to the drinkers, wherein is no intoxication, nor will they be wearied thereby."* (Qur'an 37:45–47)

and:

> *"They will be given to drink of a pure cup, a spring in it... wherein is no headiness, nor will they be intoxicated by it."* (Qur'an 37:45–47; cf. 56:18–19)

The cup of verse 34 is thus the *anti-wine*: the world's wine intoxicates, befuddles, and ends in headache and regret; the Garden's cup is the *same delight without any of the harm* — the pleasure of the drink, the warmth of the company, the relaxation of the feast, and yet no drunkenness, no loss of self, no morning-after. The *dihāq* — the fullness — is the image of the difference: the world's cup is drunk *to the bottom and left empty*, with its cost to follow; the Garden's cup is *always full*, its delight *always pure*, its pleasure *always whole*. The sūrah's single word has compressed the entire doctrine of the Garden's perfected pleasure into the image of the brimming goblet.

**The Cup and the Contrast: From the Rebel's Drink to the Reverent's**

The verse's deepest meaning in the sūrah is its *contrast*, and the contrast is the sūrah's whole method made into a single image. The rebel's drink, in verse 25, was the *ḥamīm* and the *ghassāq* — the boiling liquid and the frozen murk, the two extremes of torment, given in place of the cool and the drink that were denied (v. 24). The reverent's drink, in verse 34, is the *kaʾs dihāq* — the overflowing cup, the pure and brimming goblet, the drink given *in abundance*. The two verses are the sūrah's two cups, and they stand at the two ends of the Day of Division: the cup of the rebel, filled with the extremes of his own excess; the cup of the reverent, filled to overflowing with the Lord's gift. The commentators mark the precision of the contrast: the rebel's drink *burns and freezes* (the extremes), the reverent's cup *delights and purifies* (the mean); the rebel's drink is *withheld then mocked* (no cool, no drink, save the tormenting liquids), the reverent's cup is *given and overflowing* (the *dihāq* that never empties). The two cups are the two *destinations* made liquid — and the sūrah's hearer, standing between verse 25 and verse 34, is being asked to *choose his cup*.

And the contrast completes the sūrah's doctrine of the *mean*. The rebel's ruin was the *excess* — the *ṭughyān* that overflowed all bounds — and his drink is the *reign of excess*: the boiling and the freezing, the extremes without moderation. The reverent's safety was the *moderation* — the *taqwā* that guarded the bounds — and the reverent's cup is the *fullness of the mean*: the overflowing cup whose overflow is *moderate* — complete, pure, and harmless. The sūrah's earlier note — *torture lies in the extremes; peace lies in the mean* — is here made visible in the two drinks: the rebel's torment is the extreme made permanent, and the reverent's delight is the mean made overflowing. The *kaʾs dihāq* is thus not a small detail of the reward but the *emblem of its whole nature*: the Garden is the place where fullness and moderation are one, where the cup overflows and nothing is harmed, where the abundance is complete and the mean is permanent.

**The Cup, the Feast, and the Company: The Scene Assembled**

The verse also completes the *scene* the sūrah's bright half has been assembling, and the commentators note the deliberate order. Verse 32 gave the *setting* — the gardens and vineyards; verse 33 the *company* — the companions of like age; verse 34 the *cup* — the overflowing goblet; and verse 35 will give the *conversation* — the peace in which no idle talk is heard. The four verses together are a *feast*: the enclosed garden as the banquet-hall, the companions as the company, the overflowing cup as the drink, and the peace as the speech. The sūrah is describing the reward in the terms of the highest human joy the Arabs knew — the feast in the garden, with the beloved and the brimming cup and the untroubled talk — and it is *elevating* the feast to its eternal form: the garden that never fades, the company that never ages, the cup that never empties, the talk that never wounds. The *kaʾs dihāq* is the feast's *centerpiece* — the cup passed among the companions in the garden — and its overflowing is the image of the feast's inexhaustibility: the joy is not rationed, not measured, not ended; it is *dihāq* — full, and refilled, and pure, forever.

The cup also carries the sūrah's *social* teaching in its very form. A cup, in the feast, is *passed* — it moves from hand to hand, and the movement is the image of *companionship*: the cup is the symbol of shared joy, the vessel of the fellowship the Garden's company enjoys. The world's revelry, the Qur'an notes, ends in the *quarrels and rancor* that wine breeds (cf. 5:91 — wine and gambling are among the causes of enmity and hatred); the Garden's cup is passed in *peace* (v. 35) — the same vessel that in the world divided companions in the Garden unites them, because its contents are pure. The *kaʾs dihāq* is thus the sūrah's image of *joy perfected socially*: the shared cup, the brimming goblet, the company at peace — the human longing for festivity, fulfilled without the world's cost. The sūrah does not forbid the feast; it *perfects* it, and the overflowing cup is the sign of the perfection.

**The Giver Behind the Cup: Looking to the Next Verses**

Finally, the verse points forward, as every item in the reward has, to the *Giver*, and the next two verses will name Him: the cup of verse 34 is *"a recompense from your Lord, a gift abounding"* (v. 36) — the overflowing goblet is *poured by the Lord of the heavens and the earth* (v. 37). The verse's *dihāq* — the fullness — is thus not self-standing; it is a *gift*, and the sūrah's bright half is building, item by item, toward the moment when the reward's source is named and the reward's recipients are brought into the presence of the Giver (vv. 37–38). The cup is a *foretaste* of the presence: the brimming goblet is the Lord's hospitality, the feast is the Lord's welcome, and the whole scene of verses 32–36 is the *banquet of the Giver's giving*, which will culminate, in the sūrah's closing movement, not in any created thing but in the nearness the next verses describe. The *kaʾs dihāq* is the last created item the sūrah names before it turns to the Creator — and its overflowing is the sign that the abundance has a Source, and the Source is the Lord.

The verse's practical gift to the hearer is the *hope* the image plants: the world's joys are *partial* — the cup that empties, the feast that ends, the company that scatters — but the road of the reverent ends in the *full* — the cup that overflows, the feast that endures, the company that remains. The believer's renunciations in the world — the forbidden drink left untouched, the forbidden feast declined, the bounds kept in patience — are not the end of joy but its *deposit*: the joy deferred is returned, overflowing, in the *kaʾs dihāq*. And the sūrah's word *dihāq* — full, refilled, pure — is the promise's own summary: the cup of the Garden is the world's cup *perfected*, and the one who drinks it will know, at last, what the world's joys were always pointing to.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:35

> **They hear therein neither idle talk nor lying—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Peace That Is the Garden's Own Voice**

The fifth verse of the reward turns from the Garden's *things* to its *atmosphere*, and the turn is the sūrah's quietest and deepest: *lā yasmaʿūna fīhā laghwan wa-lā kidhdhābā* — "They hear therein neither idle talk nor lying." After the gardens, the companions, and the overflowing cup — the *objects* of the reward — the sūrah now describes the reward's *quality*, and the quality is stated as a *negation*: in that place, two things are *absent* — *laghw* (idle talk, vain speech, the empty chatter that fills the world) and *kidhdhāb* (lying, the denial of truth, the speech that wounds and deceives). The verse's form — "neither X nor Y" — is the same grammar of *complete privation* the sūrah used for the rebel's punishment in verse 24 ("neither coolness nor drink"); but here the privation is a *blessing*, not a torment, because what is absent is not the relief but the *harm*. The Garden is the place where the two great corruptions of human speech — *emptiness* and *falsehood* — have been abolished, and the abolition is the Garden's peace made audible.

The two words must be unfolded precisely, because each names a distinct disease of the tongue, and the sūrah's pairing of them is exact. *Laghw* is from the root *l-gh-w*, which means to *err, be vain, be empty* — and *laghw* in the Qur'an is the word for *idle, vain, pointless speech*: the talk that fills time and means nothing, the chatter that passes an evening and leaves no trace, the jesting and the gossip and the noise that the world mistakes for conversation. The Qur'an uses the word throughout for the speech the believers must *avoid* and the Garden *excludes* — *"those who shun vain talk (al-laghw)"* (23:3) is among the first descriptions of the successful, and the Garden is repeatedly described as the place where *"they hear therein no vain talk (laghwan)"* (19:62; 56:25; 88:11). *Kidhdhāb*, the second word, is the *intensive* form of *kadhib* — lying — and it names the speech that is not merely empty but *false*: the deliberate distortion, the wounding word, the denial of truth, the speech that deceives and divides. The source commentary's precision is worth preserving: *laghw* is the speech of *concupiscence* — the tongue running after its own idle pleasure — and *kidhdhāb* is the speech of *deviance* — the tongue turned against the truth — and the Garden excludes *both*, so that what remains in the Garden's speech is neither emptiness nor falsehood but *peace*.

**The Negation and Its Positive: What the Garden's Speech Is**

The verse's negation is, in the Qur'an's characteristic manner, a *positive* stated in reverse, and the commentators unfold the positive the negation implies. If the Garden's inhabitants hear neither idle talk nor lying, then what *do* they hear? The Qur'an answers in its parallel passages: they hear *peace*. The verse's companions in the Qur'an all complete the negation with the same positive:

> *"They hear therein no vain talk, but only 'Peace!' (salāman salāmā)"* (Qur'an 19:62; 56:26)

The Garden's speech is *salām* — peace — and the word is both a *greeting* and a *condition*: it is the greeting the Garden's inhabitants exchange (cf. 10:10, "their greeting therein shall be 'Peace!'"; 14:23), and it is the condition the Garden *is* — the peace of a place where the tongue's two corruptions have been removed. The commentators draw the full picture: in the Garden, the speech is the *greeting of peace* — the mutual salutation of the saved, the word that the world's greetings only pointed to — and the greeting is *true*, because in the Garden there is no falsehood to corrupt it. The world's speech is a *battlefield*: the idle and the false, the wounding and the deceiving, the talk that flatters and the talk that stabs; the Garden's speech is the *peace treaty* — the one word, *salām*, spoken in truth and received in truth, forever. The verse's two negations are thus the *removal of the battlefield*; the positive they imply is the *arrival of the peace*.

This is the sūrah's deepest insight into the nature of the reward, and the commentators state it clearly: *the Garden's greatest created blessing is its speech.* The physical delights — the garden, the companions, the cup — are real and described, but the sūrah's ascent reaches its created peak in this verse, where the reward is located not in what the inhabitants *possess* but in what they *hear* — and what they hear is the *absence of every harmful word*. A person who has lived a lifetime in the world's noise — the lies, the gossip, the insults, the empty hours of talk that meant nothing — can measure, by the verse's two negations, the full sweetness of the Garden's silence-and-peace. The sūrah is saying that the human ear, no less than the human body and heart, has a longing — the longing for *true and peaceful speech* — and the Garden is the place where that longing, too, is fulfilled. The reward is not only seen and tasted but *heard*: and what is heard is peace.

**The Tongue in the World: The Verse's Moral Discipline**

The verse also carries, as every verse of this sūrah does, a *worldly* teaching, and it is one of the Qur'an's most central disciplines: the *guarding of the tongue*. The Garden excludes *laghw* and *kidhdhāb* — and the believer, who hopes for the Garden, is therefore to *exclude* them from his own speech now. The Qur'an's teaching on speech is a complete ethics of the tongue: *"speak what is best"* (17:53), *"speak the truth"* (33:70), *"shun vain talk"* (23:3), *"say to people what is good"* (2:83) — and the Prophet ﷺ made the tongue's guard the very condition of safety, in the report recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"Whosoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

The verse's two negations are the *eschatological form* of this ethics: the Garden is the place of the guarded tongue, and the guard begins now. The believer's speech in the world is the *rehearsal* of the Garden's speech: the one who shuns idle talk and falsehood here is *practicing* the atmosphere of the reward there, and the practice is the preparation. The commentators connect the verse to the Qur'an's general principle that the *deeds become the dwelling* — the Garden's inhabitants are those whose *speech* in the world already tended toward the Garden's peace — and the verse is thus both a *promise* (the reward is peace) and a *command* (the peace begins now, in the tongue). The sūrah's bright half, having described the reward's objects, here describes the reward's *ethic* — and the ethic is the hearer's own to begin.

**The Verse's Placement: The Ascent Within the Ascent**

Structurally, the verse is the *pivot* of the bright half's ascent, and the commentators mark the deliberate sequence. The reward's description has moved from the *setting* (v. 32, the gardens) through the *company* (v. 33, the companions) and the *cup* (v. 34) to the *speech* (v. 35, the peace) — and it will move, in the next verse, to the *gift itself* (v. 36, the recompense from the Lord), and then, in verses 37–38, to the *Giver* — the Lord of the heavens and the earth, before whom the Spirit and the angels stand. The ascent is from the *created* to the *Creator*, from the garden's objects to the Garden's Lord — and verse 35 is the *threshold* of the ascent's highest stage: it is the last verse describing the reward's *contents* before the sūrah turns to the reward's *Source*. The verse's negations — the absence of idle talk and lying — are thus the *clearing* before the presence: the noise of the world falls away, the tongue is stilled, and the sūrah's next verses can proceed, in the silence, to the Lord Himself. The peace the verse describes is the *preparation* for the nearness the sūrah is about to announce — and the hearer who has followed the ascent is brought, in the verses that follow, from the Garden's peace to the Peace-Giver's presence.

The verse's final gift is its *hope*: the world's speech is a *wound* — the lies that hurt, the chatter that empties, the words that divide — and the sūrah promises that the wound is *not permanent*: there is a place where the ear rests, where the tongue is true, where the greeting is peace and the peace is unbroken. The believer who has been hurt by the world's speech — and who has not been? — is given, in this verse, the promise that the hurt has an end, and that the end is the *salām* the Garden speaks. The road of the reverent, walked through the world's noise, arrives at last at the place where the noise is gone — and the next verse will say, in a single word, *why*: because all of it — the garden, the company, the cup, the peace — is *a recompense from the Lord, a gift abounding.*

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:36

> **a recompense from thy Lord, a gift abounding—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Source of the Whole Reward Named**

The verse closes the description of the reward by naming its *source*: *jazāʾan min rabbika ʿaṭāʾan ḥisābā* — "a recompense from your Lord, a gift abounding." The word *jazāʾan* — "a recompense" — deliberately echoes the *jazāʾan wifāqā* of verse 26, and the echo is the sūrah's whole theology of justice in a single repetition: the same word that described the rebel's *fitting recompense* now describes the reverent's *recompense*, and the Day of Division is thus shown to be a *single scale with two pans* — the recompense that matches the deeds, whether the deeds were rebellion or reverence. The commentators mark the echo explicitly: verse 26 and verse 36 are the two *jazāʾ*s of the sūrah, the one "fitting" and the other "from your Lord," and the pairing is the sūrah's guarantee that the scale is *one* — the same justice that pays the rebel's due pays the reverent's due, and neither payment is arbitrary. The reverent's reward is thus, first of all, *earned* — a *jazāʾ*, a recompense, the fitting return of a life of *taqwā* — and the sūrah will not let the gift-language that follows obscure the earning.

But the verse immediately adds the two words that lift the recompense above all mere earning, and they are the verse's heart: *min rabbika* — "from your Lord" — and *ʿaṭāʾan ḥisābā* — "a gift abounding." The first phrase is the sūrah's sudden, intimate turn to the second person: *rabbika*, "*your* Lord" — the verse addresses the Prophet ﷺ directly, and through him every believer, and the address is the reward's greatest element. The reward is *from the Lord* — from the Lord who is *yours*, the Lord of the covenant, the Lord who guided, sustained, and called — and the commentators note the tenderness of the grammar: the garden, the companions, the cup, the peace — all of it is *from your Lord*, as a gift handed from the Giver to the one He loves. The reward's value is not only in its contents but in its *source*: a cup from the hand of a stranger is a drink, but a cup from the hand of one's *Lord* — the Lord who is *yours* — is an honor beyond all the cup's contents. The sūrah has saved this naming for the bright half's close: the reward is *from the Lord*, and the "from" is the whole point.

**The Gift Abounding: The Word Ḥisāb and Its Depth**

The final phrase, *ʿaṭāʾan ḥisābā*, is the verse's richest, and the commentators unfold it with care because the word *ḥisāb* here carries a deliberate multiplicity. The word *ʿaṭāʾ* is the *gift* — the free giving, the bestowal that is not payment but *grace* — and its pairing with *jazāʾ* (recompense) holds the two truths in their balance: the reward is *both* earned and given, both recompense and gift, and the Qur'an's pairing of the two words is its refusal to let either truth swallow the other. The word *ḥisāb* then describes the gift, and here the sūrah plays on the very root it used in verse 27. In verse 27, *ḥisāb* was the *reckoning* — the account the rebels did not anticipate. Here, the same root describes the gift: *ḥisāban* — and the commentators transmit its three readings, all of which are alive in the verse. It means *sufficient* — the gift that is *enough*, that meets every need and leaves no lack (the root's sense of *ḥasb*, "sufficiency"). It means *abounding, ample* — the gift given in full measure, beyond stinting. And, read in relation to verse 26, it can mean *fitting, corresponding* — the gift that *matches* the deeds, the "appropriate" reward for the life of reverence. The three senses together are the verse's complete statement: the reward is *sufficient* (no need left unmet), *abounding* (no measure stinted), and *fitting* (no justice departed from) — the gift that is everything the word *ḥisāb* can hold.

The deliberate echo of verse 27 is the sūrah's subtlest stroke, and the commentators mark it: the rebels *anticipated no ḥisāb* (no reckoning), and the reverent receive *a ḥisāb* (an abounding, sufficient gift). The same word that the heedless dismissed becomes the faithful's fullness; the account the rebels refused to expect is, for the reverent, transformed into the *gift* they could not have imagined. The sūrah's word-play is its theology in miniature: the reckoning is not a terror to be fled but a *scale* to be trusted — and the one who trusted it, who lived toward it, who *anticipated* it, finds at its end not the ambush but the *abounding gift*. The *ḥisāb* of verse 27 and the *ḥisāb* of verse 36 are one reality seen from its two sides: the same divine accounting that dooms the heedless blesses the reverent, and the difference is only in *which side of the account* a life was lived on.

**The Balance of the Two Truths: Earned and Given**

The verse's theology is the sūrah's most precise, and it must be stated in full because it is the heart of the Qur'an's doctrine of reward. The verse says the reward is *jazāʾan* — a *recompense*, earned, fitting — and *ʿaṭāʾan* — a *gift*, given, abounding — and the two words are not in tension but in *complement*. The recompense preserves the *justice*: the reward is not arbitrary favor, the reverent are not saved by chance, and the scale of the Day is true — the good deed is *paid*, the reverence is *returned*. The gift preserves the *grace*: the reward is not a wage, the reverent are not self-sufficient, and the payment far *exceeds* the earning — the abounding gift is the Lord's own generosity, pouring out beyond all proportion. The tradition's summary of the balance is the famous ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which the Prophet ﷺ teaches that no one enters Paradise by his deeds alone:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds." They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: "Not even me, unless God envelops me in His mercy and bounty."* (al-Bukhārī 5673; Muslim 2816)

The verse's two words are that ḥadīth in miniature: *jazāʾan* (the deeds are recompensed) and *ʿaṭāʾan* (the entry is by mercy) — the earning honored and the grace confessed, the justice kept and the bounty crowned. The reverent's reward is thus the *exact* answer to the rebel's punishment: both are *fitting* (the scale is one), and yet the one is *more than fitting* (the grace exceeds), because the Lord's mercy, for those who sought Him, overflows the scale — while His justice, for those who fled Him, holds the scale true. The sūrah's two halves, read together, are the Qur'an's complete doctrine of the Hereafter: *justice for all, and mercy for the reverent.*

**From the Reward to the Rewarder: The Verse as the Ascent's Turn**

Structurally, the verse is the *turn* of the bright half's final ascent, and the commentators mark it as the moment the sūrah changes its object. The verses 32–35 described the reward's *contents* — the garden, the companions, the cup, the peace. This verse names the reward's *Source* — "from your Lord" — and the naming sets the sūrah's face toward its climax: the next verse (v. 37) will describe the *Lord Himself* — "the Lord of the heavens and the earth, and whatsoever is between them, the Compassionate" — and the verse after (v. 38) the *presence* — the Spirit and the angels in rows before Him. The ascent is from the *gift* to the *Giver*, and this verse is the *hinge*: it is the last word on the reward's contents and the first word on the reward's Lord. The sūrah has been building toward this all along: the whole chapter's argument — the signs, the Day, the ambush, the reward — has been pointing to the *Lord*, and now the pointing ends and the *naming* begins. The reward's greatest element is still to come — not the garden or the cup, but the *Lord from whom* they come — and the verse's "from your Lord" is the doorway through which the sūrah's final verses will pass into His presence.

The verse's practical gift to the hearer is the *hope* it plants in its full form: the believer's deeds are *not lost* (the recompense), and the believer's hopes are *not too high* (the gift abounding). The one who lives reverently lives toward a reward that is *both* the fitting return of his deeds *and* the overflowing grace of his Lord — and the two together are the answer to every fear the dark half of the sūrah raised. The sūrah has taken its hearer through the ambush and the boiling drink and the "never but more" — and now, at the bright half's close, it has given him the *counter-weight*: a recompense from his Lord, a gift abounding, a cup that overflows, a peace that endures — and, in the verses that follow, the Lord Himself, in whose presence the whole reward is *seen to be* what it always was: the gift of the Giver to those He loves.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:37

> **the Lord of the heavens and the earth, and whatsoever is between them, the Compassionate, Whom they have no power to address.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rewarder Named in His Full Majesty**

The verse is the sūrah's great *naming*, and it gathers the whole chapter's argument into a single description of God: *rabbi al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍi wa-mā baynahumā al-raḥmāni lā yamlikūna minhu khiṭābā* — "the Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them, the Compassionate, Whom they have no power to address." The verse is grammatically continuous with the one before: the recompense is *from your Lord* — and then the Lord is *named*, so that the hearer might know *who* it is that gives the gift. The naming has two parts, and each is the sūrah's summary of everything it has said. The first part is the *Lordship*: *rabbi al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍ* — "Lord of the heavens and the earth" — the same heavens and earth the sūrah's middle section laid out as signs: the earth spread as a cradle (v. 6), the heavens built as firmaments (v. 12), and *wa-mā baynahumā* — "whatsoever is between them" — everything in the span from the cradle to the roof: the mountains, the pairs, the sleep, the night, the day, the sun, the rain, the grain, the gardens. The verse's "whatsoever is between them" is the sūrah's *recapitulation* of its own middle section: all the signs it described are gathered into the single phrase, and the Lord is named as their Lord. The sūrah has spent eleven verses showing the *signs*; it now names the *Signer*, and the naming is the conclusion the signs were always driving toward.

The second part is the *Name*: *al-Raḥmān* — "the Compassionate" — and its placement here is the sūrah's most tender stroke. The Lord of the heavens and the earth, the Lord of the entire cosmos, is *al-Raḥmān* — the All-Compassionate, the Name of God's *universal mercy*, the Name that, in the Qur'an's opening (the basmalah) and throughout, stands for the mercy that encompasses all things. The commentators mark the juxtaposition: the verse names the Lord's *majesty* (the heavens and the earth) and then, immediately, His *mercy* (al-Raḥmān) — so that the hearer, confronted with the cosmic Lordship, is not left in fear but is turned toward the mercy that is the Lordship's own nature. The recompense of verse 36 — "from your Lord" — is the recompense of *this* Lord: the Lord of all, who is *the Compassionate* — and the gift abounding flows from *mercy* as its source. The sūrah has saved this Name for the climax of the bright half: the reward is given, and the Giver is *al-Raḥmān*, the Compassionate — so that the believer's hope, at the sūrah's peak, rests not on any created thing but on the mercy that is God's own Name.

**"Whom They Have No Power to Address": The Silence of the Day**

The verse's final clause is its most striking, and it has generated a careful discussion among the commentators: *lā yamlikūna minhu khiṭābā* — "they have no power to address Him" — or, more literally, "they possess not, from Him, any address." The clause describes the *silence* of the Day: before the Lord of the heavens and the earth, the creatures *have no power of speech* — no one can *address* Him, no one can *speak* to Him, no one possesses the capacity for *khiṭāb* (address, speech directed at another) — except as He permits. The commentators transmit the interpretations of the clause's subject and scope, and both are essential. On one reading, "they" refers to the *disbelievers* — the rebels of the dark half — who, on the Day, will have no power to address the Lord: their mouths will be sealed, their arguments silenced, their excuses void, as the Qur'an says in its parallel:

> *"On that Day We shall seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us..."* (Qur'an 36:65)

On another reading, "they" refers to *all creatures* — every being before the Lord on the Day — for none of them, believer or denier, possesses of himself the power to address the Lord of all: the speech itself is a *gift* that must be *permitted*, and the verse's point is the *absolute dependence* of every creature on the Lord's leave even for a single word. The commentators note that the two readings converge: the verse's deepest meaning is the *majesty* before which all speech falls silent — the Day is the place where the creature, who chattered and argued and questioned in the world, stands at last in the presence of its Lord, and the presence itself is the silence, and no one speaks except by His leave.

The clause also completes the sūrah's *rhetoric of speech*, and the completion is the sūrah's method made final. The chapter *began* with speech — the questioning of verse 1, the differing of verse 3, the chatter of the disputants who questioned one another about the Great Tiding. The middle section *answered* with questions — the cascade of "Did We not...?" that led the hearer through the signs. The bright half *described* the speech of the Garden — the peace in which no idle talk is heard (v. 35). And now, at the climax, the sūrah describes the *end of speech*: before the Lord, *no one has the power to address* — the chatter, the questioning, the differing, all of it is *over*, and the only speech that remains is the speech the Lord permits. The sūrah's whole argument has been an argument about *talk* — the human talk that evades the truth — and its conclusion is the *silencing of the talk*: the Day is the place where the evasions end, and the creature stands wordless before its Maker. The verse's "no power to address" is thus the sūrah's final answer to its own opening: the question "about what do they question one another?" is answered, at the last, by the silence of the Day — the questioners will have no more questions, and no more words, before the One they questioned.

**The Permission of Speech: The Mercy Within the Silence**

Yet the silence is not absolute, and the commentators are careful about the *exception*, because the next verse states it: *"none speaking, save one whom the Compassionate permits"* (v. 38). The verse's "no power to address" is thus the *negative half* of a statement whose positive half is the permission — and the exception is the sūrah's mercy within the majesty. The creatures have no power of themselves, but the Compassionate *permits* — and the permitted speech is the speech of the *intercessors*, those whom God allows to speak on the Day, and of the *truthful*, those who speak the word of truth. The verse's silence is therefore not the silence of annihilation but the silence of *deference*: the creatures are not struck dumb by force but *stilled* by majesty, and the stillness is the *condition* of the permission — no one speaks until the Lord allows, and the Lord allows the speech that is *true* and the intercession that is *granted*. The sūrah's doctrine of the Day's speech is thus its doctrine of the Day's *justice and mercy*: the chatter of the world is silenced, the truth is permitted, and the Compassionate's leave is the only door through which any word may pass. The believer's hope on that Day is not in his own eloquence — he has none of his own — but in the *permission* of the Compassionate, who allows the truthful to speak and the intercessors to intercede.

The commentators connect the verse to the Qur'an's fullest statement of the Day's speech, and the connection is the verse's completion:

> *"That Day, no soul shall speak save by His leave..."* (Qur'an 11:105)

The Day's speech is *governed* — every word permitted, every silence decreed — and the governance is the same divine order the sūrah's middle section described in the world: the Lord who *arranged* the signs of the world arranges the *speech* of the Day, and the arrangement is the mercy of the One who is al-Raḥmān. The verse's final clause is thus not a note of terror but of *awe* — the awe of the creature before its Lord, which is the beginning of the believer's joy: for the believer's word on the Day is the word the Compassionate permits, and the permitted word is the greeting of peace, the testimony of truth, and the intercession of the Prophet ﷺ for his community. The silence the verse describes is the *threshold* of the speech the next verse grants — and the sūrah's final movement, from the silence to the permitted word, is the chapter's last and greatest picture of the Day.

**The Lord Named, the Sūrah Completed in Principle**

The verse is, in a real sense, the sūrah's *completion* — the point to which everything before it was tending. The chapter's whole argument — the question, the signs, the Day, the ambush, the reward — has been the long preparation for this naming: the Lord of the heavens and the earth, whatsoever is between them, the Compassionate, before whom all speech fails. The sūrah's hearer, who began at the question of verse 1, has been carried through the cosmos and the Hereafter to this single point: the *naming of the Lord* — and the naming is the answer to the question, the source of the signs, the judge of the Day, the Giver of the gift. The verse's two descriptions — the Lordship and the mercy, the majesty and the silence — are the sūrah's whole theology in two breaths: *He is the Lord of all, and He is the Compassionate, and before Him all else is silent.* The remaining verses (38–40) will unfold the scene — the Spirit and the angels in rows, the True Day, the warning of the punishment nigh, and the closing cry of the disbeliever — but the sūrah's *center* is here, in the naming, and the center holds everything else in place. The hearer who has reached this verse has reached the heart of the chapter — and the heart is the Lord, named at last, in whose presence the questioners of the world will stand wordless.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:38

> **That Day the Spirit and the angels stand in rows, none speaking, save one whom the Compassionate permits and who speaks aright.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Assembly of the Unseen Made Visible**

The verse opens the sūrah's final scene, and it is the most majestic picture in the chapter: *yawma yaqūmu al-rūḥu wa-al-malāʾikatu ṣaffan* — "the Day the Spirit and the angels stand in rows." The Day of Division, whose trumpet and opened sky and moving mountains the sūrah has already described, is here shown in its *assembly*: the unseen order of creation — the Spirit and the angels — stands before its Lord in ranks, and the standing is the Day's central image. The word *ṣaffan* — "in rows, in ranks" — is the word of *ordered standing*: the rows of the prayer, the ranks of an army drawn up, the disciplined array of those who wait upon a king. The angels do not mill about or scatter; they *stand in rows* — ordered, silent, attentive — and the order of their standing is itself a sign: the Day is the place where the cosmos's unseen servants are drawn up in their ranks, and the drawing-up is the *prelude to the judgment*. The sūrah's hearer, who has known the angels only as the unseen recorders of his deeds (cf. 82:10–12; 86:4), is now shown them *standing* — the hidden made visible, the recorders become the assembly, the unseen order of the world arrayed before its Lord.

The identity of *al-Rūḥ* — "the Spirit" — has generated one of the sūrah's richest discussions, and the commentators transmit several readings, all of which the text will bear. The first, adopted by Ibn Kathīr and transmitted widely, is that the Spirit is the *Archangel Gabriel* — the angel of revelation, the greatest of the angels, who brought the Qur'an down to the Prophet ﷺ; the Qur'an itself calls him *"the Trustworthy Spirit"* (al-rūḥ al-amīn, 26:193) and *"the Holy Spirit"* (rūḥ al-qudus, 2:87, 253; 16:102), and the commentators read the verse as placing Gabriel — alone among the angels, named and set apart — at the head of the ranks. The second reading, also transmitted, is that the Spirit is a *creature greater than the angels* — a being of God's own making, distinct from Gabriel, whose nature the Qur'an deliberately leaves veiled; when the disbelievers asked about the Spirit, the answer was the famous verse:

> *"They ask you about the Spirit. Say: 'The Spirit is from the command of my Lord, and you have not been given of knowledge save a little.'"* (Qur'an 17:85)

The third reading, associated with some early authorities, takes the Spirit as the *souls of mankind* — the human spirits, standing on the Day with the angels in the great assembly. The sūrah's own text, and the tradition's care, hold these readings together: the Spirit is *some* great being of the unseen order — whether Gabriel or a creature beyond the angels or the gathered souls — and the verse's point is not the identification but the *standing*: the Spirit and the angels, the noblest of creation's unseen servants, stand in rows before the Lord, and the standing includes, in the sūrah's closing verse, the human creature whose account is being read.

**The Rows and the Silence: The Posture of the Day**

The verse's second half completes the picture with the Day's two great facts: *lā yatakallamūna illā man adhina lahu al-raḥmānu wa-qāla ṣawābā* — "none speaking, save one whom the Compassionate permits, and who speaks aright." The first fact is the *silence*: the Spirit and the angels — the beings of the unseen, the greatest of creation's servants — do not speak. The ranks stand wordless; the assembly is held in a silence that the world's noise never knew; and the silence is the *awe* of the presence — no creature, not even the greatest, opens its mouth before the Lord except by His leave. The second fact is the *permission*: *illā man adhina lahu al-raḥmān* — "save one whom the Compassionate permits" — and the permission is the sūrah's mercy within the majesty: the silence is not absolute but *governed*, and the Compassionate allows speech to whom He wills. The third fact is the *condition*: *wa-qāla ṣawābā* — "and who speaks aright" — the permitted speech is the *true* speech: *ṣawāb* is the Arabic for *rightness, correctness, hitting the mark* — and the verse's grammar makes the permission and the rightness one: the one who is permitted speaks *only* what is right, because the permission itself is the guarantee of the truth. On the Day, no one may speak falsely; the permitted word is the true word, and the true word alone is spoken.

The commentators unfold the permission's *content*, and the sūrah's own structure supplies it. The permitted speech, they transmit, is first the speech of *intercession*: the one whom the Compassionate permits may intercede — and the Qur'an's doctrine of intercession is precise: *"Who is there that may intercede with Him save by His leave?"* (2:255) — the intercessors are those whom God allows, and they speak *aright* about those for whom they intercede, because the intercession of the Day is the intercession of the *truthful*. The tradition names the Prophet ﷺ first among the intercessors — his *shafāʿah* for his community is the Day's great permitted speech — and the commentators note that the verse's "one whom the Compassionate permits" is, in its first instance, the Prophet ﷺ, given leave to intercede, and after him the angels and the prophets and the truthful, each speaking only what the Compassionate allows. The second content of the permitted speech is the *testimony of truth* — the word *ṣawābā* itself: some of the commentators read the verse's second clause as naming those who *say* the truth — *"there is no god but God"* — the word of pure *ṣawāb* that the Day permits and the world suppressed. Both contents converge: the Day's speech is the speech of *truth and intercession* — the two things the world's chatter lacked — and the Compassionate's leave is the door through which both pass.

**The Day of the Ranks and the Day of the World: The Contrast Completed**

The verse's deepest meaning in the sūrah is its *contrast* with the chapter's opening, and the contrast is the sūrah's whole argument made into a single picture. The sūrah *began* with speech — the questioning of verse 1, the differing of verse 3, the human chatter that circled the truth without reaching it. The verse now *ends* the theme with the opposite picture: the Spirit and the angels in rows, *silent* — and the only speech the permitted, true word. The two pictures are the two poles of the sūrah's teaching about the tongue: in the world, speech is *unlicensed* — everyone chatters, questions, differs, denies; on the Day, speech is *licensed* — no one speaks but by the Compassionate's leave, and the leave is only for the true. The sūrah's hearer is thus taught the *weight* of speech by seeing its two conditions: the world's license, which is really license to err, and the Day's permission, which is the only speech worth the name. And the teaching lands with the force of the contrast: the questioners of verse 1, who used speech to evade the truth, will stand on the Day in the same rows as the angels — and will have *no* speech, because they used the world's license to waste the tongue's gift, while the angels, who guarded their speech, are granted the permitted word. The verse is the sūrah's final judgment on the *talk* with which it began.

The commentators also mark the verse's *inclusion* of the human creature in the assembly, and the inclusion is the sūrah's climax of hope. The rows of the Day are not the angels' alone: the verse's scene — the Spirit and the angels standing — is the scene the sūrah's closing verse will complete, when the human being stands before the same Lord and beholds what his hands sent forth (v. 40). The believer, in the rows of the Day, is granted the same thing the verse describes for the angels: the *permitted word* — the greeting of peace, the testimony of truth, the intercession of the Prophet ﷺ on his behalf. The believer's hope on the Day is precisely this: that he will be among those for whom the Compassionate permits speech — the speech of the truthful, the intercession of the Prophet ﷺ — and the verse's "save one whom the Compassionate permits" is the door of that hope left standing open. The sūrah's final scene, read in full, is thus not a picture of exclusion but of *ordered mercy*: the ranks stand, the silence holds, the Compassionate permits, and the permitted speak aright — and the believer's place in the scene is the place of the one for whom the permission is granted.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Doctrine of the Unseen Made Complete**

Finally, the verse completes the sūrah's teaching about the *unseen* — the realm the sūrah's middle section implied and its final movement has been unveiling. The middle section showed the *seen* signs — earth, sky, rain, grain — the pointers to the unseen Lord. The final movement has been opening the *unseen* itself — the trumpet, the opened sky, the ambush, the reward — and now, in this verse, the unseen's *inhabitants*: the Spirit and the angels, the beings the world never saw, standing in their ranks. The sūrah's arc is thus the arc of *revelation*: from the seen to the unseen, from the signs to the Signer, from the world's surface to the world's depths — and the verse is the deepest point of the unveiling: the assembly of the unseen before the Lord, in silence and in rows, awaiting the judgment. The hearer who has followed the sūrah has been led, step by step, from the ground beneath his feet to the ranks of the angels above — and the journey's end is the *presence* the next verses describe: the True Day, the path of return, and the single human being before his Lord. The Spirit and the angels stand in rows; the judgment is about to begin; and the sūrah turns, in its final two verses, to the *path* and the *warning* that close the chapter.

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:39

> **That is the True Day. So let whosoever will, take a path of return unto his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day Named True, and the Door Left Open**

The verse is the sūrah's *summons*, and it stands at the climax of the chapter's final movement: *dhālika al-yawmu al-ḥaqqu fa-man shāʾa ittakhadha ilā rabbihi maʾābā* — "That is the True Day. So let whosoever will, take a path of return unto his Lord." The first clause is the sūrah's verdict on everything it has described: *al-yawm al-ḥaqq* — "the True Day" — the Day that is *al-ḥaqq*, the Real, the Certain, the thing that cannot be doubted. The word *ḥaqq* is the Qur'an's word for *truth in its fullest sense* — the real as opposed to the illusory, the certain as opposed to the doubtful, the established as opposed to the claimed — and the verse's naming of the Day as *al-ḥaqq* is the sūrah's answer to every doubt it opened with: the questioners of verse 1, the differers of verse 3, the deniers who "anticipated not a reckoning" — all of them are answered by this single word. The Day is *True* — not probable, not hopeful, not threatened, but *True* — and the sūrah's whole argument, from the signs of the middle section to the scenes of the final movement, has been the demonstration of the Day's *truth*. The commentators connect the naming to the Qur'an's repeated declarations of the Day's certainty:

> *"...the Day about which there is no doubt..."* (Qur'an 3:9, 25; 4:87; 6:12; 42:7; 45:26)

The Day is *ḥaqq* because it is the day of the *Ḥaqq* — the day of God, the Real — and the one who has followed the sūrah to this point has been shown, in the signs and the scenes, enough of the truth to *know* it.

**The Choice Offered: The Grammar of the Open Door**

The verse's second clause is the sūrah's most important turn, and its grammar must be examined precisely, because the whole mercy of the chapter is in it: *fa-man shāʾa ittakhadha ilā rabbihi maʾābā* — "So let whosoever will, take a path of return unto his Lord." The particle *fa-* — "so, therefore" — draws the conclusion from the Day's truth: *because* the Day is true, *therefore* there is something to do about it — and the something is a *choice*. The verb *shāʾa* — "whosoever *wills*" — places the matter in the hearer's own hand: the Day is true, and the response to the truth is *voluntary* — no one is compelled, and the choice is offered. The commentators mark the grammar's mercy: the verse does not say "let the compelled take a path" or "let the doomed take a path"; it says *whosoever wills* — and the "whosoever" is the *openness* of the invitation: the door of return is open to *every* willer, whoever he is, whatever he has been, from the first hearer in Makkah to the last reader of the sūrah. The word *maʾāb* — "a path of return, a place of return" — is the same word the sūrah used, with bitter irony, for the rebel's destination in verse 22: there, Hell was the *maʾāb* of the *ṭāghīn* — the "return" they earned. Here, the *maʾāb* is offered as a *choice*: the hearer may *take* a path of return to his Lord — the good return, the return of the reverent, the *maʾāb* that verse 31 promised and verse 36 described. The same word that sealed the rebel's doom is now *offered* as the believer's choice, and the sūrah's mercy is in the offering: the *maʾāb* is not only a destination but a *path* — *ittakhadha ilā rabbihi maʾābā*, "take, unto his Lord, a return-path" — and a path is something to be *walked* — now, from this moment, by choice.

The commentators are careful about the verb *ittakhadha* — "take" — because it carries the verse's whole ethics. The path of return is not *given* without the taking; it is *taken* — the Arabic is the verb of *acquiring, adopting, making for oneself*: *ittakhadha* is what one does with a thing one chooses and keeps. The verse thus teaches the *two-sidedness* of salvation the sūrah has held throughout: the Day is true (God's side — the reality), and the path must be *taken* (man's side — the response). The truth is established, and the choice is offered, and the one who *wills* and *takes* — who adopts the path and keeps to it — is the one who reaches the *maʾāb*. The grammar joins the two truths the sūrah has balanced from the first: *God's* certainty and *man's* freedom, the Day's reality and the will's choice, the gift and the taking. The verse is the sūrah's doctrine of *free will* in a single sentence: the Day is true — and because it is true, the will is *invited*, not compelled; the path is *offered*, not forced; and the one who takes it takes it *by his own willing* — with all the dignity and all the responsibility that willing carries.

**The Path of Return: What the Taking Means**

The commentators fill in the content of the "path of return," and their answer is the sūrah's whole ethics in summary: the path is *belief and good deeds* — the two things the sūrah's hearer has been taught from the first question. The path of return to the Lord is the path of *accepting the signs* (the opposite of verse 28's denial), *anticipating the reckoning* (the opposite of verse 27's heedlessness), and *guarding the bounds* (the *taqwā* of verse 31) — and the path is *walked* by the deeds the sūrah has been implicitly commanding: the recognition of the signs, the gratitude for the provisions, the preparation for the Day. The Qur'an states the path's content plainly in its parallels: *"So hasten to forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden... prepared for the reverent"* (3:133), *"and seek the way to your Lord"* (cf. 5:35; 2:148), *"whosoever hopes for the meeting with his Lord, let him work righteous deeds"* (18:110). The "path of return" is thus the *road of the reverent* the sūrah has been describing all along — and the verse's "take" is the *decision to walk it*: the moment the hearer stops questioning and differing, accepts the signs, expects the account, and turns his face toward his Lord. The *maʾāb* is the end of that road, and the sūrah's offer is that the road's first step is *now*.

And the path's *direction* is the verse's most beautiful word: *ilā rabbihi* — "unto *his Lord*." The return is not to an abstract reward or a distant judge but to *the Lord* — the Lord who is *his*, the Lord of the covenant, the same *rabbika* ("your Lord") of verse 36 whose gift is abounding. The commentators note the tenderness of the pronoun: the path of return is a path *home* — the creature returning to the One who made him, the servant to the Master who is also the Compassionate (v. 37), the traveler to the Lord who is *his*. The sūrah's whole argument has been building to this word: the signs point to the Lord, the Day is the Lord's, the reward is from the Lord — and the *return* is to the Lord, as the final home of the creature He made. The *maʾāb ilā rabbihi* is thus the sūrah's answer to the human heart's deepest question — *where am I going?* — and the answer is the most consoling the Qur'an gives: *you are going home, to your Lord, if you will take the path.*

**The Choice in the Light of the Day: Why the Verse Is Placed Here**

The verse's placement in the sūrah is its final teaching, and the commentators mark it as the chapter's *mercy at the threshold*. The sūrah has now described *everything* — the Day (v. 17), the ambush and its torments (vv. 21–30), the reward and its gifts (vv. 31–36), the Lord and the assembly (vv. 37–38) — and it has laid the whole reality before the hearer. And then, at the end of the description, before the final warning of verse 40, it *stops* and *offers*: the Day is true, and the door is open, and *whosoever wills* may take the path. The placement is the sūrah's rhetoric at its height: the choice is offered *after* the reality is shown and *before* the warning is sealed — so that the hearer, with the full picture in his mind and the final warning still to come, stands at the exact point of decision. The verse is the sūrah's *invitation*, issued at the last possible moment before the closing: the Day is true, the path is open, and the one word the sūrah still awaits is the hearer's *willing*. The chapter has argued, shown, warned, and promised; it has not yet *compelled* — and it will not compel, because the *fa-man shāʾa* ("whosoever wills") is the sūrah's final word on the matter: the choice is the hearer's, and the sūrah's whole work has been to make the choice *possible* and *informed*.

The verse thus teaches the Qur'an's doctrine of *human responsibility* in its clearest form: the truth is *established* by God, and the response is *chosen* by man — and the two together are the meaning of the moral life. The Day's truth does not remove the will's freedom; it *grounds* it, because a choice matters only if its consequences are real. The sūrah's offer — "whosoever wills, let him take a path of return" — is the Qur'an's standing address to every human being: the reality is known, the path is open, the end is certain, and the only remaining variable is *the will*. The hearer who has followed the sūrah to this verse has been brought to the threshold of the choice — and the chapter's final verse, which follows immediately, is the *warning* that closes the offer: the punishment is nigh, and the one who does not take the path will behold, at the end, what his hands sent forth.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Gospel**

Finally, the verse must be heard in its full register, because it is the sūrah's *good news* as much as its summons. "That is the True Day" is the Qur'an's *gospel* — the announcement that the world is not aimless, that the wrongs will be righted, that the deeds are recorded, that the reward is real — and "let whosoever will take a path of return unto his Lord" is the gospel's *invitation* — the door opened, the path offered, the home promised. The sūrah's dark half was the warning; its bright half the promise; and this verse is the *call* that turns the warning and the promise into a *choice*. The hearer is not left with information about the Day but with a *decision* about himself — and the decision, the sūrah insists, is his to make: *whosoever wills.* The chapter that began with the questioners' chatter ends with the individual's choice; the sūrah of the Great Tiding closes with the tiding's demand; and the demand, heard aright, is the Qur'an's tenderest word — *the Day is true, and the door is open, and your Lord is the home at the end of the road. Take the path.*

---
## Sūrah al-Nabaʾ 78:40

> **Truly We have warned you of a punishment nigh, on a day when a man beholds what his hands have sent forth, and the disbeliever says, "Oh, would that I were dust!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Verse: Warning, Reckoning, and the Cry of the Lost**

The sūrah's last verse gathers the whole chapter into a single, closing picture, and it is the most human verse in the sūrah: *innā andharnākum ʿadhāban qarīban* — "Truly We have warned you of a punishment nigh" — *yawma yanẓuru al-marʾu mā qaddamat yadāhu* — "on a day when a man beholds what his hands have sent forth" — *wa-yaqūlu al-kāfiru yā laytanī kuntu turābā* — "and the disbeliever says: 'Oh, would that I were dust!'" The verse's three clauses are the sūrah's three final movements — the *warning*, the *reckoning*, and the *regret* — and each deserves its full unfolding, because the sūrah has saved its most personal word for its last breath.

The first clause, *innā andharnākum* — "Truly We have warned you" — is the sūrah's own summary of everything it has done. The verb *andharnā* is from *nudhur* — the *warning* — and it is the same word the Qur'an uses for the Prophet's ﷺ whole mission: he is the *nadhīr*, the warner, sent to warn before the punishment (cf. 34:46; 50:45; 67:26). The verse's "We have warned you" is thus the sūrah's declaration that its work is complete: the question was posed (v. 1), the signs were laid out (vv. 6–16), the Day was described (vv. 17–20), the ambush was pictured (vv. 21–30), the reward was promised (vv. 31–36), the Lord was named (vv. 37–38), the path was offered (v. 39) — and now, in the last verse, the sūrah declares that the *warning has been delivered*. The hearer can never say he was not told: the warning is *complete*, and the completeness is the sūrah's justice — the punishment that follows will have been *preceded* by the warning, and the warned have no excuse. The word *qarīban* — "nigh" — then adds the warning's urgency: the punishment is *near* — not distant, not hypothetical, but *nigh* — and the nearness is the sūrah's final pressure on the hearer: the Day is close, the warning is now, and the time for the path of verse 39 is the present moment, before the near becomes the present.

**The Man and His Hands: The Individual Reckoning**

The second clause is the sūrah's most personal image, and it is the whole chapter's argument reduced to a single human being: *yawma yanẓuru al-marʾu mā qaddamat yadāhu* — "on a day when a man beholds what his hands have sent forth." The word *al-marʾ* — "a man, a person, a human being" — is the *singular*: after forty verses of throngs and ranks and crowds, the sūrah closes on *one* man, alone, looking at the record of his own life. The commentators mark the shift: the Day's crowds (v. 18) and the assembly of the Spirit and the angels (v. 38) are the *scene*; but the sūrah's last picture is the *individual* — because on the Day, for each person, the judgment is *personal*: each soul beholds *its own* record, and the crowd dissolves into the single, solitary confrontation of a man with his deeds. The phrase *mā qaddamat yadāhu* — "what his hands have sent forth" — is one of the Qur'an's most repeated and most significant idioms, and it carries the chapter's whole ethics: the deeds are *sent forward* — *qaddamat* is the verb of *sending ahead*, of *advancing* something to a destination — and the hands are the *instruments* of the sending. Every act a person does is a *sending forward*: the deed leaves the hand and goes ahead, into the future, to await its doer at the reckoning. The world's deeds are not *lost*; they are *sent* — and the Day is the day of their *arrival*, when the sender beholds what he sent.

The commentators read the clause at two levels, both true. The first level: the verse describes *the disbeliever* beholding his evil deeds — the denier looking at what his hands sent forward of denial, heedlessness, and rebellion, and seeing, at last, the record his life wrote (cf. 75:13; 82:5). The second level: the verse describes *every human being* — believer and denier alike — beholding the full record, because the Qur'an's principle is universal: *"whosoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whosoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it"* (99:7–8). The believer beholds his good deeds and his bad, and is met with the mercy of verse 36; the disbeliever beholds his, and utters the cry the verse records. The two readings converge on the verse's central point: the Day is the day of *beholding* — the day the sent-forth deeds arrive and are seen — and the seeing is the judgment, because the record is complete and the sender is his own witness. The sūrah's closing image is thus the *account made visible*: one man, his own two hands, and everything they sent — gathered at last, in a single glance, on the Day the sūrah has spent forty verses announcing.

**"Oh, Would That I Were Dust!": The Cry of the Lost**

The third clause is the sūrah's most haunting, and the commentators have given it their deepest attention: *wa-yaqūlu al-kāfiru yā laytanī kuntu turābā* — "and the disbeliever says: 'Oh, would that I were dust!'" The word *al-kāfir* — "the disbeliever" — is the singular: after the plural throngs of the rebels, the sūrah closes on *one* denier, and his cry is the chapter's last human word. The cry itself — *yā laytanī kuntu turābā* — is the Arabic of *wistful regret*: *layta* is the particle of *wishing for the impossible*, the sigh of the one who longs for what cannot be — and the wish is *to be dust* — the lowest, least, most lifeless thing, the very substance from which the human being was first made (cf. 3:59; 18:37; 22:5; 30:20; 35:11; 40:67). The disbeliever, beholding his deeds and the punishment they have earned, wishes he were *dust* — not a man, not a soul, not a creature with a record, but *dust* — the inanimate, the unaccountable, the nothing from which he came. The cry is the sūrah's final statement of the rebel's condition: the man who, in the world, refused the bounds and denied the reckoning, reaches the reckoning and wishes he had never been a *man* at all — because only a man can be called to account, and the dust is spared.

The commentators transmit several readings of the cry, and each deepens it. The first and most common: the disbeliever wishes he had *never been created* — that he had remained dust from the first, never given life, never given the test, never given the record — because the nothingness of dust is preferable to the account he now faces. The second: the disbeliever wishes he were *back in the grave*, dust again, *not resurrected* — the cry of the one who sees the resurrection he denied and wishes it could be undone, as the Qur'an's parallel states: *"On that Day those who disbelieved and disobeyed the Messenger will wish that they were level with the earth"* (4:42). The third, transmitted with particular poignancy: the disbeliever wishes for the *fate of the animals* — because the tradition teaches that the animals will be resurrected, their mutual wrongs judged, and then they will be told "Be dust" and become dust — and the disbeliever, seeing the animals' release, wishes he had their fate: the dust's unaccountability, the end without the reckoning. All three readings converge: the cry is the *wish to escape the account* — the final, impossible longing of the one who spent his life denying the account, and now, facing it, wishes for the one thing that would spare him: *to be nothing, to be dust, to be unaccountable.*

**The Cry and the Chapter: The Sūrah's Answer to Its Own Opening**

The verse's deepest function is to *close the circle* the sūrah opened, and the commentators mark the arc with precision. The sūrah *began* with the disbelievers *questioning* — "About what do they question one another?" — the chatter of those who debated the Great Tiding (vv. 1–3). It *continued* with the warning — "Nay, but they will know!" (vv. 4–5) — the promise that the knowledge was coming. It *built* the case — the signs of the middle section (vv. 6–16). It *described* the Day — the trumpet, the opened sky, the moved mountains (vv. 17–20), the ambush and its drinks (vv. 21–30), the reward and its gifts (vv. 31–36), the Lord and the ranks (vv. 37–38), the path and the choice (v. 39). And now, in the final verse, the circle closes: the questioners of verse 1 have become the *beholder* of verse 40 — the man who questioned now *sees*, the one who differed now *knows*, the denier who chattered now *cries* — and the cry *"would that I were dust"* is the *answer* to the opening question, delivered by the questioner himself. The sūrah's whole argument has been the journey from the *question* to the *cry* — from the heedless "what is this?" to the despairing "would that I were nothing" — and the journey is the sūrah's warning made complete: the tiding is true, the knowledge came, and the one who refused it in words now regrets it in a single, unanswerable sigh. The chapter that opened with the most arrogant of human sounds — the casual questioning of the truth — closes with the most abject — the wish to be dust — and the distance between the two sounds is the sūrah's whole teaching about the cost of heedlessness.

The verse also completes the sūrah's *psychological* arc, and the commentators mark this too: the disbeliever's cry is the *inversion of his world*. In the world, he *exalted* himself — the *ṭughyān* that refused all bounds, the self that would not be limited, the creature who made his own desire the law (vv. 22, 27–28). At the end, he wishes to be the *lowest* — dust, the thing he trod upon, the nothing he scorned. The pride that would not bow in the world begs for the dust's annihilation at the end; the self that would not accept its creaturehood wishes it had never been a creature; the rebel who would not be a servant longs to be a *thing*. The inversion is the sūrah's final statement of the rebel's ruin: the punishment's deepest cut is not the fire but the *self's collapse* — the proud one reduced to wishing for nothingness, the high one longing for the dust. The commentators note the irony the sūrah has prepared throughout: the rebel was made *from* dust (cf. 22:5) and *exalted above* it by God's gift of life and intellect and guidance — and he spent the gift on rebellion, and ends by wishing he had kept the dust. The wish is the *summary of the waste*: the whole tragedy of the disbeliever, in one sigh.

**The Warning's Last Word: The Mercy That Closes the Sūrah**

And yet — and this is the sūrah's final, unspoken point — the cry of the disbeliever is *quoted now*, in the text, *before* the Day, so that its hearer might never have to utter it. The verse's "Truly We have warned you" is the sūrah's own last word, and the word is *mercy*: the cry of the lost is recorded *in advance*, placed in the hearer's mouth *as a warning*, so that the hearer — reading the cry now, in the world, while the path of verse 39 is still open — might take the path and never say the words. The sūrah ends on the disbeliever's despair precisely so that the believer might end on the believer's hope: the last verse is the *mirror* held up to the heedless, and the mirror's purpose is the *turning* — the cry of "would that I were dust" is quoted so that no one who reads it need ever say it. The chapter that began with a question ends with a cry — and the cry, heard in time, is the sūrah's final gift: the warning completed, the choice offered, and the whole forty verses gathered into the one thing that remains for the hearer to do — *take the path, before the cry becomes your own.*

The sūrah closes, then, as it opened — on the individual, on the choice, on the Hereafter — and its last image is the image of the whole: a man, his hands, and the dust. The one who sent forward good will find, in the dust's place, the Garden; the one who sent forward evil will wish for the dust and not find it. And the sūrah's final word, heard aright, is not the disbeliever's cry but the *warning* that precedes it — *innā andharnākum* — "Truly We have warned you" — the voice of the Compassionate, spoken through forty verses, that the path is open, the Day is true, and the dust is not the end the believer is bound for.

---

**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Nabaʾ]**
